## I Storm / 一 风暴

===Original===
Aunt Hager Williams stood in her doorway and looked out at the sun. The western sky was a sulphurous yellow and the sun a red ball dropping slowly behind the trees and housetops. Its setting left the rest of the heavens grey with clouds.

“Huh! A storm’s comin’,” said Aunt Hager aloud.

A pullet ran across the backyard and into a square-cut hole in an unpainted piano-box which served as the roosting-house. An old hen clucked her brood together and, with the tiny chicks, went into a small box beside the large one. The air was very still. Not a leaf stirred on the green apple-tree. Not a single closed flower of the morning-glories trembled on the back fence. The air was very still and yellow. Something sultry and oppressive made a small boy in the doorway stand closer to his grandmother, clutching her apron with his brown hands.

“Sho is a storm comin’,” said Aunt Hager.

“I hope mama gets home ’fore it rains,” remarked the brown child holding to the old woman’s apron. “Hope she gets home.”

“I does, too,” said Aunt Hager. “But I’s skeared she won’t.”

===Chinese===
黑格外婆（Aunt Hager Williams）站在自家门口，望着外头的太阳。西天是一片硫磺似的黄色，太阳成了一团红球，正慢慢沉落到树梢和屋顶后面。它一落，天上其余的地方便都让灰云占满了。

「哼！要来暴风雨了。」黑格外婆大声说。

一只小母鸡跑过后院，钻进一只没上漆的钢琴货箱上裁得方方正正的洞里——那箱子就权当鸡窝。一只老母鸡咯咯地把一窝雏鸡唤拢，带着毛茸茸的小鸡，钻进大箱旁边一只小些的箱子。空气凝滞极了。绿苹果树上一片叶子也不颤。后院篱笆上，牵牛花闭合着的花朵，没有一朵打颤。空气凝滞极了，还昏黄昏黄的。一股闷热逼人的东西，让站在门口的小男孩往外婆身边偎得更紧，用他棕色的小手攥住她的围裙。

「可不是嘛，一场暴风雨要来了。」黑格外婆说。

「我盼着妈妈赶在下雨前到家，」攥着老太太围裙的棕色孩子说，「盼着她到家。」

「俺也盼呐，」黑格外婆说，「可俺怕她赶不回来。」

===Original===
Just then great drops of water began to fall heavily into the backyard, pounding up little clouds of dust where each drop struck the earth. For a few moments they pattered violently on the roof like a series of hammer-strokes; then suddenly they ceased.

“Come in, chile,” said Aunt Hager.

She closed the door as the green apple-tree began to sway in the wind and a small hard apple fell, rolling rapidly down the top of the piano-box that sheltered the chickens. Inside the kitchen it was almost dark. While Aunt Hager lighted an oil-lamp, the child climbed to a chair and peered through the square window into the yard. The leaves and flowers of the morning-glory vines on the back fence were bending with the rising wind. And across the alley at the big house, Mrs. Kennedy’s rear screen-door banged to and fro, and Sandy saw her garbage-pail suddenly tip over and roll down into the yard, scattering potato-peelings on the white steps.

“Sho gwine be a terrible storm,” said Hager as she turned up the wick of the light and put the chimney on. Then, glancing through the window, she saw a black cloud twisting like a ribbon in the western sky, and the old woman screamed aloud in sudden terror: “It’s a cyclone! It’s gwine be a cyclone! Sandy, let’s get over to Mis’ Carter’s quick, ’cause we ain’t got no cellar here. Come on, chile, let’s get! Come on, chile!﻿ ﻿… Come on, chile!”

Hurriedly she blew out the light, grabbed the boy’s hand; and together they rushed through the little house towards the front. It was quite dark in the inner rooms, but through the parlor windows came a sort of sooty grey-green light that was rapidly turning to blackness.

“Lawd help us, Jesus!”

===Chinese===
就在这时，豆大的雨点开始沉甸甸地砸向后院，每一滴落在地上，都溅起一小股尘土。片刻之间，雨点像一连串锤击，噼里啪啦狠砸在屋顶上；跟着忽然就停了。

「进来吧，娃儿。」黑格外婆说。

她关上门，这时绿苹果树已经在风里摇曳起来，一只小小的硬苹果落下来，顺着鸡笼箱顶骨碌碌滚了下去。厨房里几乎黑了。黑格外婆点油灯的当口，孩子爬上椅子，从那扇方窗往院子里张望。后院篱笆上，牵牛花的藤蔓、叶子和花朵，都随着越刮越大的风弯下了腰。隔着小巷，对面大宅那边，肯尼迪太太家的后纱门被风拍得来回乱响，桑迪（Sandy）看见她家的垃圾桶猛地翻倒，一路滚进院子，把土豆皮撒了一白台阶。

「准是一场吓死人的大风暴，」黑格说着，把灯芯捻亮，罩上玻璃灯罩。接着她朝窗外一瞥，看见西天有一团乌云，像一条带子似的扭卷着，老太太猛地惊叫起来：「是龙卷风！要刮龙卷风了！桑迪，快跟俺上卡特太太家去，咱家这儿没地窖。快走，娃儿，赶紧走！快呀，娃儿！……快呀，娃儿！」

她慌忙吹灭灯，一把抓住孩子的手；两人一起穿过小屋朝前屋奔去。里屋已经相当黑，只有客厅的窗户透进一种烟灰似的灰绿色光，正飞快地变成一团漆黑。

「主啊，救救我们，耶稣啊！」

===Original===
Aunt Hager opened the front door, but before she or the child could move, a great roaring sound suddenly shook the world, and, with a deafening division of wood from wood, they saw their front porch rise into the air and go hurtling off into space. Sailing high in the gathering darkness, the porch was soon lost to sight. And the black wind blew with terrific force, numbing the eardrums.

For a moment the little house trembled and swayed and creaked as though it were about to fall.

“Help me to shut this do’,” Aunt Hager screamed; “help me to shut it, Lawd!” as with all her might she struggled against the open door, which the wind held back, but finally it closed and the lock caught. Then she sank to the floor with her back against the wall, while her small grandson trembled like a leaf as she took him in her lap, mumbling: “What a storm!﻿ ﻿… O, Lawdy!﻿ ﻿… O, ma chile, what a storm!”

They could hear the crackling of timbers and the rolling limbs of trees that the wind swept across the roof. Her arms tightened about the boy.

“Dear Jesus!” she said. “I wonder where is yo’ mama? S’pose she started out fo’ home ’fore this storm come up!” Then in a scream: “Have mercy on ma Annjee! O, Lawd, have mercy on this chile’s mama! Have mercy on all ma chillens! Ma Harriett, an’ ma Tempy, an’ ma Annjee, what’s maybe all of ’em out in de storm! O, Lawd!”

A dry crack of lightning split the darkness, and the boy began to wail. Then the rain broke. The old woman could not see the crying child she held, nor could the boy hear the broken voice of his grandmother, who had begun to pray as the rain crashed through the inky blackness. For a long while it roared on the roof of the house and pounded at the windows, until finally the two within became silent, hushing their cries. Then only the lashing noise of the water, coupled with the feeling that something terrible was happening, or had already happened, filled the evening air.

===Chinese===
黑格外婆打开前门，可她和孩子还没来得及挪步，一声巨大的轰鸣突然撼动了整个世界；紧跟着，木头与木头撕分开来的一声震耳巨响里，他们眼看着自家的前廊腾空而起，飞也似的抛进了天空。前廊在愈聚愈浓的黑暗里高高飘飞，很快就没了踪影。黑色的风以骇人的力量刮着，刮得人耳膜发麻。

一时间，小屋颤抖着，摇晃着，嘎吱作响，仿佛就要倒了。

「帮俺把这扇门关上，」黑格外婆尖声喊道，「帮俺把它关上吧，主啊！」她使出全身的力气，跟那扇敞开的门搏斗，风死死抵住门不放，可门终于关上了，锁扣咔哒一声扣住了。她背靠着墙，瘫坐到地板上，把小外孙搂进怀里，孩子抖得像一片叶子。她嘴里喃喃着：「好大的风暴啊！……哦，主啊！……哦，俺的娃儿，好大的风暴啊！」

他们听得见木头断裂的噼啪声，听得见风卷着大树枝从屋顶上滚过去的声响。她搂着孩子的手臂收紧了。

「亲爱的耶稣啊！」她说。「你妈妈这会儿在哪儿呢？别是风暴上来之前，她刚动身往家走吧！」接着她尖叫起来：「怜悯俺的安吉（Annjee）吧！哦，主啊，怜悯这娃儿的妈妈吧！怜悯俺所有的娃儿们吧！俺的哈丽雅特（Harriett）、俺的坦皮（Tempy）、俺的安吉，这会儿怕是全都在风暴里头呢！哦，主啊！」

一道闪电带着干脆的爆裂声撕开黑暗，孩子哇地哭了起来。接着，大雨倾盆而下。老太太看不见怀里哭泣的孩子，孩子也听不见外婆那断断续续的嗓音——大雨冲开墨黑一团的世界时，她已经念开了祷告。好一阵子，雨在屋顶上咆哮，捶打着窗户，直到屋里的两个人终于静了下来，止住了哭喊。此后，只剩鞭子似的抽打的水声，再加上那种有什么可怕的事正在发生、或者已经发生了的感觉，充满了黄昏的空气。

---

===Original===
After the rain the moon rose clear and bright and the clouds disappeared from the lately troubled sky. The stars sparkled calmly above the havoc of the storm, and it was still early evening as people emerged from their houses and began to investigate the damage brought by the twisting cyclone that had come with the sunset. Through the rubbish-filled streets men drove slowly with horse and buggy or automobile. The fire-engine was out, banging away, and the soft tang-tang-tang of the motor ambulance could be heard in the distance carrying off the injured.

Black Aunt Hager and her brown grandson put their rubbers on and stood in the water-soaked front yard looking at the porchless house where they lived. Platform, steps, pillars, roof, and all had been blown away. Not a semblance of a porch was left and the front door opened bare into the yard. It was grotesque and funny. Hager laughed.

“Cyclone sho did a good job,” she said. “Looks like I ain’t never had no porch.”

Madam de Carter, from next door, came across the grass, her large mouth full of chattering sympathy for her neighbor.

“But praise God for sparing our lives! It might’ve been worse, Sister Williams! It might’ve been much more calamitouser! As it is, I lost nothin’ more’n a chimney and two washtubs which was settin’ in the backyard. A few trees broke down don’t ’mount to nothin’. We’s livin’, ain’t we? And we’s more importanter than trees is any day!” Her gold teeth sparkled in the moonlight.

===Chinese===
雨过之后，月亮清清亮亮地升起来，方才还翻腾不安的天空里，云都散尽了。星星在风暴肆虐过的狼藉上空平静地闪烁。夜色还早，人们纷纷走出家门，开始查看这场随日落而来的、打着旋的龙卷风造成了什么损坏。男人们赶着马车、开着汽车，在满是残骸的街道上缓缓驶过。救火车出动了，当当地敲着警铃，远处还传来救护车那轻柔的铛——铛——铛声，正把伤者载走。

黑皮肤的黑格外婆和她棕色皮肤的外孙穿上雨鞋，站在浸透了水的前院里，望着他们住的那座没了门廊的房子。廊台、台阶、廊柱、廊顶，一股脑儿全给吹跑了。连一点门廊的样子都没剩下，前门光秃秃地直对着院子敞开。那样子又怪又好笑。黑格笑了。

「龙卷风这活儿可干得真利索，」她说，「瞧那样子，俺压根儿就像从来没过门廊。」

隔壁的德·卡特夫人（Madam de Carter）穿过草地走过来，大嘴巴里喋喋不休，满是对邻居的同情。

「不过要赞美上帝，保全了咱们的性命！本来兴许更糟呢，威廉姆斯姊妹！本来兴许要遭难得多多呢！就说眼下，俺统共丢了一根烟囱，还有后院里搁着的两只洗衣盆。几棵树折断了算不得啥。咱们不都还活着吗？咱们人可比树金贵，哪天都是！」她的金牙在月光下闪闪发亮。

===Original===
“ ’Deed so,” agreed Hager emphatically. “Let’s move on down de block, Sister, an’ see what mo’ de Lawd has ’stroyed or spared this evenin’. He’s gin us plenty moonlight after de storm so we po’ humans can see this lesson o’ His’n to a sinful world.”

The two elderly colored women picked their way about on the wet walk, littered with twigs and branches of broken foliage. The little brown boy followed, with his eyes wide at the sight of baby-carriages, window-sashes, shingles, and tree-limbs scattered about in the roadway. Large numbers of people were out, some standing on porches, some carrying lanterns, picking up useful articles from the streets, some wringing their hands in a daze.

Near the corner a small crowd had gathered quietly.

“Mis’ Gavitt’s killed,” somebody said.

“Lawd help!” burst from Aunt Hager and Madam de Carter simultaneously.

===Chinese===
「可不是咋的，」黑格深以为然。「咱们顺着街区往前走走吧，姊妹，瞧瞧主今儿晚上又毁了啥、又饶了啥。风暴过后，他给咱们这么足的月光，好叫咱们这些可怜人看清他给这个罪孽世界的一课。」

两位上了年纪的黑人妇人，在撒满细枝和断叶残枝的湿人行道上择着道走。棕色皮肤的小男孩跟在后头，看见婴儿车、窗框、木瓦和树枝散落一路，眼睛瞪得溜圆。大批的人都出来了：有的站在门廊上，有的提着灯笼，从街上捡拾顶用的物件，有的失魂落魄地绞着两手。

街角附近，一小群人静悄悄地聚着。

「加维特太太死啦。」有人说。

「主啊，保佑！」黑格外婆和德·卡特夫人同时脱口喊出。

===Original===
“Mister and Mis’ Gavitt’s both dead,” added a nervous young white man, bursting with the news. “We live next door to ’em, and their house turned clean over! Came near hitting us and breaking our sidewall in.”

“Have mercy!” said the two women, but Sandy slipped away from his grandmother and pushed through the crowd. He ran round the corner to where he could see the overturned house of the unfortunate Gavitts.

Good white folks, the Gavitts, Aunt Hager had often said, and now their large frame dwelling lay on its side like a doll’s mansion, with broken furniture strewn carelessly on the wet lawn﻿—and they were dead. Sandy saw a piano flat on its back in the grass. Its ivory keys gleamed in the moonlight like grinning teeth, and the strange sight made his little body shiver, so he hurried back through the crowd looking for his grandmother. As he passed the corner, he heard a woman sobbing hysterically within the wide house there.

His grandmother was no longer standing where he had left her, but he found Madam de Carter and took hold of her hand. She was in the midst of a group of excited white and colored women. One frail old lady was saying in a high determined voice that she had never seen a cyclone like this in her whole life, and she had lived here in Kansas, if you please, going on seventy-three years. Madam de Carter, chattering nervously, began to tell them how she had recognized its coming and had rushed to the cellar the minute she saw the sky turn green. She had not come up until the rain stopped, so frightened had she been. She was extravagantly enjoying the telling of her fears as Sandy kept tugging at her hand.

“Where’s my grandma?” he demanded. Madam de Carter, however, did not cease talking to answer his question.

“What do you want, sonny?” finally one of the white women asked, bending down when he looked as if he were about to cry. “Aunt Hager?﻿ ﻿… Why, she’s inside helping them calm poor Mrs. Gavitt’s niece. Your grandmother’s good to have around when folks are sick or grieving, you know. Run and set on the steps like a nice boy and wait until she comes out.” So Sandy left the women and went to sit in the dark on the steps of the big corner house where the niece of the dead Mrs. Gavitt lived. There were some people on the porch, but they soon passed through the screen-door into the house or went away down the street. The moonlight cast weird shadows across the damp steps where Sandy sat, and it was dark there under the trees in spite of the moon, for the old house was built far back from the street in a yard full of oaks and maples, and Sandy could see the light from an upstairs window reflecting on the wet leaves of their nearest boughs. He heard a girl screaming, too, up there where the light was burning, and he knew that Aunt Hager was putting cold cloths on her head, or rubbing her hands, or driving folks out of the room, and talking kind to her so that she would soon be better.

===Chinese===
「加维特先生和太太双双死了，」一个沉不住气的白人小伙子补充道，一门心思憋着要抖出这条新闻。「我们就住他们隔壁，他们家的房子整个儿翻了个底朝天！差点儿砸着我们，把我们家的侧墙都差点撞穿了。」

「老天爷可怜呐！」两个女人说。可桑迪从外婆身边溜走，从人群里挤了过去。他绕过街角，跑到能望见不幸的加维特夫妇那座翻倒的房子的地方。

黑格外婆平日常念叨，加维特一家是难得的好白人；如今他们那座高大的木架宅子侧身倒着，像一座翻倒的玩偶公馆，砸坏的家具胡乱撒在湿草坪上——而他们死了。桑迪看见一架钢琴仰面朝天躺在草里，象牙琴键在月光下发亮，像一排龇着笑的白牙。这奇怪的景象使他小小的身子打了个寒噤，于是他急急穿过人群，回头去找外婆。经过街角时，他听见那座宽敞的宅子里有个女人在歇斯底里地抽泣。

外婆已经不在他离开时的老地方了，不过他找到了德·卡特夫人，拉住了她的手。她正夹在一群激动的白人和黑人妇女中间。一位瘦弱的老太太正用又尖又坚决的嗓音说，她这辈子从没见过这样的龙卷风，而她——您听听——就在堪萨斯这儿住着，都快七十三年了。德·卡特夫人紧张地絮絮叨叨，开始对她们讲，自己怎么一眼认出龙卷风要来，一看见天色发绿就冲进了地窖。她吓得要命，直到雨停才上来。桑迪一个劲儿地拽她的手，她却正把这一场惊惶讲得绘声绘色，过足了瘾。

「我外婆在哪儿？」他问道。可德·卡特夫人说个不停，没有停下来回答他的问题。

「你要干什么呀，小家伙？」终于有一个白人妇女弯下腰来问他，因为他看上去眼看就要哭了。「找黑格外婆？……哎呀，她在里头呢，帮着安抚可怜的加维特太太的侄女。家里有人生病或者遭了难，有你外婆这样的人在跟前是顶好的，知道吧。跑去坐在台阶上，做个乖孩子，等她出来。」于是桑迪离开那些妇女，坐到了黑地里，坐在死去的那位加维特太太的侄女所住的街角大宅的台阶上。门廊上还有些人，但他们很快就穿过纱门进了屋，或是沿街走远了。月光把怪模怪样的影子投在桑迪坐着的湿台阶上；虽然有月亮，树底下还是黑黢黢的，因为这座老宅子远远缩在街后头，院子里长满橡树和枫树，桑迪能看见楼上一扇窗户里的灯光，映在近处那几根枝条的湿叶子上。他还听见一个姑娘在上头亮着灯的地方尖叫。他知道，黑格外婆这会儿正在给她头上敷凉布巾，或是揉着她的手，或是把人们撵出屋去，跟她和声细气地说话，好叫她快些安生下来。

===Original===
All the neighborhood, white or colored, called his grandmother when something happened. She was a good nurse, they said, and sick folks liked her around. Aunt Hager always came when they called, too, bringing maybe a little soup that she had made or a jelly. Sometimes they paid her and sometimes they didn’t. But Sandy had never had to sit outdoors in the darkness waiting for her before. He leaned his small back against the top step and rested his elbows on the porch behind him. It was growing late and the people in the streets had all disappeared.

There, in the dark, the little fellow began to think about his mother, who worked on the other side of town for a rich white lady named Mrs. J. J. Rice. And suddenly frightful thoughts came into his mind. Suppose she had left for home just as the storm came up! Almost always his mother was home before dark﻿—but she wasn’t there tonight when the storm came﻿—and she should have been home! This thought appalled him. She should have been there! But maybe she had been caught by the storm and blown away as she walked down Main Street! Maybe Annjee had been carried off by the great black wind that had overturned the Gavitts’ house and taken his grandma’s porch flying through the air! Maybe the cyclone had gotten his mother, Sandy thought. He wanted her! Where was she? Had something terrible happened to her? Where was she now?

The big tears began to roll down his cheeks﻿—but the little fellow held back the sobs that wanted to come. He decided he wasn’t going to cry and make a racket there by himself on the strange steps of these white folks’ house. He wasn’t going to cry like a big baby in the dark. So he wiped his eyes, kicked his heels against the cement walk, lay down on the top step, and, by and by, sniffled himself to sleep.

===Chinese===
这一带的街坊，不论白人黑人，谁家出了事都来请他外婆。他们说她是顶好的看护，病人都喜欢有她在跟前。人家来叫的时候，黑格外婆也总是去，兴许带上一小锅自己熬的汤，或是一份果子冻。有时人家给她钱，有时不给。可是以前，桑迪还从来没有这样坐在外头的黑地里等过她。他把小小的脊背靠在最上一级台阶上，两只胳膊肘支在身后的廊台上。夜渐深了，街上的人已经走得精光。

在黑暗里，小家伙开始想他的妈妈。她在城那头给一位有钱的白人太太帮佣，太太名叫赖斯太太（Mrs. J. J. Rice）。忽然，种种可怕的念头钻进了他的脑子。要是风暴起来的时候，她刚好动身往家走呢！天黑以前，妈妈差不多总是到家的——可今晚风暴来的时候她不在家——她本该到家的呀！这个念头吓坏了他。她本该在家的呀！可说不定她正走在大街（Main Street）上，就被风暴追上、刮跑了呢！说不定安吉已经被那股掀翻加维特家的房子、把他外婆家的门廊卷上天的大黑风给卷走了呢！说不定龙卷风把妈妈抓去了，桑迪想。他要妈妈！她在哪儿？她是不是出了什么可怕的事？她这会儿在哪儿？

大颗大颗的泪珠开始顺着他的脸颊往下滚——可小家伙憋住了要涌上来的呜咽。他打定了主意：不在这白人人家陌生的台阶上，就他自己一个人，哭天喊地地闹出动静。他才不要像个大娃娃似的在黑地里哭呢。于是他擦擦眼睛，脚后跟磕着水泥步道，往最上一级台阶上一躺，不多一会儿，就抽抽噎噎地睡着了。

---

===Original===
“Wake up, son!” Someone was shaking him. “You’ll catch your death o’ cold sleeping on the wet steps like this. We’re going home now. Don’t want me to have to carry a big man like you, do you, boy?﻿ ﻿… Wake up, Sandy!” His mother stooped to lift his long little body from the wide steps. She held him against her soft heavy breasts and let his head rest on one of her shoulders while his feet, in their muddy rubbers, hung down against her dress.

“Where you been, mama?” the boy asked drowsily, tightening his arms about her neck. “I been waiting for you.”

“Oh, I been home a long time, worried to death about you and ma till I heard from Madam Carter that you-all was down here nursing the sick. I stopped at your Aunt Tempy’s house when I seen the storm coming.”

“I was afraid you got blowed away, mama,” murmured Sandy sleepily. “Let’s go home, mama. I’m glad you ain’t got blowed away.”

On the porch Aunt Hager was talking to a pale white man and two thin white women standing at the door of the lighted hallway. “Just let Mis’ Agnes sleep,” she was saying. “She’ll be all right now, an’ I’ll come back in de mawnin’ to see ’bout her.﻿ ﻿… Good night, you-all.”

The old colored woman joined her daughter and they started home, walking through the streets filled with debris and puddles of muddy water reflecting the moon.

===Chinese===
「醒醒，儿子！」有人在摇他。「睡在这么湿的台阶上，要冻出人命来的。咱们这就回家。你总不想叫妈妈不得不抱着你这么个大汉子走吧，嗯，小子？……醒醒，桑迪！」他妈妈俯下身去，把那长长的小身子从宽阔的台阶上抱起来。她把他搂在自己柔软而沉实的胸口上，让他的头靠住她一边肩膀；他脚上那双沾满泥的雨鞋悬下来，蹭着她的裙子。

「妈妈，你上哪儿去了？」孩子睡意朦胧地问，搂着她脖子的胳膊收紧了。「我一直在这儿等你。」

「哦，我到家都好半天了，为你和你外婆急得要死，后来听德·卡特夫人说，你们都在这儿看护病人呢。我瞧见风暴要来，就拐到你坦皮大姨家待下了。」

「我还怕你被刮跑了呢，妈妈，」桑迪睡意沉沉地咕哝。「咱们回家吧，妈妈。我真高兴你没被刮跑。」

门廊上，黑格外婆正跟一个脸色苍白的白人男人和两个瘦削的白人女人说话，他们站在亮着灯的门厅门口。「就让阿格尼丝小姐（Mis' Agnes）睡吧，」她正这么说着。「她这会儿没事了，明儿一早俺再过来看望她。……晚安，各位。」

这位年老的黑人妇人走到女儿跟前，她们动身回家，穿过满是断枝瓦砾的街道，走过一个个映着月亮的泥水洼。

===Original===
“You’re certainly heavy, boy,” remarked Sandy’s mother to the child she held, but he didn’t answer.

“I’m right glad you come for me, Annjee,” Hager said. “I wonder is yo’ sister all right out yonder at de country club.﻿ ﻿… An’ I was so worried ’bout you I didn’t know what to do﻿—skeared you might a got caught in this twister, ’cause it were cert’ly awful!”

“I was at Tempy’s!” Annjee replied. “And I was nearly crazy, but I just left everything in the hands o’ God. That’s all.” In silence they walked on, a piece; then hesitantly, to her mother: “There wasn’t any mail for me today, was there, ma?”

“Not a speck!” the old woman replied shortly. “Mailman passed on by.”

For a few minutes there was silence again as they walked. Then, “It’s goin’ on three weeks he’s been gone, and he ain’t written a line,” the younger woman complained, shifting the child to her right arm. “Seems like Jimboy would let a body know where he is, ma, wouldn’t it?”

“Huh! That ain’t nothin’! He’s been gone before this an’ he ain’t wrote, ain’t he? Here you is worryin’ ’bout a letter from that good-for-nothing husband o’ your’n﻿—an’ there’s ma house settin’ up without a porch to its name!﻿ ﻿… Ain’t you seed what de devil’s done done on earth this evenin’, chile?﻿ ﻿… An’ yet de first thing you ask me ’bout is de mailman!﻿ ﻿… Lawd! Lawd!﻿ ﻿… You an’ that Jimboy!”

===Chinese===
「你可真沉哪，小子，」桑迪的妈妈对怀里抱着的孩子说，可他没有答腔。

「你能来接俺，俺真高兴，安吉，」黑格说。「也不知你妹妹在那头的乡村俱乐部里好不好。……俺为你担心得不知道咋办好——就怕你叫这股旋风卷上了，那风可真是怕人！」

「我那时候在坦皮家！」安吉答道。「我都快急疯了，可我把一总事情全交到上帝手里了。就这么回事。」两人默默走了一截；随后她迟疑着问妈妈：「今天没有我的信，是吧，妈？」

「一星半点儿也没有！」老太太没好气地答道。「邮差打门前过去了。」

走着的这几分钟里，又是一阵沉默。后来，年轻些的那个女人开了腔，抱怨道——一边把孩子换到右臂上：「他都走了快三个星期了，一行字也没写来。照理说吉姆博伊（Jimboy）总该让人知道他在哪儿吧，妈，你说是不是？」

「哼！那算个啥！他以前不也走开过，也没写过信吗？你在这儿操心你那个不中用的丈夫来的信——俺那房子却光溜溜地立在那儿，连个门廊都没剩下！……娃儿，你没瞧见魔鬼今儿晚上在地上干下的这些事吗？……可你张口问俺的头一件事，偏偏是邮差！……主啊！主啊！……你呀，还有你那个吉姆博伊！」

===Original===
Aunt Hager lifted her heavy body over fallen tree-trunks and across puddles, but between puffs she managed to voice her indignation, so Annjee said no more concerning letters from her husband. Instead they went back to the subject of the cyclone. “I’m just thankful, ma, it didn’t blow the whole house down and you with it, that’s all! I was certainly worried!﻿ ﻿… And then you-all was gone when I got home! Gone on out﻿—nursing that white woman.﻿ ﻿… It’s too bad ’bout poor Mis’ Gavitt, though, and old man Gavitt, ain’t it?”

“Yes, indeedy!” said Aunt Hager. “It’s sho too bad. They was certainly good old white folks! An’ her married niece is takin’ it mighty hard, po’ little soul. I was nigh two hours, her husband an’ me, tryin’ to bring her out o’ de hysterics. Tremblin’ like a lamb all over, she was.” They were turning into the yard. “Be careful with that chile, Annjee, you don’t trip on none o’ them boards nor branches an’ fall with him.”

“Put me down, ’cause I’m awake,” said Sandy.

The old house looked queer without a porch. In the moonlight he could see the long nails that had held the porch roof to the weatherboarding. His grandmother climbed slowly over the doorsill, and his mother lifted him to the floor level as Aunt Hager lit the large oil-lamp on the parlor table. Then they went back to the bedroom, where the youngster took off his clothes, said his prayers, and climbed into the high feather bed where he slept with Annjee. Aunt Hager went to the next room, but for a long time she talked back and forth through the doorway to her daughter about the storm.

“We was just startin’ out fo’ Mis’ Carter’s cellar, me an’ Sandy,” she said several times. “But de Lawd was with us! He held us back! Praise His name! We ain’t harmed, none of us﻿—’ceptin’ I don’t know ’bout ma Harriett at de club. But you’s all right. An’ you say Tempy’s all right, too. An’ I prays that Harriett ain’t been touched out there in de country where she’s workin’. Maybe de storm ain’t passed that way.”

===Chinese===
黑格外婆拖着沉重的身子，跨过倒伏的树干，蹚过一个个水坑，可喘口气的工夫，她还不忘往外倒她的火气，于是安吉不再提丈夫来信的事。她们转而重新谈起龙卷风。「妈，我就是谢天谢地，它没把整座房子连你一块儿刮倒，就这么着！我真是担心坏了！……结果我到家，你们又不见了！出去了——看护那个白人女人去了。……不过，可怜的加维特太太，还有加维特老爷子，也真惨，是不？」

「是啊，可不是！」黑格外婆说。「是真惨。他们老两口真是难得的好白人！她那个成了家的侄女受不了这个打击，怪可怜的小人儿。她男人跟俺俩，折腾了快两个钟头，才把她从歇斯底里里劝过来。她浑身抖得像只小羊羔。」她们正拐进院子。「小心着点那娃儿，安吉，别叫那些板子啊树枝啊绊着，摔着他。」

「放我下来吧，我醒着呢。」桑迪说。

老房子没了门廊，看上去怪模怪样。月光下，他看得见当初把廊顶钉在墙板上的那些长钉子。外婆慢慢跨过门槛，妈妈把他举到跟地板一般高，这时黑格外婆点起了客厅桌上的大油灯。然后他们回到卧室，小家伙脱了衣裳，做了祷告，爬上那张高高的羽绒床——他是跟安吉一起睡在那上面的。黑格外婆到隔壁房间去了，可好长一段时间里，她还隔着门口跟女儿你一句我一句地聊着这场风暴。

「俺跟桑迪正要动身去卡特太太家的地窖，」这句话她说了好几遍。「可主跟俺们同在！是他拦住了俺们！赞美他的名！俺们谁都没伤着——只是不知道俺的哈丽雅特在俱乐部里咋样。不过你没事。你也说坦皮没事。俺就祷告，求主让哈丽雅特在她干活的那个乡下地方别遭了灾。兴许风暴没打那边过。」

===Original===
Then they spoke about the white people where Annjee worked﻿ ﻿… and about the elder sister Tempy’s prosperity. Then Sandy heard his grandmother climb into bed, and a few minutes after the springs screaked under her, she had begun to snore. Annjee closed the door between their rooms and slowly began to unlace her wet shoes.

“Sandy,” she whispered, “we ain’t had no word yet from your father since he left. I know he goes away and stays away like this and don’t write, but I’m sure worried. Hope the cyclone ain’t passed nowhere near wherever he is, and I hope ain’t nothin’ hurt him.﻿ ﻿… I’m gonna pray for him, Sandy. I’m gonna ask God right now to take care o’ Jimboy.﻿ ﻿… The Lawd knows, I wants him to come back!﻿ ﻿… I loves him.﻿ ﻿… We both loves him, don’t we, child? And we want him to come on back!”

She knelt down beside the bed in her nightdress and kept her head bowed for a long time. Before she got up, Sandy had gone to sleep.

===Chinese===
后来，她们谈起安吉帮佣的那户白人人家……也谈起大姐坦皮家光景兴旺。接着桑迪听见外婆上了床，不多几分钟，她身下的弹簧嘎吱一响，呼噜就打起来了。安吉关上两间屋中间的门，慢慢解起湿鞋的鞋带来。

「桑迪，」她悄声说，「你爸爸走了以后，咱们还没得到他一点儿音信。我知道他就是这个样子，说走就走，一走就待在外头不回来，也不写信，可我实在是担心。但愿龙卷风没刮到他待的任何地方附近，但愿没伤着他。……我要为他祈祷，桑迪。我这就求上帝看顾吉姆博伊。……主知道，我盼着他回来！……我爱他。……咱们俩都爱他，对不对，孩子？咱们都想让他回来！」

她穿着睡衣，在床边跪了下来，久久地垂着头。还没等她起身，桑迪已经睡着了。

## II Conversation / 二 闲谈

===Original===
It was broad daylight in the town of Stanton and had been for a long time.

“Get out o’ that bed, boy!” Aunt Hager yelled. “Here’s Buster waitin’ out in de yard to play with you, an’ you still sleepin’!”

“Aw, tell him to cut off his curls,” retorted Sandy, but his grandmother was in no mood for fooling.

“Stop talkin’ ’bout that chile’s haid and put yo’ clothes on. Nine o’clock an’ you ain’t up yet! Shame on you!” She shouted from the kitchen, where Sandy could hear the fire crackling and smell coffee boiling.

He kicked the sheet off with his bare feet and rolled over and over on the soft feather tick. There was plenty of room to roll now, because his mother had long since got up and gone to Mrs. J. J. Rice’s to work.

“Tell Bus I’m coming,” Sandy yelled, jumping into his trousers and running with bare feet towards the door. “Is he got his marbles?”

===Chinese===
斯坦顿城里早已是大天亮，而且亮了有一阵子了。

“快给我从床上下来，小子！”黑格外婆嚷道。“巴斯特在院子里等着跟你玩儿呢，你倒还在睡！”

“哼，叫他先把那头小卷毛铰了吧。”桑迪回了一句嘴，可外婆没心思跟他逗闷子。

“少念叨人家娃儿的头发，快把衣裳穿上。都九点了还赖在床上！不害臊吗！”她在厨房里喊——桑迪听得见炉膛里柴火噼啪作响，闻得着咖啡煮沸的香味。

他光着两只脚丫把床单蹬开，在软乎乎的羽绒褥垫上滚来滚去。如今满床尽是地方滚，因为母亲早就起了身，上赖斯太太（Mrs. J. J. Rice）家帮佣去了。

“跟巴斯特说我这就来，”桑迪一边嚷，一边往裤子里蹦，光着脚朝门口跑去。“他的弹珠带上了没有？”

===Original===
“Come back here, sir, an’ put them shoes on,” cried Hager, stopping him on his way out. “Yo’ feet’ll get long as yardsticks and flat as pancakes runnin’ round barefooted all de time. An’ wash yo’ face, sir. Buster ain’t got a thing to do but wait. An’ eat yo’ breakfast.”

The air was warm with sunlight, and hundreds of purple and white morning-glories laughed on the back fence. Earth and sky were fresh and clean after the heavy night-rain, and the young corn-shoots stood straight in the garden, and green pea-vines wound themselves around their crooked sticks. There was the mingled scent of wet soil and golden pollen on the breeze that blew carelessly through the clear air.

Buster sat under the green apple-tree with a pile of black mud from the alley in front of him.

“Hey, Sandy, gonna make marbles and put ’em in the sun to dry,” he said.

“All right,” agreed Sandy, and they began to roll mud balls in the palms of their hands. But instead of putting them in the sun to dry they threw them against the back of the house, where they flattened and stuck beautifully. Then they began to throw them at each other.

Sandy’s playmate was a small ivory-white Negro child with straight golden hair, which his mother made him wear in curls. His eyes were blue and doll-like and he in no way resembled a colored youngster; but he was colored. Sandy himself was the shade of a nicely browned piece of toast, with dark, brown-black eyes and a head of rather kinky, sandy hair that would lie smooth only after a rigorous application of vaseline and water. That was why folks called him Annjee’s sandy-headed child, and then just—Sandy.

===Chinese===
“给我回来，小子，把鞋穿上，”黑格喊住正要往外冲的他。“成天光着脚满处跑，脚丫子要跑得跟码尺一样长、跟烙饼一样扁啦。还有，把脸洗了，小子。巴斯特闲着没事，等就等着呗。早饭也得吃。”

空气被阳光晒得暖洋洋的，后院栅栏上成百朵紫色、白色的牵牛花开得正欢。一夜大雨过后，天地间清新明净，园子里的玉米幼苗笔直地立着，绿色的豌豆藤缠绕在歪歪扭扭的支棍上。清风漫不经心地吹过澄澈的天空，风里裹着湿土和金色花粉的混合气息。

巴斯特坐在绿荫荫的苹果树下，面前堆着一小堆从巷子里挖来的黑泥。

“嘿，桑迪，咱们捏泥弹珠吧，捏好搁太阳底下晒。”他说。

“行啊。”桑迪应道，两人便在手掌心里搓起泥球来。可他们没把泥球摆到太阳底下晾，反倒朝着屋子后墙扔过去——泥球啪地拍扁在墙上，粘得别提多漂亮了。接着他们开始互相扔着打起来。

桑迪的玩伴巴斯特（Buster）是个象牙色皮肤的黑人小孩，一头金色直发，他娘非要他留着卷曲不可。他的眼睛是蓝色的，像洋娃娃的眼睛，怎么看都不像个有色人种的孩子；可他偏偏就是个有色人种。桑迪自己呢，肤色像烤得恰到好处的一片吐司，一双深棕近黑的眼睛，满头沙色的鬈发倔头倔脑地支棱着，非得狠抹一通凡士林、再浇上水，才能压得服帖。就为这个，大伙儿先管他叫安吉家那个沙色头发的娃，后来干脆就叫——桑迪。

===Original===
“He takes after his father,” Sister Lowry said, “’cept he’s not so light. But he’s gonna be a mighty good-lookin’ boy when he grows up, that’s sho!”

“Well, I hopes he does,” Aunt Hager said. “But I’d rather he’d be ugly ’fore he turns out anything like that good-for-nothing Jimboy what comes here an’ stays a month, goes away an’ stays six, an’ don’t hit a tap o’ work ’cept when he feels like it. If it wasn’t for Annjee, don’t know how we’d eat, ’cause Sandy’s father sho don’t do nothin’ to support him.”

All the colored people in Stanton knew that Hager bore no love for Jimboy Rodgers, the tall good-looking yellow fellow whom her second daughter had married.

“First place, I don’t like his name,” she would say in private. “Who ever heard of a nigger named Jimboy, anyhow? Next place, I ain’t never seen a yaller dude yet that meant a dark woman no good—an’ Annjee is dark!” Aunt Hager had other objections, too, although she didn’t like to talk evil about folks. But what she probably referred to in her mind was the question of his ancestry, for nobody knew who Jimboy’s parents were.

===Chinese===
“这娃儿随他爹，”洛里姊妹（Sister Lowry）说，“只是肤色没他爹那么浅。不过等他长大，准是个百里挑一的俊小子，没跑！”

“哎，俺也巴望他俊，”黑格外婆说。“可俺宁可他丑点儿，也别变成那个没出息的吉姆博伊第二——来这儿住上一个月，一走就是半年，活儿一点不沾，高兴了才摸一摸。要不是安吉（Annjee），真不知道这个家吃什么；桑迪他爹呀，养儿子是一星半点指望不上的。”

斯坦顿城里的有色人全都知道，黑格对吉姆博伊·罗杰斯（Jimboy Rodgers）没有半点好感——她二女儿嫁的就是这个高高大大、相貌堂堂的浅肤色汉子。

“头一条，俺就不喜欢他那名字，”她私下里常说。“再说，谁听说过黑人取名叫吉姆博伊的？第二条，俺打没见过一个浅皮肤的花花公子，对黑皮肤的女人安过什么好心——安吉可就是黑皮肤！”黑格外婆还有别的嫌弃，只是她不爱讲人坏话。不过她心里指的多半是出身这件事——因为谁也不知道吉姆博伊的爹娘是什么人。

---

===Original===
“Sandy, look out for the house while I run down an’ see how is Mis’ Gavitt’s niece. An’ you-all play outdoors. Don’t bring no chillen in, litterin’ up de place.” About eleven o’clock Aunt Hager pulled a dustcap over her head and put on a clean white apron. “Here, tie it for me, chile,” she said, turning her broad back. “An’ mind you don’t hurt yo’self on no rusty nails and rotten boards left from de storm. I’ll be back atter while.” And she disappeared around the house, walking proudly, her black face shining in the sunlight.

Presently the two boys under the apple-tree were joined by a coal-colored little girl who lived next door, one Willie-Mae Johnson, and the mud balls under her hands became mud pies carefully rounded and patted and placed in the sun on the small box where the little chickens lived. Willie-Mae was the mama, Sandy the papa, and Buster the baby as the old game of “playing house” began anew.

By and by the mailman’s whistle blew and the three children scampered towards the sidewalk to meet him. The carrier handed Sandy a letter. “Take it in the house,” he said. But instead the youngsters sat on the front-door-sill, their feet dangling where the porch had been, and began to examine the envelope.

“I bet that’s Lincoln’s picture,” said Buster.

===Chinese===
“桑迪，俺去瞧瞧加维特太太家那侄女怎么样了，你在家看着点儿门户。你们几个都在外头玩儿，别把哪家娃儿领进屋来，把屋里糟蹋得乱七八糟。”十一点来钟，黑格外婆往头上罩了顶防尘帽，系上一条干净的白围裙。“来，娃儿，替俺把带子系上。”她说着转过宽阔的脊背。“千万留神，别叫风暴留下的锈钉子、烂板子扎着碰着。俺过一阵就回来。”说罢她绕过屋角走远了，脚步昂昂的，黑脸膛在太阳底下放着亮。

不多时，住在隔壁的煤黑煤黑的小姑娘薇莉-梅·约翰逊（Willie-Mae Johnson）也加入了苹果树下的两个男孩。到她手里，泥团变成了泥巴饼：仔细地团了又团、拍了又拍，摆在小鸡住的小木箱上晒太阳。老一套的“过家家”又开演了——薇莉-梅当妈妈，桑迪当爸爸，巴斯特当宝宝。

过了半晌，邮差的哨声响了，三个孩子撒着欢儿奔到人行道跟前迎他。邮递员把一封信递给桑迪。“拿进屋里去。”他说。可几个小家伙偏不，一屁股坐在门槛上——两条小腿在原先门廊的地方悬空晃荡——翻来覆去地端详那只信封。

“我打赌上头是林肯的头像。”巴斯特说。

===Original===
“No, ’taint,” declared Willie-Mae. “It’s Rossiefelt!”

“Aw, it’s Washington,” said Sandy. “And don’t you-all touch my mama’s letter with your hands all muddy. It might be from my papa, you can’t tell, and she wants it kept clean.”

“’Tis from Jimboy,” Aunt Hager declared when she returned, accompanied by her old friend, Sister Whiteside, who peddled on foot fresh garden-truck she raised herself. Aunt Hager had met her at the corner.

“I knows his writin’,” went on Hager. “An’ it’s got a postmark, K-A-N—Kansas City! That’s what ’tis! Niggers sho do love Kansas City!… Huh!… So that’s where he’s at. Well, yo’ mama’ll be glad to get it. If she knowed it was here, she’d quit work an’ come home now.… Sit down, Whiteside. We gwine eat in a few minutes. You better have a bite with us an’ stay an’ rest yo’self awhile, ’cause I knows you been walkin’ this mawnin’!”

===Chinese===
“才不是呢，”薇莉-梅一口咬定，“是罗斯福！”

“咳，那是华盛顿，”桑迪说。“你们俩可别拿泥手碰我妈的信。说不定是我爸寄来的呢——说不准的——她可要这信干干净净的。”

“准是吉姆博伊来的信。”黑格外婆回来看见信便下了断语。跟她一道回来的还有她的老朋友怀特赛德姊妹（Sister Whiteside）——一个徒步走街串巷、卖自家种的时鲜菜蔬的妇人。黑格外婆是在街角碰上她的。

“俺认得他的笔迹，”黑格接着说。“上头还打着邮戳呢，K—A—N——堪萨斯城！错不了！老黑们可真爱堪萨斯城！……哼！……原来他蹲在那儿呢。好嘛，你妈见了这信保管高兴。她要是晓得信到了家，这会儿准撂下活儿往回赶了。……坐吧，怀特赛德。俺们这就开饭，你也凑着吃一口，待下歇歇脚——俺知道你走了一上午啦！”

===Original===
“’Deed I is,” the old sister declared, dropping her basket of lettuce and peas on the floor and taking a chair next to the table in the kitchen. “An’ I ain’t sold much neither. Seems like folks ain’t got no buyin’ appetite after all that storm an’ wind last night—but de Lawd will provide! I ain’t worried.”

“That’s right,” agreed Hager. “Might o’ been me blowed away maself, ’stead o’ just ma porch, if Jesus hadn’t been with us.… You Sandy! Make haste and wash yo’ hands, sir. Rest o’ you chillens go on home, ’cause I know yo’ ma’s lookin’ for you.… Huh! This wood fire’s mighty low!”

Hager uncovered a pot that had been simmering on the stove all morning and dished up a great bowlful of black-eyed peas and salt pork. There was biscuit bread left from breakfast. A plate of young onions and a pitcher of lemonade stood on the white oilcloth-covered table. Heads were automatically bowed.

“Lawd, make us thankful for this food. For Christ’s sake, amen,” said Hager; then the two old women and the child began to eat.

===Chinese===
“可不是，走了不老少，”老姊妹说着，把那篮子莴苣和豌豆搁在地板上，在厨房桌边拣了把椅子坐下。“也没卖出多少。昨晚那场风暴一刮，大伙儿像是连买菜的胃口都没了——不过主自会预备！俺不发愁。”

“这话不假，”黑格应道。“要不是耶稣跟俺们同在，刮走的兴许就是俺这条老命，不光是个门廊喽。……桑迪你！快点洗手去，小子！其余的娃儿都回各家去，俺晓得你们娘正找呢。……哼！这炉子的柴火可太弱啦！”

黑格揭开在炉子上煨了一上午的锅，盛出满满一大碗黑眼豆炖咸猪肉，还有早饭剩下的烤饼。一盘嫩洋葱、一罐柠檬水，摆在铺白油布的桌上。几个脑袋不由自主地垂了下来。

“主啊，叫我们为这饭食献上感恩。奉基督之名，阿们。”黑格祷告罢，两个老妇人和孩子便吃了起来。

===Original===
“That’s Elvira’s boy, ain’t it—that yaller-headed young-one was here playin’ with Sandy?” Sister Whiteside had her mouth full of onions and beans as she asked the question.

“Shsss!… That’s her child!” said Hager. “But it ain’t Eddie’s!” She gave her guest a meaning glance across the table, then lowered her voice, pretending all the while that Sandy’s ears were too young to hear. “They say she had that chile ’fore she married Eddie. An’ black as Eddie is, you knows an’ I knows ain’t due to be no golden hair in de family!”

“I knowed there must be something funny,” whispered the old sister, screwing up her face. “That’s some white man’s chile!”

“Sho it is!” agreed Hager.… “I knowed it all de time.… Have some mo’ meat, Whiteside. Help yo’self! We ain’t got much, but such as ’tis, you’re welcome.… Yes, sir, Buster’s some white man’s chile.… Stop reachin’ cross de table for bread, Sandy. Where’s yo’ manners, sir? I declare, chillens do try you sometimes.… Pass me de onions.”

===Chinese===
“那是伊尔维拉家的娃儿吧——方才跟桑迪一块儿玩的那个金头发崽子？”怀特赛德姊妹问这话时，嘴里塞满了洋葱和豆子。

“嘘！……娃是她生的没错！”黑格说。“可不是埃迪（Eddie）的种！”她隔着桌子朝客人递了个心照不宣的眼色，随即压低嗓音，只装作桑迪耳朵还嫩、听不懂似的。“听说她嫁埃迪之前就怀上那娃儿了。埃迪黑成那样，你晓得、俺也晓得——他家门里哪能蹦出个金头发来！”

“俺就知道这里头必有蹊跷，”老姊妹挤眉皱脸地悄声道。“那是哪个白人的种！”

“可不是嘛！”黑格深表同意。……“俺打头就知道。……再吃点儿肉，怀特赛德，别客气！俺们没啥好东西，可就这么些，也管你吃个够。……可不，巴斯特就是哪个白人的种。……桑迪，别隔着桌子够面包。你的规矩呢，小子？真是的，娃儿们有时候真磨人的性子。……把洋葱递给俺。”

===Original===
“Truth, they tries you, yit I gits right lonesome since all ma young ones is gone.” Sister Whiteside worked her few good teeth vigorously, took a long swallow of lemonade, and smacked her lips. “Chillen an’ grandchillen all in Chicago an’ St. Louis an’ Wichita, an’ nary chick nor child left with me in de house.… Pass me de bread, thank yuh.… I feels kinder sad an’ sorry at times, po’ widder-woman that I is. I has ma garden an’ ma hens, but all ma chillens done grown and married.… Where’s yo’ daughter Harriett at now, Hager? Is she married, too? I ain’t seen her lately.”

Hager pulled a meat skin through her teeth; then she answered: “No, chile, she too young to marry yet! Ain’t but sixteen, but she’s been workin’ out this summer, waitin’ table at de Stanton County Country Club. Been in de country three weeks now, since school closed, but she comes in town on Thursdays, though. It’s nigh six miles from here, so de women-help sleeps there at night. I’s glad she’s out there, Sister. Course Harriett’s a good girl, but she likes to be frisky—wants to run de streets ’tendin’ parties an’ dances, an’ I can’t do much with her no mo’, though I hates to say it.”

“But she’s a songster, Hager! An’ I hears she’s sho one smart chile, besides. They say she’s up with them white folks when it comes to books. An’ de high school where she’s goin’ ain’t easy.… All ma young ones quit ’fore they got through with it—wouldn’t go—ruther have a good time runnin’ to Kansas City an’ galavantin’ round.”

“De Lawd knows it’s a hard job, keepin’ colored chillens in school, Sister Whiteside, a mighty hard job. De niggers don’t help ’em, an’ de white folks don’t care if they stay or not. An’ when they gets along sixteen an’ seventeen, they wants this, an’ they wants that, an’ t’other—an’ when you ain’t got it to give to ’em, they quits school an’ goes to work.… Harriett say she ain’t goin’ back next fall. I feels right hurt over it, but she ’clares she ain’t goin’ back to school. Says there ain’t no use in learnin’ books fo’ nothin’ but to work in white folks’ kitchens when she’s graduated.”

===Chinese===
“说的是，磨人归磨人，可俺那些娃儿一个个都走了，俺冷清得慌呢。”怀特赛德姊妹费劲地咀嚼着——她没剩几颗好牙了——又灌下一大口柠檬水，咂了咂嘴。“儿子闺女、孙子孙辈全在芝加哥、圣路易斯、威奇托，家里连只鸡带个娃都不剩。……面包递俺一下，谢谢你。……俺这苦命的寡妇，有时候心里怪凄惶、怪委屈的。园子是俺的，老母鸡也是俺的，可娃儿们全都长大成人、成家立业喽。……你家闺女哈丽雅特这会儿在哪儿呢，黑格？她也成家了吗？好久没见着她了。”

黑格从牙缝里拽出一条肉皮，这才答话：“没有呢，老妹子，她还小，不到嫁人的年纪！才十六。不过今年夏天出去做工了，在斯坦顿县乡村俱乐部（Stanton County Country Club）当侍应。学堂放假起，就在乡下待了三个礼拜了，不过每逢礼拜四倒也进趟城。离这儿足有六英里，所以女帮工夜里都睡在那边。她出去，俺倒是放心的，姊妹。哈丽雅特（Harriett）本是个好闺女，就是爱疯玩——想满街跑着赴宴会、赶舞会，俺如今管不大动她了，虽说这话俺不愿意讲。”

“可她天生是块唱歌的料啊，黑格！俺还听说，她顶顶聪明着呢。都说念起书来，她不输那些白人孩子。她要上的那所高中，可不是好念的。……俺那些娃儿，没一个念出头——不肯念——宁可跑堪萨斯城寻快活、四处撒野。”

“主晓得，供有色人家的娃儿念书是桩难事啊，怀特赛德姊妹，天大的难事。黑人自己不帮衬，白人呢，念不念书都懒得管。等他们长到十六七，要这个、要那个、还要别的——你拿不出来给他们，他们就退学做工去了。……哈丽雅特说秋天不回学堂了。俺心里怪不好受的，可她一口咬定不回。说念书念到头，毕了业还不是进白人的厨房干活——白念一场。”

===Original===
“Do she, Hager? I’s sho sorry! I’s gwine to talk to that gal. Get Reverend Berry to talk to her, too.… You’s struggled to bring up yo’ chillens, an’ all we Christians in de church ought to help you! I gwine see Reverend Berry, see can’t he ’suade her to stay in school.” The old woman reached for the onions. “But you ain’t never raised no boys, though, has you, Hager?”

“No, I ain’t. My two boy-chillens both died ’fore they were ten. Just these three girls—Tempy, an’ Annjee, an’ Harriett—that’s all I got. An’ this here grandchile, Sandy.… Take yo’ hands off that meat, sir! You had ’nough!”

“Lawd, you’s been lucky! I done raised seven grandchillen ’sides eight o’ ma own. An’ they don’t thank me. No, sir! Go off and kick up they heels an’ git married an’ don’t thank me a bit! Don’t even write, some of ’em.… Waitin’ fo’ me to die, I reckon, so’s they can squabble over de little house I owns an’ ma garden.” The old visitor pushed back her chair. “Huh! Yo’ dinner was sho good!… Waitin’ fo’ me to die.”

“Unhuh!… That’s de way with ’em, Sister Whiteside. Chillens don’t care—but I reckon we old ones can’t kick much. They’s got to get off fo’ themselves. It’s natural, that’s what ’tis. Now, my Tempy, she’s married and doin’ well. Got a fine house, an’ her husband’s a mail-clerk in de civil service makin’ good money. They don’t ’sociate no mo’ with none but de high-toned colored folks, like Dr. Mitchell, an’ Mis’ Ada Walls, an’ Madam C. Frances Smith. Course Tempy don’t come to see me much ’cause I still earns ma livin’ with ma arms in de tub. But Annjee run in their house out o’ the storm last night an’ she say Tempy’s just bought a new pianer, an’ de house looks fine.… I’s glad fo’ de chile.”

===Chinese===
“是吗，黑格？俺真难过！俺得去跟那丫头好好谈谈。再请贝里牧师（Reverend Berry）也去劝劝。……你苦哈哈拉扯大这几个娃儿，咱们教会里的基督徒都该搭把手！俺这就去找贝里牧师，看他能不能劝她留下念书。”老太太伸手去够洋葱。“不过你到底没拉扯过男娃，是吧，黑格？”

“是没有。俺两个男娃都没活过十岁。就这三个闺女——坦皮（Tempy）、安吉、哈丽雅特——这就是俺全部的指望了。再有就是这个外孙，桑迪。……手从肉上拿开，小子！你吃够啦！”

“主啊，你算有福的！俺自己生了八个，之外又拉扯大七个孙辈。他们可不领俺的情。才不呢！拍拍屁股走人，撒欢儿、结婚，一点儿不念俺的好！有的连信都不来一封。……俺估摸着，就巴着俺死呢，好来争俺这几间小破屋跟那个园子。”做客的老太太把椅子往后一推。“哼！你这顿饭可真香！……巴着俺死呢。”

“嗯哼！……娃儿们都是这德行，怀特赛德姊妹。娃儿的心不在老人身上——不过俺们这些老的，也没什么好抱怨的。他们总得各奔前程。天经地义，就是这么回事。就说俺家坦皮吧，结了婚，过得挺不错。房子体面，男人在邮政里当文职，挣着挺不错的钱。如今除了米切尔医生（Dr. Mitchell）、艾达·沃尔斯太太（Mis' Ada Walls）、C·弗朗西丝·史密斯夫人（Madam C. Frances Smith）这些上流体面的有色人，他们是谁也不来往了。坦皮当然不大来看俺，因为俺还靠这双胳膊在洗衣盆里挣饭吃呢。不过昨晚安吉为躲风暴跑进他们家，回来说坦皮刚买了一架新钢琴，家里布置得可气派了。……俺替这闺女高兴。”

===Original===
“Sho, sho you is, Sister Williams, you’s a good mother an’ I knows you’s glad. But I hears from Reverend Berry that Tempy’s done withdrawed from our church an’ joined de Episcopals!”

“That’s right! She is. Last time I seed Tempy, she told me she couldn’t stand de Baptist no mo’—too many low niggers belonging, she say, so she’s gonna join Father Hill’s church, where de best people go.… I told her I didn’t think much o’ joinin’ a church so far away from God that they didn’t want nothin’ but yaller niggers for members, an’ so full o’ forms an’ fashions that a good Christian couldn’t shout—but she went on an’ joined. It’s de stylish temple, that’s why, so I ain’t said no mo’. Tempy’s goin’ on thirty-five now, she’s ma oldest chile, an’ I reckon she knows how she wants to act.”

“Yes, I reckon she do.… But there ain’t no church like de Baptist, praise God! Is there, Sister? If you ain’t been dipped in that water an’ half drowned, you ain’t saved. Tempy don’t know like we do. No, sir, she don’t know!”

There was no fruit or dessert, and the soiled plates were not removed from the table for a long time, for the two old women, talking about their children, had forgotten the dishes. Young flies crawled over the biscuit bread and hummed above the bowl of peas, while the wood fire died in the stove, and Sandy went out into the sunshine to play.

===Chinese===
“可不是，你当然高兴，威廉斯姊妹（Sister Williams），你是个好娘，俺晓得你心里欢喜。可俺听贝里牧师说，坦皮已经退了咱们教会，改入圣公会了！”

“是真的！入了。上回俺见着坦皮，她跟俺说，她再也受不了浸信会——说入会的下等老黑太多，她要入希尔神父（Father Hill）的教会，体面人都往那儿去。……俺跟她说，这种教会离上帝远着呢，会员专挑浅皮肤的黑人，礼数排场多得连个好基督徒都没法喊灵（shout，圣灵感动下的呼喊舞）——俺看不上——可她还是入了自己的。图它时兴体面呗，所以俺也没再言语。坦皮眼瞅着三十五了，是俺最大的闺女，她想怎么做人，想必自己有数。”

“是啊，想必她有数。……可天下没有哪个教会比得上咱们浸信会，荣耀归主！是不是，姊妹？不经那一浸、不在水里呛个半死，就算不得得救。坦皮可不懂得咱们的道理。不懂，先生，她真不懂！”

桌上没有水果，也没有甜点，用过的盘子久久没人收拾——两个老妇人聊起各自的儿女，把碗碟全忘到了脑后。初生的小苍蝇在烤饼上爬，在豆碗上方嗡嗡打转；炉膛里的柴火渐渐熄了，桑迪出到太阳地里玩去了。

===Original===
“Now, ma girl, Maggie,” said Sister Whiteside; “de man she married done got to be a big lawyer in St. Louis. He’s in de politics there, an’ Maggie’s got a fine job herself—social servin’, they calls it. But I do’t hear from her once a year. An’ she don’t send me a dime. Ma boys looks out for me, though, sometimes, round Christmas. There’s Lucius, what runs on de railroad, an’ then Andrew, what rides de horses, an’ John, in Omaha, sends me a little change now an’ then—all but Charlie, an’ he never was thoughtful ’bout his mother. He ain’t never sent me nothin’.”

“Well, you sho is lucky,” said Hager, “’cause they ain’t no money comes in this house, Christmas nor no other time, less’n me an’ Annjee brings it here. Jimboy ain’t no good, an’ what Harriett makes goes for clothes and parties an’ powderin’-rags. Course, I takes some from her every week, but I gives it right back for her school things. An’ I ain’t taken nothin’ from her these three weeks she’s been workin’ at de club. She say she’s savin’ her money herself. She’s past sixteen now, so I lets her have it.… Po’ little thing!… She does need to look purty.” Hager’s voice softened and her dark old face was half abashed, kind and smiling. “You know, last month I bought her a gold watch—surprise fo’ her birthday, de kind you hangs on a little pin on yo’ waist. Lawd knows, I couldn’t ’ford it—took all de money from three week’s o’ washin’, but I knowed she’d been wantin’ a watch. An’ this front room—I moved ma bed out last year an’ bought that new rug at de secondhand store an’ them lace curtains so’s she could have a nice place to entertain her comp’ny.… But de chile goes with such a kinder wild crowd o’ young folks, Sister Whiteside! It worries me! The boys, they cusses, an’ the girls, they paints, an’ some of ’em live in de Bottoms. I been tried to get her out of it right along, but seems like I can’t. That’s why I’s glad she’s in de country fo’ de summer an’ comes in but only once a week, an’ then she’s home with me. It’s too far to come in town at night, she say, so she gets her rest now, goin’ to bed early an’ all, with de country air round her. I hopes she calms down from runnin’ round when she comes back here to stay in de fall.… She’s a good chile. She don’t lie to me ’bout where she goes, nor nothin’ like that, but she’s just wild, that’s all, just wild.”

“Is she a Christian, Sister Williams?”

“No, she ain’t. I’s sorry to say it of a chile o’ mine, but she ain’t. She’s been on de moaner’s bench time after time, Sunday mawnin’s an’ prayer-meetin’ evenin’s, but she never would rise. I prays for her.”

===Chinese===
“再说俺闺女玛吉（Maggie），”怀特赛德姊妹说；“她嫁的那个男人，如今在圣路易斯当上了大律师，还在那儿掺和政治；玛吉自己也有一份好差事——他们管那叫社会工作。可她一年也搭不上一封信，一分钱也不捎给俺。倒是俺那几个儿子，有时候还惦记着俺，圣诞节前后孝敬点儿。卢修斯（Lucius）在铁路上跑车，安德鲁（Andrew）是赛马骑师，约翰（John）在奥马哈，时不时捎点零钱来——就数查利（Charlie）不成器，他打小心眼儿里就没有娘，啥也没给俺捎过。”

“哎，你算有福气的，”黑格说，“这个家可没钱进来——圣诞节不进，别的日子也不进，除非俺跟安吉挣回来。吉姆博伊指望不上，哈丽雅特挣的呢，都花在衣裳、舞会跟粉扑上了。往常俺每礼拜也拿她点儿，可转身就给她添了书本文具还回去了。这三个礼拜她在俱乐部帮工，俺一分钱没动她的。她说她自个儿攒着呢。她过了十六了，俺就由她攥着。……可怜的小东西！……她也确实得穿得俊点儿。”黑格的声音软了下来，那张黑苍苍的老脸半是羞赧，又慈祥，又含着笑。“你知道吗，上个月俺给她买了块金表——给她生日的一个惊喜，就是那种用小别针别在腰上挂着的那种表。主晓得，俺哪里买得起——花掉了整整三个礼拜的洗衣钱——可俺知道她一直盼着块表。还有这间前屋——去年俺把床搬了出去，在旧货店买了那条新地毯，又添了那副花边窗帘，好让她有个体面地方招待她的客人。……可这娃儿净跟一伙野里野气的年轻人混，怀特赛德姊妹！愁死俺了！男孩儿满嘴脏话，女孩儿个个涂脂抹粉，有几个还住在洼地区（the Bottoms，城边的黑人贫民区）。俺一直想把她从那伙人里拽出来，可就是拽不动。所以她这个夏天待在乡下，一礼拜只进城一趟，回来了还守着俺，俺倒踏实。她说夜里进城太远，所以如今歇息得也好，早早睡觉，乡下空气围着。但愿她秋天搬回来长住，能收收心，别再满处野跑。……她是个好娃儿。去哪儿从不骗俺，没那些事——就是野，就这么回事，野。”

“她是基督徒吗，威廉斯姊妹？”

“不是，还不是。自己的娃儿，说这话俺心里难受，可她真不是。主日早上、祷告会的晚上，她一回又一回坐上哀哭席（moaner's bench，教会里尚未得救的人坐的祷告席），可她就是不肯站起来归主。俺只有为她祷告。”

===Original===
“Well, when she takes Jesus, she’ll see de light! That’s what de matter with her, Sister Williams, she ain’t felt Him yit. Make her go to church when she comes back here.… I reckon you heard ’bout when de big revival’s due to come off this year, ain’t you?”

“No, I ain’t, not yet.”

“Great colored tent-meetin’ with de Battle-Ax of de Lawd, Reverend Braswell preachin’! Yes, sir! Gwine start August eighteenth in de Hickory Woods yonder by de edge o’ town.”

“Good news,” cried Hager. “Mo’ sinners than enough’s in need o’ savin’. I’s gwine to take Sandy an’ get him started right with de Lawd. An’ if that onery Jimboy’s back here, I gwine make him go, too, an’ look Jesus in de face. Annjee an’ me’s saved, chile!… You, Sandy, bring us some drinkin’ water from de pump.” Aunt Hager rapped on the window with her knuckles to the boy playing outside. “An’ stop wrastlin’ with that gal.”

===Chinese===
“唉，等她接受了耶稣，自会见到光明！她的病根儿就在这儿，威廉斯姊妹——她还没感受到主呢。她搬回来以后，你可千万押着她上教堂。……今年那场大奋兴会定在什么时候开，你总归听说了吧？”

“没呢，还没听着。”

“有色人的大帐篷布道会，有‘主的战斧’布拉斯韦尔牧师（Reverend Braswell）主道！可不！八月十八开锣，就在城边那头山核桃林（Hickory Woods）里。”

“好消息啊！”黑格嚷道。“等着拯救的罪人多着呢，越多越好。俺要带上桑迪，让他在主的道上起个好头。那个不长进的吉姆博伊要是回来了，俺也押他去，叫他也正眼瞧一瞧耶稣。安吉跟俺可是得了救的，老妹子！……哎，桑迪！从压水井给俺们打点儿凉水来。”黑格外婆屈起指节敲敲窗子，唤着在外头玩的男孩。“还有，别跟那丫头摔跤打闹了！”

===Original===
Sandy rose triumphant from the prone body of black little Willie-Mae, lying squalling on the cinderpath near the back gate. “She started it,” he yelled, running towards the pump. The girl began a reply, but at that moment a rickety wagon drawn by a white mule and driven by a grey-haired, leather-colored old man came rattling down the alley.

“Hy, there, Hager!” called an old Negro, tightening his reins on the mule, which immediately began to eat corn-tops over the back fence. “How you been treatin’ yo’self?”

“Right tolable,” cried Hager, for she and Sister Whiteside had both emerged from the kitchen and were approaching the driver. “How you doin’, Brother Logan?”

“Why, if here ain’t Sis’ Whiteside, too!” said the old beau, sitting up straight on his wagon-seat and showing a row of ivory teeth in a wide grin. “I’s doin’ purty well for a po’ widower what ain’t got nobody to bake his bread. Doin’ purty well. Hee! Hee! None o’ you-all ain’t sorry for me, is you? How de storm treat you, Hager?… Says it carried off yo’ porch?… That’s certainly too bad! Well, it did some o’ these white folks worse’n that. I got ’nough work to do to last me de next fo’ weeks, cleanin’ up yards an’ haulin’ off trash, me an’ dis mule here.… How’s yo’ chillen, Sis’ Williams?”

===Chinese===
桑迪从黑黢黢的小薇莉-梅身上得胜爬起——小姑娘仰面躺在后门边煤渣小径上，正扯着嗓子嚎呢。“是她先动手的。”他一边嚷一边朝压水井跑。小姑娘刚要回嘴，正在这当口，一辆嘎啦作响的破马车顺着小巷辘辘驶来——拉车的是头白骡子，赶车的是个头发花白、脸膛皮革色的老头。

“嗨，黑格！”一个黑人老头扬声招呼，一边收紧骡子的缰绳；那骡子立刻探过头去，啃起后栅栏外探进来的玉米梢。“这一向待自己可好啊？”

“还凑合！”黑格扬声答道——她和怀特赛德姊妹都已从厨房出来，正朝赶车的走近。“你怎么样，洛根老爹（Brother Logan）？”

“哟嗬，这不是怀特赛德姊妹也在这儿嘛！”老风流在车座上坐直了身子，咧开大嘴一笑，露出一口象牙般的牙。“俺这穷鳏夫，没人给烤面包，混得还算不赖。不赖。嘻嘻！你们里头没一个心疼俺的，是不是？风暴待你可好，黑格？……听说把你家门廊刮跑啦？……那可真遭罪！哼，它把有些白人家里糟践得比这还厉害呢。往后四个礼拜，俺跟这头骡子有得忙喽——清扫院子、拉运垃圾什么的。……你家娃儿们都好啊，威廉斯姊妹？”

===Original===
“Oh, they all right, thank yuh. Annjee’s still at Mis’ Rice’s, an’ Harriet’s in de country at de club.”

“Is she?” said Brother Logan. “I seed her in town night ’fore last down on Pearl Street ’bout ten o’clock.”

“You ain’t seed Harriett no night ’fore last,” disputed Hager vigorously. “She don’t come in town ’ceptin’ Thursday aftenoons, an’ that’s tomorrow.”

“Sister, I ain’t blind,” said the old man, hurt that his truth should be doubted. “I—seen—Harriett Williams on Pearl Street… with Maudel Smothers an’ two boys ’bout ten o’clock day before yestidy night! An’ they was gwine to de Waiters’ Ball, ’cause I asked Maudel where they was gwine, an’ she say so. Then I says to Harriett: ‘Does yo’ mammy know you’s out this late?’ an’ she laughed an’ say: ‘Oh, that’s all right!’… Don’t tell me I ain’t seen Harriett, Hager.”

===Chinese===
“哦，都好，谢你惦记。安吉还在赖斯太太家，哈丽雅特在乡下俱乐部里。”

“是吗？”洛根老爹说。“前天夜里十来点钟，俺在珀尔街上可瞧见她了。”

“你前天夜里可见不着哈丽雅特，”黑格斩钉截铁地反驳。“她除了礼拜四下午从来不进城——礼拜四可是明天。”

“老姊妹，俺眼又没瞎，”老头儿见自己的实话遭了怀疑，老大委屈。“俺——亲——眼——瞧见哈丽雅特·威廉斯在珀尔街上……跟莫德尔·斯莫瑟斯（Maudel Smothers），还有两个小伙子，前天夜里十点来钟！他们是去赴侍者舞会（Waiters' Ball）——俺问莫德尔上哪儿去，她亲口说的。俺还跟哈丽雅特讲：‘你娘晓得你在外头逛到这么晚吗？’她笑说：‘嗐，没事儿！’……别跟俺说俺没瞧见哈丽雅特，黑格。”

===Original===
“Well, Lawd help!” Aunt Hager cried, her mouth open. “You done seed my chile in town an’ she ain’t come anear home! Stayed all night at Maudel’s, I reckon.… I tells her ’bout runnin’ with that gal from de Bottoms. That’s what makes her lie to me—tellin’ me she don’t come in town o’ nights. Maudel’s folks don’t keep no kind o’ house, and mens goes there, an’ they sells licker, an’ they gambles an’ fights.… Is you sho that’s right, Brother Logan, ma chile done been in town an’ ain’t come home?”

“It ain’t wrong!” said old man Logan, cracking his long whip on the white mule’s haunches. “Gittiyap! You ole jinny!” and he drove off.

“Um-uh!” said Sister Whiteside to Hager as the two toil-worn old women walked towards the house. “That’s de way they does you!” The peddler gathered up her things. “I better be movin’, ’cause I got these greens to sell yit, an’ it’s gittin’ ’long towards evenin’.… That’s de way chillens does you, Sister Williams! I knows! That’s de way they does!”

===Chinese===
“哎呀，主啊！”黑格外婆张着嘴喊。“你瞧见俺娃儿进了城，她却连家门边都没沾一下！俺琢磨着，是在莫德尔家过的夜。……俺总跟她说，别跟洼地区那个丫头片子混。瞧瞧——这就是她跟俺撒谎的根儿——哄俺说她夜里不进城。莫德尔家算个什么人家：男人成天往那儿跑，卖酒，赌钱，还动拳脚。……洛根老爹，你说的千真万确——俺娃儿真进了城，没回家？”

“一点不假！”洛根老爹说着，长鞭一扬，啪地抽在白骡子的胯上。“驾！老骒马！”赶着车走远了。

“哼嗯！”两个操劳一世的老妇人朝屋子走去，怀特赛德姊妹对黑格说。“他们就是这么待你的！”卖菜的收拾起自己的东西。“俺该动身了——这担子青菜还没卖脱呢，天也快擦黑儿了。……娃儿们就是这么待你的，威廉斯姊妹！俺晓得！就是这么待你的！”

## III Jimboy’s Letter / 三 吉姆博伊的信

===Original===
> I been laying off to written you ever since I left home but you know how it is. Work has not been so good here. Am with a section gang of coloreds and greeks and somehow strained my back on the Union Pacific laying ties so I will be home on Saturday. Will do my best to try and finish out weak here. Love my darling wife also kiss my son Sandy for me. Am dying to see you,
> affectionately as ever and allways 

 till the judgment day,
 

 **Jimboy Rodgers**

===Chinese===
> 俺自打离家就一门心思想给你写信，可你也知道是个啥光景。这边的活计不咋地。俺跟一帮有色人和希腊人编在一个养路班里，在联合太平洋铁路（Union Pacific）上铺枕木，不知咋的就把腰闪了，所以俺礼拜六就回家。俺尽量想法儿把这儿这个礼拜干完再说。爱俺的宝贝媳妇，也替俺亲亲俺的儿子桑迪。俺想死你们了，
> 深情如昔、永永远远

 直到审判日，

 吉姆博伊·罗杰斯（Jimboy Rodgers）

===Original===
“Strained his back, has he? Unhuh! An’ then comes writin’ ’bout till de judgment day!” Hager muttered when she heard it. “Always something wrong with that nigger! He’ll be back here now, layin’ round, doin’ nothin’ fo’ de rest o’ de summer, turnin’ ma house into a theatre with him an’ Harriett singin’ their ragtime, an’ that guitar o’ his’n wangin’ ever’ evenin’! ’Tween him an’ Harriett both it’s a wonder I ain’t plumb crazy. But Harriett do work fo’ her livin’. She ain’t no loafer. … Huh! … Annjee, you was sho a fool when you married that boy, an’ you still is! … I’s gwine next do’ to Sister Johnson’s!” Aunt Hager went out the back and across the yard, where, next door, Tom and Sarah Johnson, Willie-Mae’s grandparents, sat on a bench against the sidewall of an unpainted shanty. They were both quietly smoking their corncob pipes in the evening dusk.

Sandy, looking at the back of the letter that his mother held, stood at the kitchen-table rapidly devouring a large piece of fresh lemon pie, which she had brought from Mrs. J. J. Rice’s. Annjee had said to save the two cold fried lamb chops until tomorrow, or Sandy would have eaten those, too.

“Wish you’d brought home some more pie,” the boy declared, his lips white with meringue, but Annjee, who had just got in from work, paid no attention to her son’s appreciative remarks on her cookery.

===Chinese===
「闪了腰，是吧？哼！回头还写什么『直到审判日』！」黑格一听就嘟囔开了。「那个老黑总是毛病不断！这下他可要回来了，往后一夏天就闲晃荡，啥也不干，跟哈丽雅特唱他们的拉格泰姆（ragtime），他那把吉他天天晚上弹得叮叮咣咣，把俺家变成个戏园子！有他跟哈丽雅特这俩，俺没彻底疯掉真是个奇迹。可哈丽雅特好歹还干活挣饭吃，她可不是个懒汉。……哼！……安吉，你嫁那小子的时候真是个傻瓜，到如今还是！……俺去隔壁约翰逊姊妹家了！」黑格外婆从后门出去，穿过院子。隔壁院子里，薇莉-梅的爷爷奶奶汤姆·约翰逊和萨拉·约翰逊正坐在一间没刷漆的破棚屋墙根儿的长凳上，两人在暮色里静静地抽着玉米芯烟斗。

桑迪瞅着母亲手里那封信的背面，站在餐桌旁飞快地吞一大块新鲜柠檬派——那是她从赖斯太太家带回来的。安吉说过，那两块炸冷的羊排要留到明天，不然桑迪连那个也一并吃下肚了。

「真希望你多带几块派回来，」男孩嘴上沾满蛋白霜，白花花的。可刚下班进门的安吉，没理会儿子对她手艺的这番夸赞。

===Original===
Instead she said: “Ma certainly ain’t got no time for Jimboy, has she?” and then sat down with the open letter still in her hand—a single sheet of white paper pencilled in large awkward letters. She put it on the table, rested her dark face in her hands, and began to read it again. … She knew how it was, of course, that her husband hadn’t written before. That was all right now. Working all day in the hot sun with a gang of Greeks, a man was tired at night, besides living in a boxcar, where there was no place to write a letter anyway. He was a great big kid, that’s what Jimboy was, cut out for playing. But when he did work, he tried to outdo everybody else. Annjee could see him in her mind, tall and well-built, his legs apart, muscles bulging as he swung the big hammer above his head, driving steel. No wonder he hurt his back, trying to lay more ties a day than anybody else on the railroad. That was just like Jimboy. But she was kind of pleased he had hurt it, since it would bring him home.

“Ain’t you glad he’s comin’, Sandy?”

“Sure,” answered the child, swallowing his last mouthful of pie. “I hope he brings me that gun he promised to buy last Easter.” The boy wiped his sticky hands on the dishcloth and ran out into the backyard, calling: “Willie-Mae! Willie-Mae!”

===Chinese===
她反倒说：「妈是真的一点儿都容不下吉姆博伊，是不是？」说着便坐下来，手里还攥着那封摊开的信——一张白纸，铅笔写的大大咧咧、笨手笨脚的字。她把信放在桌上，双手托住黝黑的脸，又从头读了起来。……他先前一直没写信是怎么回事，她当然明白。现在没事了。整天跟一帮希腊人在毒日头底下干活，夜里人早就乏透了，何况住的还是棚车，那里面压根儿连个写字的地方都没有。他就是个大孩子，吉姆博伊就是这种人，天生是玩的料。可他真干起活来，偏要争着赛过所有人。安吉能在心里看见他：高高大大的好身板，两腿叉开，抡着大锤举过头顶砸钢钎，肌肉一鼓一鼓的。难怪他闪了腰——一天铺的枕木非要比铁路上谁都多不可。这就是吉姆博伊的做派。不过他这腰闪了，她倒有几分高兴，因为这样他就能回家了。

「你爸要回来了，你不高兴吗，桑迪？」

「高兴呀，」孩子咽下最后一口派，答道。「我盼着他把去年复活节答应给我买的那把枪带回来呢。」男孩在抹碗布上擦了擦黏糊糊的手，跑进后院，喊：「薇莉-梅！薇莉-梅！」

===Original===
“Stay right over yonder!” answered his grandmother through the dusk. “Willie-Mae’s in de bed, sir, an’ we old folks settin’ out here tryin’ to have a little peace.” From the tone of Hager’s voice he knew he wasn’t wanted in the Johnson’s yard, so he went back into the house, looked at his mother reading her letter again, and then lay down on the kitchen floor.

“Affectionately as ever and allways till the judgment day,” she read, “Jimboy Rodgers.”

He loved her, Annjee was sure of that, and it wasn’t another woman that made him go away so often. Eight years they’d been married. No, nine—because Sandy was nine, and he was ready to be born when they had had the wedding. And Jimboy left the week after they were married, to go to Omaha, where he worked all winter. When he came back, Sandy was in the world, sitting up sucking meat skins. It was springtime and they bought a piano for the house—but later the instalment man came and took it back to the store. All that summer her husband stayed home and worked a little, but mostly he fished, played pool, taught Harriett to buck-dance, and quarrelled with Aunt Hager. Then in the winter he went to Jefferson City and got a job at the Capitol.

Jimboy was always going, but Aunt Hager was wrong about his never working. It was just that he couldn’t stay in one place all the time. He’d been born running, he said, and had run ever since. Besides, what was there in Stanton anyhow for a young colored fellow to do except dig sewer ditches for a few cents an hour or maybe porter around a store for seven dollars a week. Colored men couldn’t get many jobs in Stanton, and foreigners were coming in, taking away what little work they did have. No wonder he didn’t stay home. Hadn’t Annjee’s father been in Stanton forty years and hadn’t he died with Aunt Hager still taking in washings to help keep up the house?

===Chinese===
「就在那边待着，别过来！」外婆隔着暮色应道。「薇莉-梅睡觉了，小家伙，俺们老人家坐在这儿想图个清静呢。」听黑格这口气，他就知道约翰逊家的院子不欢迎他，于是回屋去了，看了看又在读信的母亲，便在厨房的地板上躺下了。

「『深情如昔、永永远远，直到审判日』，」她念出声来，「吉姆博伊·罗杰斯。」

他是爱她的，这一点安吉笃定，他三天两头往外跑，并不是因为有了别的女人。他们结婚八年了。不对，九年——因为桑迪九岁了，他们办喜事那会儿他已经要出世了。婚后一个礼拜吉姆博伊就走了，去奥马哈，在那儿干了一冬天的活。等他回来，桑迪已经来到这世上，会坐着嘬肉皮了。正是春天，他们给家里买了一架钢琴——可后来分期付款的收账人上了门，又把它抬回了铺子。那一夏天她丈夫待在家里，零零星星干了点活，多半时候钓鱼、打台球，教哈丽雅特跳巴克舞（buck-dance），跟黑格外婆拌嘴。到了冬天，他去杰斐逊城，在州议会大厦寻了个差事。

吉姆博伊总是往外跑，可说他从来不干活，黑格外婆可就冤枉他了。他只是一年到头守不住一个地方。他说自己生下来就在跑，打那以后一直跑到现在。再说，斯坦顿这地方，一个年轻的黑人小伙子能干啥？无非是挖阴沟，一小时挣几分钱，再不就是在铺子里当搬运打杂，一个礼拜七块钱。黑人在斯坦顿找不着几个活儿，外国人还不断涌进来，把本来就少得可怜的活计也抢走了。难怪他不愿待在家里。安吉的父亲在斯坦顿住了四十年，到死不还得靠黑格外婆给人洗衣裳，挣钱撑着这个家？

===Original===
There was no well-paid work for Negro men, so Annjee didn’t blame Jimboy for going away looking for something better. She’d go with him if it wasn’t for her mother. If she went, though, Aunt Hager wouldn’t have anybody for company but Harriett, and Harriett was the youngest and wildest of the three children. With Pa Williams dead going on ten years, Hager washing every day, Tempy married, and Annjee herself out working, there had been nobody to take much care of the little sister as she grew up. Harriett had had no raising, even though she was smart and in high school. A female child needed care. But she could sing! Lawdy! And dance, too! That was another reason why Aunt Hager didn’t like Jimboy. The devil’s musicianer, she called him, straight from hell, teaching Harriett buck-and-winging! But when he took his soft-playing guitar and picked out spirituals and old-time Christian hymns on its sweet strings, Hager forgot she was his enemy, and sang and rocked with the rest of them. When Jimboy was home, you couldn’t get lonesome or blue.

“Gee, I’ll be glad when he comes!” Annjee said to herself. “But if he goes off again, I’ll feel like dying in this dead old town. I ain’t never been away from here nohow.” She spoke aloud to the dim oil-lamp smoking on the table and the sleeping boy on the floor. “I believe I’ll go with him next time. I declare I do!” And then, realizing that Jimboy had never once told her when he was leaving or for what destination, she amended her utterance. “I’ll follow him, though, as soon as he writes.” Because, almost always after he had been away two or three weeks, he would write. “I’ll follow him, sure, if he goes off again. I’ll leave Sandy here and send money back to mama. Then Harriett could settle down and take care of ma and stop runnin’ the streets so much. … Yes, that’s what I’ll do next time!”

===Chinese===
黑人男子没有来钱的工作，所以吉姆博伊出门另寻出路，安吉并不怪他。若不是为了母亲，她早就跟他一起走了。可她要是走了，黑格外婆身边除了哈丽雅特就没个做伴的人了，而哈丽雅特又是三个孩子里最小、最野的一个。威廉斯老爹去世快十年了，黑格天天洗衣裳，坦皮出了嫁，安吉自己在外帮佣，小妹妹长大的这些年，家里就没人怎么管教过她。哈丽雅特算是没人调教着长大的，尽管她人机灵，都上中学了。女孩子家总得有人照管。可她多会唱啊！老天爷！还那么会跳舞！这也是黑格外婆不喜欢吉姆博伊的另一个缘由。她管他叫魔鬼的乐师，从地狱里直接来的，教哈丽雅特跳巴克翼步舞（buck-and-wing）！可每当吉姆博伊抱起他那把音色柔润的吉他，在甜甜的琴弦上拨出黑人灵歌和老派的基督教赞美诗，黑格就忘了自己是他的对头，跟着大伙儿一块儿唱啊、摇啊。吉姆博伊在家的时候，你怎么也孤独不起来、忧郁不起来。

「哎呀，他回来我可就高兴了！」安吉自言自语。「可他要是再走，我待在这个死气沉沉的老城里，真会觉得活不下去。我长这么大压根儿没离开过这儿。」她是对着桌上冒烟的昏黄油灯和地板上熟睡的儿子说出声来的。「我想下回我就跟他一块儿走。真的，我就是这么想的！」接着她想起，吉姆博伊从来不曾告诉过她什么时候动身、要去哪儿，于是又改了口。「不过，等他来信，我就追他去。」因为几乎回回都是这样：他外出两三个礼拜之后就会来信。「他要是再走，我肯定追去。我把桑迪留在家里，给妈寄钱回来。那样哈丽雅特就能定下心来照顾妈，也不至于老在大街上野跑了。……对，下回我就这么办！」

===Original===
This going away was a new thought, and the dark, strong-bodied young woman at the table suddenly began to dream of the cities she had never seen to which Jimboy would lead her. Why, he had been as far north as Canada and as far south as New Orleans, and it wasn’t anything for him to go to Chicago or Denver any time! He was a travelling man—and she, Annjee, was too meek and quiet, that’s what she was—too stay-at-homish. Never going nowhere, never saying nothing back to those who scolded her or talked about her, not even sassing white folks when they got beside themselves. And every colored girl in town said that Mrs. J. J. Rice was no easy white woman to work for, yet she had been there now five years, accepting everything without a murmur! Most young folks, girls and boys, left Stanton as soon as they could for the outside world, but here she was, Annjelica Williams, going on twenty-eight, and had never been as far as Kansas City!

“I want to travel,” she said to herself. “I want to go places, too.”

But that was why Jimboy married her, because she wasn’t a runabout. He’d had enough of those kind of women before he struck Stanton, he said. St. Louis was full of them, and Chicago running over. She was the first nice girl he’d ever met who lived at home, so he took her. … There were mighty few dark women had a light, strong, good-looking young husband, really a married husband, like Jimboy, and a little brown kid like Sandy.

===Chinese===
「出走」是个新念头。桌旁这个皮肤黝黑、身板结实的年轻女人，忽然做起梦来，梦见那些她从没见过、吉姆博伊将会带她去的城市。可不是嘛，他北到过加拿大，南到过新奥尔良，去芝加哥、去丹佛，对他来说什么时候都是说走就走的事！他是个走南闯北的人——而她，安吉，太温顺、太安静了，就是这么个人——太恋家了。哪儿也不去，谁骂她、嚼她的舌根，她从来不还一句嘴，连白人火头上对她出言不逊的时候，她也不顶撞。城里每个黑人姑娘都说，赖斯太太可不是个好伺候的白人太太，可她到如今在她家干了五年，什么都一声不吭地受着！大多数年轻人，姑娘小子们，一有机会就离开斯坦顿去闯外面的世界，可她呢，安杰莉卡·威廉斯（Annjelica Williams），都快二十八了，连堪萨斯城都没去过！

「我也想去走走，」她对自己说。「我也想去见见世面。」

可吉姆博伊娶她，恰恰因为她不是个四处招摇的女人。他说，来斯坦顿之前，那种女人他见够了。圣路易斯到处都是，芝加哥更是满得往外溢。她是他头一个碰到的正正经经、老老实实住在家里的好姑娘，所以他要了她。……皮肤黝黑的女人里，很少有谁能有一个像吉姆博伊这样浅肤色、壮实、俊俏的年轻丈夫——一个明媒正娶的丈夫——还有一个像桑迪这样的棕色小家伙。

===Original===
“I’m mighty lucky,” Annjee thought, “even if he ain’t here.” And two tears of foolish pride fell from the bright eyes in her round black face. They trickled down on the letter, with its blue lines and pencil-scrawled message, and some of the words on the paper began to blur into purple blots because the pencil had been an indelible one. Quickly she fumbled for a handkerchief to wipe the tears away, when a voice made her start.

“You, Annjee!” cried Aunt Hager in the open door. “Go to bed, chile! Go on! Settin’ up here this late, burnin’ de light an’ lurin’ all sorts o’ night-bugs an’ creepers into de house!” The old woman came in out of the dark. “Lawd! I might anigh stumbled over this boy in de middle o’ de flo’! An’ you ain’t even took off yo’ hat since you got home from work! Is you crazy? Settin’ up here at night with yo’ hat on, an’ lettin’ this chile catch his death o’ cold sleepin’ down on de flo’ long after his bedtime!”

Sheepishly Annjee folded her letter and got up. It was true that she still had on her hat and the sweater she had worn to Mrs. Rice’s. True, too, the whole room was alive with soft-winged moths fluttering against the hot glass of the light—and on the kitchen floor a small, brown-skin, infinitely lovable edition of Jimboy lay sprawled contentedly in his grandmother’s path, asleep!

“He’s my baby!” Annjee said gently, stooping to pick him up. “He’s my baby—me and Jimboy’s baby!”

===Chinese===
「我可真够有福的，」安吉想，「哪怕他不在身边。」两滴傻乎乎、带着得意的泪珠，从她圆圆的黑脸上那双明亮的眼睛里落了下来，滴到那封印着蓝格线、涂满铅笔字的信上。纸上有些字迹开始晕开，成了一团团紫斑——因为用的是不褪色的紫铅笔。她赶紧摸索手帕要擦眼泪，一个声音吓了她一跳。

「你呀，安吉！」黑格外婆站在敞开的门口喊。「睡觉去，孩子！快去！这么晚还坐在这儿，点着灯，把各色各样的夜虫子、爬虫全引进屋里来了！」老太太从黑暗里走进来。「主啊！俺差点儿让这小子绊个跟头，就在这地板当间儿！你打下班进家门到现在，连帽子都没摘！你疯了吗？大晚上戴着帽子干坐着，任这孩子早过了睡觉的钟点还睡在地板上，冻出个好歹来！」

安吉不好意思地折起信，站起身。她确实还戴着帽子，身上还穿着去赖斯太太家时穿的那件毛衣。也确实，满屋子都是扑腾着软翅膀的飞蛾，一只只往油灯滚烫的玻璃罩上撞——而厨房的地板上，一个小小的、棕色皮肤的、无比招人疼的吉姆博伊翻版，正四仰八叉、心满意足地摊在外婆过路的当间儿，睡得香呢！

「他是我的宝贝！」安吉轻声说，弯腰把他抱了起来。「他是我的宝贝——我和吉姆博伊的宝贝！」

## IV Thursday Afternoon / 四 星期四的下午

===Original===
Hager had risen at sunrise. On Thursday she did the Reinharts’ washing, on Fridays she ironed it, and on Saturdays she sent it home, clean and beautifully white, and received as pay the sum of seventy-five cents. During the winter Hager usually did half a dozen washings a week, but during the hot season her customers had gone away, and only the Reinharts, on account of an invalid grandmother with whom they could not travel, remained in Stanton.

Wednesday afternoon Sandy, with a boy named Jimmy Lane, called at the back door for their soiled clothes. Each child took a handle and between them carried the large wicker basket seven blocks to Aunt Hager’s kitchen. For this service Jimmy Lane received five cents a trip, although Sister Lane had repeatedly said to Hager that he needn’t be given anything. She wanted him to learn his Christian duties by being useful to old folks. But Jimmy was not inclined to be Christian. On the contrary, he was a very bad little boy of thirteen, who often led Sandy astray. Sometimes they would run with the basket for no reason at all, then stumble and spill the clothes out on the sidewalk﻿—Mrs. Reinhart’s summer dresses, and drawers, and Mr. Reinhart’s extra-large B.V.D.’s lying generously exposed to the public. Sometimes, if occasion offered, the youngsters would stop to exchange uncouth epithets with strange little white boys who called them “niggers.” Or, again, they might neglect their job for a game of marbles, or a quarter-hour of scrub baseball on a vacant lot; or to tease any little colored girl who might tip timidly by with her hair in tight, well-oiled braids﻿—while the basket of garments would be left forlornly in the street without guardian. But when the clothes were safe in Aunt Hager’s kitchen, Jimmy would usually buy candy with his nickel and share it with Sandy before he went home.

After soaking all night, the garments were rubbed through the suds in the morning; and in the afternoon the colored articles were on the line while the white pieces were boiling seriously in a large tin boiler on the kitchen-stove.
===Chinese===
黑格天一亮就起了床。每逢星期四，她洗赖因哈特家（the Reinharts）的衣裳，星期五熨烫，星期六把洗得干干净净、白白净净的衣裳送回去，挣七毛五分钱。冬天里，黑格一礼拜通常接六家的洗衣活儿，可到了大热天，主顾们都出城避暑去了，只有赖因哈特一家，因为家里有位病弱不堪、没法带出门旅行的老奶奶，还留在斯坦顿。

星期三下午，桑迪和一个叫吉米·莱恩（Jimmy Lane）的男孩到人家后门去取脏衣裳。两个孩子一人抓一边提手，合力把那只大柳条筐抬过七个街区，送到黑格外婆的厨房。跑这一趟，吉米·莱恩能得五分钱，虽然莱恩姊妹（Sister Lane）一再跟黑格说，不必给他什么。她想让儿子通过给老人出力，学会基督徒的本分。可吉米偏偏没什么基督徒气。恰恰相反，他是个十三岁的坏小子，常把桑迪带坏。有时候，俩人会莫名其妙地抬着筐子疯跑，绊一下，衣裳全撒在人行道上——赖因哈特太太的夏裙、衬裤，还有赖因哈特先生特大号的 B.V.D. 牌男式内衣，大大方方摊在众人眼前。有时候，赶上机会，俩小子还要停下来，跟那些开口就骂他们「黑鬼」的陌生白人小男孩对骂几句粗话。再不然，他们也会撂下正事不干，蹲下来打弹子，或在空地上打一刻钟野棒球；或是去逗那些把头发编成抹足了油的紧辫子、怯生生踮着脚走过的黑人小女孩——那筐衣裳就凄凄凉凉撂在街上，没人看管。不过只要衣裳平安到了黑格外婆的厨房，吉米多半会用那五分镍币买糖果，分给桑迪一些，然后才回家。

衣裳泡了一整夜，清早就在肥皂水里搓过一遍；到了下午，有色衣物都晾上了绳，白衣物还在炉子上的一大口白铁煮锅里咕嘟咕嘟地认真煮着。

===Original===
“They sho had plenty this week,” Hager said to her grandson, who sat on the stoop eating a slice of bread and apple butter. “I’s mighty late gettin’ ’em hung out to dry, too. Had no business stoppin’ this mawnin’ to go see sick folks, and me here got all I can do maself! Looks like this warm weather old Mis’ Reinhart must change ever’ piece from her dress to her shimmy three times a day﻿—sendin’ me a washin’ like this here!” They heard the screen-door at the front of the house open and slam. “It’s a good thing they got me to do it fo’ ’em!﻿ ﻿… Sandy, see who’s that at de do’.”

It was Harriett, home from the country club for the afternoon, cool and slender and pretty in her black uniform with its white collar, her smooth black face and neck powdered pearly, and her crinkly hair shining with pomade. She smelled nice and perfumy as Sandy jumped on her like a dog greeting a favorite friend. Harriett kissed him and let him hang to her arm as they went through the bedroom to the kitchen. She carried a brown cardboard suitcase and a wide straw hat in one hand.

“Hello, mama,” she said.
===Chinese===
「他们这礼拜衣裳可真不少，」黑格对孙子说。孩子正坐在门阶上，啃一片抹了苹果膏的面包。「俺晾衣裳也晾得忒晚了。今儿早上压根儿不该停下活儿去看望病人——俺自个儿的活儿都忙不过来哩！瞧这大热天，赖因哈特老太太多半是从外衣到贴身小衫，一天换三遍——给俺送这么一大堆衣裳来洗！」前门纱门「砰」地一响，开了又关上。「多亏他们雇了俺来干！……桑迪，去瞧瞧门口是谁。」

是哈丽雅特，下午从乡村俱乐部回来了。她穿着白领子的黑制服，又清爽又苗条又俊俏，光滑的黑脸蛋和脖子扑了珍珠白的粉，卷曲的头发抹了头油，亮闪闪的。桑迪像小狗迎接喜欢的人似的扑上去，闻到她身上香喷喷的味道。哈丽雅特亲了亲他，让他吊着自己的胳膊，穿过卧室走进厨房。她一手拎着棕色纸板手提箱，拿着一顶宽边草帽。

「你好啊，妈，」她说。

===Original===
Hager poked the boiling clothes with a vigorous splash of her round stick. The steam rose in clouds of soapy vapor.

“I been waitin’ for you, madam!” her mother replied in tones that were not calculated to welcome pleasantly an erring daughter. “I wants to know de truth﻿—was you in town last Monday night or not?”

Harriett dropped her suitcase against the wall. “You seem to have the truth,” she said carelessly. “How’d you get it?﻿ ﻿… Here, Sandy, take this out in the yard and eat it, seed and all.” She gave her nephew a plum she had brought in her pocket. “I *was* in town, but I didn’t have time to come home. I had to go to Maudel’s because she’s making me a dress.”
===Chinese===
黑格拿圆搅衣棍使劲往煮着的衣裳里一捅，溅起一片水花。肥皂水的热气一团一团腾起来。

「俺可等着你呢，大小姐！」做母亲的回话了，那腔调可不是要好好迎接一个不省心的闺女。「俺要知道实话——上礼拜一夜里你到底进没进城？」

哈丽雅特把提箱往墙边一撂。「你倒是把实话弄到手了，」她满不在乎地说。「怎么弄到的？……喏，桑迪，把这个拿到院子里吃掉，连核儿一起。」她递给外甥一颗揣在兜里带回来的李子。「我*确实*进城了，可没工夫回家。我得去莫德尔家，她正给我做一条裙子呢。」

===Original===
“To Maudel’s!﻿ ﻿… Unhuh! An’ to de Waiters’ Ball, besides galavantin’ up an’ down Pearl Street after ten o’clock! I wouldn’t cared so much if you’d told me beforehand, but you said you didn’t come in town ’ceptin’ Thursday afternoon, an’ here I was believing yo’ lies.”

“It’s no lies! I haven’t been in town before.”

“Who brung you here at night anyhow﻿—an’ there ain’t no trains runnin’.”

“O, I came in with the cook and some of the boys, mama, that’s who! They hired an auto for the dance. What would be the use coming home, when you and Annjee go to bed before dark like chickens?”
===Chinese===
「上莫德尔家！……哼！还去了侍者舞会（Waiters' Ball），十点钟以后还在珀尔街上逛来逛去地招摇！你要是事先跟俺说一声，俺也不至于这么气，可你说你除了礼拜四下午根本不进城，俺还傻乎乎信了你的鬼话。」

「才不是鬼话！我以前没进过城。」

「那夜里到底是谁送你来的——又没有火车。」

「嗷，是跟厨娘还有几个小子一块儿来的，妈，就他们呗！他们包了辆汽车去跳舞。你和安吉天没黑就像老母鸡似的上床睡了，我回家干什么呀？」

===Original===
“That’s all right, madam! Annjee’s got sense﻿—savin’ her health an’ strength!”

Harriett was not impressed. “For what? To spend her life in Mrs. Rice’s kitchen?” She shrugged her shoulders.

“What you bring yo’ suitcase home fo’?”

“I’m quitting the job Saturday,” she said. “I’ve told them already.”
===Chinese===
「说得好，大小姐！安吉就懂事——知道爱惜自个儿的身子骨！」

哈丽雅特不吃这一套。「爱惜来干什么？好在赖斯太太家的厨房里熬一辈子？」她耸了耸肩膀。

「你把提箱带回家来干啥？」

「我礼拜六就不干了，」她说。「已经跟他们说了。」

===Original===
“Quitting!” her mother exclaimed. “What fo’? Lawd, if it ain’t one thing, it’s another!”

“What for?” Harriett retorted angrily. “There’s plenty what for! All that work for five dollars a week with what little tips those pikers give you. And white men insulting you besides, asking you to sleep with ’em. Look at my fingernails, all broke from scrubbing that dining-room floor.” She thrust out her dark slim hands. “Waiting table and cleaning silver, washing and ironing table-linen, and then scrubbing the floor besides﻿—that’s too much of a good thing! And only three waitresses on the job. That old steward out there’s a regular white folks’ nigger. He don’t care how hard he works us girls. Well, I’m through with the swell new Stanton County Country Club this coming Saturday﻿—I’m telling everybody!” She shrugged her shoulders again.

“What you gonna do then?”

“Maudel says I can get a job with her.”

“Maudel?﻿ ﻿… Where?” The old woman had begun to wring the clothes dry and pile them in a large dishpan.

“At the Banks Hotel, chambermaid, for pretty good pay.”
===Chinese===
「不干了！」她妈嚷起来。「为啥呀？主啊，这事刚消停，那事又来了！」

「为什么？」哈丽雅特气冲冲地顶回去。「原因多着呢！一礼拜干那么多活儿才五块钱，那帮小气鬼给的小费还少得可怜。白人男人还要欺负你，叫你陪他们睡觉。瞧瞧我的指甲，全刷饭厅地板刷劈了。」她伸出黝黑纤细的双手。「端盘子、擦银器、洗熨桌布，完了还得刷地板——这好事儿全让我一个人占全了！而且当班的拢共就三个女招待。外头那个老领班，十足一个白人跟前讨好的老黑奴才。他才不管把我们这些姑娘往死里使唤呢。哼，这礼拜六我就跟那气派的新斯坦顿县乡村俱乐部一刀两断——我见谁跟谁说！」她又耸了耸肩膀。

「那你打算干啥？」

「莫德尔说，我能跟她一起找个活儿干。」

「莫德尔？……在哪儿？」老太太已经开始把衣裳拧干，一堆一堆码进一只大洋铁盆里。

「在班克斯旅馆（Banks Hotel），做客房女侍，工钱还挺不错。」

===Original===
Hager stopped again and turned decisively towards her daughter. “You ain’t gonna work in no hotel. You hear me! They’s dives o’ sin, that’s what they is, an’ a child o’ mine ain’t goin’ in one. If you was a boy, I wouldn’t let you go, much less a girl! They ain’t nothin’ but strumpets works in hotels.”

“Maudel’s no strumpet.” Harriett’s eyes narrowed.

“I don’t know if she is or ain’t, but I knows I wants you to stop runnin’ with her﻿—I done tole you befo’.﻿ ﻿… Her mammy ain’t none too straight neither, raisin’ them chillen in sin. Look at Sammy in de reform school ’fore he were fifteen for gamblin’. An’ de oldest chile, Essie, done gone to Kansas City with that yaller devil she ain’t married. An’ Maudel runnin’ de streets night an’ day, with you tryin’ to keep up with her!﻿ ﻿… Lawd a mercy!﻿ ﻿… Here, hang up these clothes!”
===Chinese===
黑格又停下手，斩钉截铁地转身冲着女儿。「你可不许去什么旅馆干活。听见没有！那种地方全是罪恶的渊薮，就是这么回事，俺的娃儿一个也不许进去。你要是个小子，俺都不放你去，何况是个闺女！在旅馆里干活的，没一个是正经女人，全是娼妇。」

「莫德尔才不是娼妇。」哈丽雅特眯起了眼睛。

「她是也好不是也好，俺不晓得，可俺晓得俺要你别再跟她瞎混——俺早跟你说过了。……她那个当妈的也不怎么正经，在罪孽里养大那几个孩子。瞧瞧萨米（Sammy），不到十五岁就因为赌钱进了感化院。还有大闺女埃茜（Essie），跟那个没结婚的浅肤色魔鬼跑了堪萨斯城。莫德尔呢，没日没夜在街上野，你还偏要追着跟她比！……老天爷可怜见！……来，把这些衣裳晾上去！」

===Original===
Her mother pointed to the tin pan on the table filled with damp, twisted, white underwear. Harriett took the pan in both hands. It was heavy and she trembled with anger as she lifted it to her shoulders.

“You can bark at me if you want to, mama, but don’t talk about my friends. I don’t care what they are! Maudel’d do anything for me. And her brother’s a good kid, whether he’s been in reform school or not. They oughtn’t to put him there just for shooting dice. What’s that? I like him, and I like Mrs. Smothers, too. She’s not always scolding people for wanting a good time and for being lively and trying to be happy.”

Hot tears raced down each cheek, leaving moist lines in the pink powder. Sandy, playing marbles with Buster under the apple-tree, heard her sniffling as she shook out the clothes and hung them on the line in the yard.

“You, Sandy,” Aunt Hager called loudly from the kitchen-door. “Come in here an’ get me some water an’ cut mo’ firewood.” Her black face was wet with perspiration and drawn from fatigue and worry. “I got to get the rest o’ these clothes out yet this evenin’.﻿ ﻿… That Chile Harriett’s aggravatin’ me to death! Help me, Sandy, honey.”
===Chinese===
母亲指了指桌上那盆湿漉漉、绞成一团的白内衣。哈丽雅特双手端起盆。盆沉甸甸的，她把它扛上肩的时候，气得直发抖。

「你想冲我嚷嚷就嚷嚷吧，妈，可别说我朋友的坏话。我不管他们是什么人！莫德尔为我什么都肯干。她弟弟是个好孩子，进没进过感化院都一样。就因为掷骰子就把他关进去，就不该。那又怎么样？我就是喜欢他，我也喜欢斯莫瑟斯太太（Mrs. Smothers）。她就从来不骂人想痛快玩玩、想活得热闹点、想找点快活。」

热泪顺着两边的脸颊直往下淌，在粉红的香粉上冲出两道湿痕。桑迪正在苹果树下和巴斯特打弹子，听见她一边抖开衣裳往院里的绳上晾，一边抽抽搭搭地抹鼻子。

「桑迪，」黑格外婆在厨房门口大声喊。「进来给俺打点水，再劈些柴火。」她那张黑脸汗津津的，又累又愁，都走了形。「今儿傍晚俺还得把剩下的衣裳全晾出去。……哈丽雅特那丫头要把俺气死了！帮帮俺，桑迪宝贝。」

===Original===
They ate supper in silence, for Hager’s attempts at conversation with her young daughter were futile. Once the old woman said: “That onery Jimboy’s comin’ home Saturday,” and Harriett’s face brightened a moment.

“Gee, I’m glad,” she replied, and then her mouth went sullen again. Sandy began uncomfortably to kick the tableleg.

“For Christ’s sake!” The girl frowned, and the child stopped, hurt that his favorite aunt should yell at him peevishly for so slight an offense.

“Lawd knows, I wish you’d try an’ be mo’ like yo’ sisters, Annjee an’ Tempy,” Hager began as she washed the dishes, while Harriett stood near the stove, cloth in hand, waiting to dry them. “Here I is, an old woman, an’ you tries ma soul! After all I did to raise you, you don’t even hear me when I speak.” It was the old theme again, without variation. “Now, there’s Annjee, ain’t a better chile livin’﻿—if she warn’t crazy ’bout Jimboy. An’ Tempy married an’ doin’ well, an’ respected ever’where.﻿ ﻿… An’ you runnin’ wild!”
===Chinese===
晚饭吃得一声不响，黑格想跟小闺女搭话，全是白费劲。老太太有一句：「那个混小子吉姆博伊礼拜六要回来了，」哈丽雅特的脸一下亮了。

「哎呀，太好了，」她答道，随即嘴巴又撅了起来。桑迪心里不安，开始一下一下踢桌子腿。

「看在基督份上！」姑娘皱起眉头。孩子停下了，心里挺委屈——不过轻轻踢了两下桌子腿，他最喜欢的小姨竟为这么点儿小过错冲他嚷嚷。

「老天爷有眼，俺巴不得你学学你那两个姐姐，安吉和坦皮，」黑格一边洗盘子一边开了腔。哈丽雅特站在炉子边上，手里拿着抹布，等着擦干。「俺都这把老骨头了，你还净来磨俺的魂儿！俺含辛茹苦把你拉扯大，到头来俺说话你连听都不听。」又是那个老调子，一个字都不带变的。「就说安吉吧，天底下找不出比她更好的娃儿——要不是一门心思迷着吉姆博伊。坦皮呢，嫁了人，日子过得红火，走到哪儿都受人敬重。……可你呢，野得没边儿！」

===Original===
“Tempy?” Harriett sneered suddenly, pricked by this comparison. “So respectable you can’t touch her with a ten-foot pole, that’s Tempy!﻿ ﻿… Annjee’s all right, working herself to death at Mrs. Rice’s, but don’t tell me about Tempy. Just because she’s married a mail-clerk with a little property, she won’t even see her own family any more. When niggers get up in the world, they act just like white folks﻿—don’t pay you no mind. And Tempy’s that kind of a nigger﻿—she’s up in the world now!”

“Close yo’ mouth, talking that way ’bout yo’ own sister! I ain’t asked her to be always comin’ home, is I, if she’s satisfied in her own house?”

“No, you aren’t asking her, mama, but you’re always talking about her being so respectable.﻿ ﻿… Well, I don’t want to be respectable if I have to be stuck up and dicty like Tempy is.﻿ ﻿… She’s colored and I’m colored and I haven’t seen her since before Easter.﻿ ﻿… It’s not being black that matters with her, though, it’s being poor, and that’s what we are, you and me and Annjee, working for white folks and washing clothes and going in back doors, and taking tips and insults. I’m tired of it, mama! I want to have a good time once in a while.”

“That’s ’bout all you does have is a good time,” Hager said. “An’ it ain’t right, an’ it ain’t Christian, that’s what it ain’t! An’ de Lawd is takin’ notes on you!” The old woman picked up the heavy iron skillet and began to wash it inside and out.
===Chinese===
「坦皮？」这个比法戳着了哈丽雅特，她冷不丁嗤笑一声。「体面得十尺杆子都够不着边儿，这就是坦皮！……安吉是没得说，在赖斯太太家把自己往死里累，可别跟我提坦皮。不就嫁了个有点家底的邮局文书吗，连自家人都不认了。黑人一旦发了迹，做派就跟白人一个样——眼里再没有你。坦皮就是那种黑人——她如今可是抖起来啦！」

「闭上你的嘴，这么说自己亲姐姐！只要她在自个儿家里过得舒坦，俺又没求她老往娘家跑，是不是？」

「是啊，你是没求她回来，妈，可你张口闭口她多体面。……哼，要是体面就得像坦皮那样自抬身价、装腔作势，我才不要这种体面。……她是黑人，我也是黑人，可打复活节前到现在我就没见过她一面。……话又说回来，在她眼里要紧的不是黑不黑，是穷不穷，而咱们正是穷人，你、我，还有安吉，伺候白人、洗衣裳、从人家的后门进出，收小费，受窝囊气。我受够了，妈！我也想隔三差五痛快玩一玩。」

「你呀，也就剩痛快玩这一样了，」黑格说。「这可不对，这可不合教门，就是这么回事！主啊可都拿小本本记着你呢！」老太太拿起沉重的铁煎锅，里里外外刷起来。

===Original===
“Aw, the church has made a lot of you old Negroes act like Salvation Army people,” the girl returned, throwing the dried knives and forks on the table. “Afraid to even laugh on Sundays, afraid for a girl and boy to look at one another, or for people to go to dances. Your old Jesus is white, I guess, that’s why! He’s white and stiff and don’t like niggers!”

Hager gasped while Harriett went on excitedly, disregarding her mother’s pain: “Look at Tempy, the highest-class Christian in the family﻿—Episcopal, and so holy she can’t even visit her own mother. Seems like all the good-time people are bad, and all the old Uncle Toms and mean, dried-up, long-faced niggers fill the churches. I don’t never intend to join a church if I can help it.”

“Have mercy on this chile! Help her an’ save her from hellfire! Change her heart, Jesus!” the old woman begged, standing in the middle of the kitchen with uplifted arms. “God have mercy on ma daughter.”
===Chinese===
「嗐，教会把你们这些老黑人都弄得跟救世军（Salvation Army）似的，」姑娘回敬道，把擦干的刀叉往桌上一撂。「礼拜天连笑都不敢笑，姑娘小子互相看一眼都害怕，人家去跳个舞也不行。我看哪，你们那位老耶稣是白人，所以才会这样！他又白又死板，还不喜欢黑人！」

黑格听得倒抽一口气，哈丽雅特却全不顾母亲的痛楚，只管激动地说下去：「瞧瞧坦皮，全家档次最高的基督徒——圣公会（Episcopal）的，圣洁得连亲妈都不能去看一眼。照这么讲，凡是会享乐的都是坏人，满教堂倒坐满了老汤姆大叔（Uncle Tom）和刻薄干瘪、拉着长脸的黑人。只要我躲得开，这辈子就不打算进教会的门。」

「可怜可怜这孩子吧！帮帮她，救她躲开地狱之火！感化她的心吧，耶稣！」老太太举起双臂，站在厨房当间儿央告着。「上帝可怜可怜俺的闺女。」

===Original===
Harriett, her brow wrinkled in a steady frown, put the dishes away, wiped the table, and emptied the water with a splash through the kitchen-door. Then she went into the bedroom that she shared with her mother, and began to undress. Sandy saw, beneath her thin white underclothes, the soft black skin of her shapely young body.

“Where you goin’?” Hager asked sharply.

“Out,” said the girl.

“Out where?”

“O, to a barbecue at Willow Grove, mama! The boys are coming by in an auto at seven o’clock.”
===Chinese===
哈丽雅特的眉头一直拧着，皱个不停。她把盘子归置好，擦了桌子，端起脏水哗啦一声泼出厨房门外。然后她走进和母亲同住的那间卧室，动手换衣裳。透过薄薄的白内衣，桑迪看见她那身段匀称的年轻身体上，黑皮肤柔柔软软。

「你要上哪儿去？」黑格厉声问。

「出去，」姑娘说。

「出去上哪儿？」
