「嗷，去柳林（Willow Grove）吃烤肉野餐呗，妈！几个小子七点钟开汽车来接我。」

===Original===
“What boys?”

“Maudel’s brother and some fellows.”

“You ain’t goin’ a step!”

A pair of curling-irons swung in the chimney of the lighted lamp on the dresser. Harriett continued to get ready. She was making bangs over her forehead, and the scent of scorching hair-oil drifted by Sandy’s nose.
===Chinese===
「哪几个小子？」

「莫德尔的弟弟，还有几个相熟的。」

「你一步也不许出去！」

梳妆台上点着的油灯，玻璃罩口里别着一对烫发钳烤着。哈丽雅特只管梳妆打扮。她在额前卷刘海，烧焦的头油味儿从桑迪鼻子跟前飘过去。

===Original===
“Up half de night in town Monday, an’ de Lawd knows how late ever’ night in de country, an’ then you comes home to run out agin!﻿ ﻿… You ain’t goin’!” continued her mother.

Harriett was pulling on a pair of red silk stockings, bright and shimmering to her hips.

“You quit singin’ in de church choir. You say you ain’t goin’ back to school. You won’t keep no job! Now what is you gonna do? Yo’ pappy said years ago, ’fore he died, you was too purty to ’mount to anything, but I ain’t believed him. His last dyin’ words was: ‘Look out fo’ ma baby Harriett.’ You was his favourite chile.﻿ ﻿… Now look at you! Runnin’ de streets an’ wearin’ red silk stockings!” Hager trembled. “Spose yo’ pappy was to come back an’ see you?”
===Chinese===
「礼拜一在城里闹到半夜，在乡下呢，主啊才知道你夜夜熬到多晚，好不容易回了家，扭头又要往外跑！……你就是不许去！」她妈接着数落。

哈丽雅特正往腿上套一双红丝袜，鲜亮闪光，一直套到胯上。

「你不唱教堂诗班了。你说不回学校念书了。什么活儿你都干不长！如今你到底打算咋样？你爹好些年前还没过世的时候就说，你太俊了，成不了什么气候，俺偏不信他的话。他咽气前最后一句话是：『照顾好俺的宝贝哈丽雅特。』你是他最疼的娃儿。……瞧瞧你现在这样儿！满街野，还穿红丝袜！」黑格气得直哆嗦。「要是你爹回来，瞧见你这副模样，可怎么好？」

===Original===
Harriett powdered her face and neck, pink on ebony, dashed white talcum at each armpit, and rubbed her ears with perfume from a thin bottle. Then she slid a light-blue dress of many ruffles over her head. The skirt ended midway between the ankle and the knee, and she looked very cute, delicate, and straight, like a black porcelain doll in a Vienna toy shop.

“Some o’ Maudel’s makin’s, that dress﻿—anybody can tell,” her mother went on quarrelling. “Short an’ shameless as it can be! Regular bad gal’s dress, that’s what ’tis.﻿ ﻿… What you puttin’ it on fo’ anyhow, an’ I done told you you ain’t goin’ out? You must think I don’t mean ma words. Ain’t more’n sixteen last April an’ runnin’ to barbecues at Willer Grove! De idee! When I was yo’ age, wasn’t up after eight o’clock, ’ceptin’ Sundays in de church house, that’s all.﻿ ﻿… Lawd knows where you young ones is headin’. An’ me prayin’ an’ washin’ ma fingers to de bone to keep a roof over yo’ head.”

The sharp honk of an automobile horn sounded from the street. A big red car, full of laughing brown girls gaily dressed, and coatless, slick-headed black boys in green and yellow silk shirts, drew up at the curb. Somebody squeezed the bulb of the horn a second time and another loud and saucy *honk!* struck the ears.
===Chinese===
哈丽雅特往脸上和脖子上扑粉，粉红扑在乌黑上；两边腋下各扑了一层白爽身粉，又拿细瓶颈子里的香水抹了抹耳朵。然后她把一条层层叠叠荷叶边的浅蓝色裙子从头上套下去。裙摆不长不短，正吊在膝盖和脚踝当中，她显得又俏皮、又纤巧、又挺拔，活像维也纳玩具店里的黑瓷娃娃。

「那条裙子就是莫德尔的手艺——明眼人一看就知道，」她妈还在吵个不停。「短得没边儿，羞都不知道羞！正经是个坏丫头才穿的裙子，就是这个货色。……俺都告诉你不许出去了，你还套上它干啥？你当俺说话不算数。四月里才刚满十六岁，就跑去柳林吃烤肉！成何体统！俺像你这么大的时候，八点一过就睡了，顶多礼拜天在教堂里待着，如此而已。……天知道你们这些年轻人要往哪儿奔。俺又祷告又洗衣裳，十个指头都磨得露出骨头，就为给你头顶上挣片屋顶。」

街上传来汽车喇叭尖利的一声。一辆大红汽车靠上了路缘，车里坐满了笑闹的棕色皮肤姑娘，个个穿得花枝招展，还有一群不穿外衣、头发抹得溜光的黑人小子，穿着绿色黄色的绸衬衫。有人又捏了一下喇叭橡皮球，又一声又响又俏的*叭！*钻进耳朵。

===Original===
“You, Sandy,” Hager commanded. “Run out there an’ tell them niggers to leave here, ’cause Harriett ain’t goin’ no place.”

But Sandy did not move, because his young and slender aunt had gripped him firmly by the collar while she searched feverishly in the dresser-drawer for a scarf. She pulled it out, long and flame-colored, with fiery, silky fringe, before she released the little boy.

“You ain’t gwine a step this evenin’!” Hager shouted. “Don’t you hear me?”
===Chinese===
「桑迪，」黑格命令道。「跑出去叫那些老黑离开这儿，就说哈丽雅特哪儿也不去。」

可桑迪一动没动，因为他那年轻苗条的小姨一把攥住了他的领子，正火烧火燎地在梳妆台抽屉里翻一条围巾。她抽出一条长长的火红色围巾，缀着火焰似的丝穗子，这才放开小外甥。

「今儿傍晚你一步也不许出去！」黑格喊道。「你听见没有？」

===Original===
“O, no?” said Harriett coolly, in a tone that cut like knives. “You’re the one that says I’m not going﻿—*but I am!*”

Then suddenly something happened in the room﻿—the anger fell like a veil from Hager’s face, disclosing aged, helpless eyes full of fear and pain.

“Harriett, honey, I wants you to be good,” the old woman stammered. The words came pitiful and low﻿—not a command any longer﻿—as she faced her terribly alive young daughter in the ruffled blue dress and the red silk stockings. “I just wants you to grow up decent, chile. I don’t want you runnin’ to Willer Grove with them boys. It ain’t no place fo’ you in the nighttime﻿—an’ you knows it. You’s mammy’s baby girl. She wants you to be good, honey, and follow Jesus, that’s all.”
===Chinese===
「哦，是吗？」哈丽雅特冷冷地说，那声调像刀子一样扎人。「说我不去的是你——*可我就是要去！*」

接着，屋子里忽然起了变化——怒气像一层面纱从黑格脸上褪了下去，露出上了年纪的、无助的眼睛，盛满了恐惧和伤痛。

「哈丽雅特，宝贝，俺要你学好，」老太太结结巴巴地说。这些话说得又可怜又低声——不再是命令了——她面对着自己的女儿，那穿荷叶边蓝裙、红丝袜的闺女，鲜活泼辣得吓人。「俺就是想让你正正经经长大，娃儿。俺不想让你跟那帮小子跑去柳林。大半夜的那不是你去的地方——你自个儿也晓得。你是妈的宝贝闺女。妈要你学好，宝贝，跟从耶稣，就这样。」

===Original===
The baritone giggling of the boys in the auto came across the yard as Hager started to put a timid, restraining hand on her daughter’s shoulder﻿—but Harriett backed away.

“You old fool!” she cried. “Lemme go! You old Christian fool!”

She ran through the door and across the sidewalk to the waiting car, where the arms of the young men welcomed her eagerly. The big machine sped swiftly down the street and the rapid *sput! sput! sput!* of its engine grew fainter and fainter. Finally, the auto was only a red taillight in the summer dusk. Sandy, standing beside his grandmother in the doorway, watched it until it disappeared.
===Chinese===
汽车里小子们低沉的笑闹声穿过院子传过来，黑格怯生生地伸出一只手，想按住女儿的肩膀拦住她——哈丽雅特却直往后退。

「你这老糊涂！」她嚷道。「放开我！你这老基督徒糊涂蛋！」

她跑出门，穿过人行道，奔向等在路边的汽车，小伙子们的胳膊急切切地迎上来接她。大汽车顺街飞驰而去，引擎急促的*噗！噗！噗！*声越来越远，越来越轻。到后来，那辆汽车只剩夏日暮色里一粒红色尾灯。桑迪站在门口外婆身边，望着它，直到它消失不见。

## V Guitar / 五 吉他

===Original===
> Throw yo’ arms around me, baby.
>
> Like de circle round de sun!
>
> Baby, throw yo’ arms around me
>
> Like de circle round de sun,
>
> An’ tell yo’ pretty papa
>
> How you want yo’ lovin’ done!

Jimboy was home. All the neighborhood could hear his rich low baritone voice giving birth to the blues. On Saturday night he and Annjee went to bed early. On Sunday night Aunt Hager said: “Put that guitar right up, less’n it’s hymns you plans on playin’. An’ I don’t want too much o’ them, ’larmin’ de white neighbors.”

But this was Monday, and the sun had scarcely fallen below the horizon before the music had begun to float down the alley, over back fences and into kitchen-windows where nice white ladies sedately washed their supper dishes.

===Chinese===
> 张开胳膊搂住俺吧，宝贝，
>
> 像绕着太阳的那个圈圈！
>
> 宝贝，张开胳膊搂住俺，
>
> 像绕着太阳的那个圈圈，
>
> 再告诉你俊俏的爹爹，
>
> 你的情爱要怎么办！

吉姆博伊到家了。街坊四邻都听得见他那一副浑厚低沉的男中音，正把一支支布鲁斯唱得呱呱坠地。礼拜六晚上，他和安吉早早上了床。礼拜天晚上，黑格外婆发了话：“快把那吉他收起来，除非你打算弹的是赞美诗。就是赞美诗俺也不想多听，别惊动了白人邻居。”

可这天是礼拜一，太阳才刚刚沉到地平线下，乐声就已经顺着小巷飘开了，飘过后院的栅栏，飘进一间间厨房的窗户——窗户里头，体面的白人太太们正端端正正地洗着晚饭的碗碟。

===Original===
> Did you ever see peaches
>
> Growin’ on a watermelon vine?
>
> Says did you ever see peaches
>
> On a watermelon vine?
>
> Did you ever see a woman
>
> That I couldn’t get for mine?

Long, lazy length resting on the kitchen-door-sill, back against the jamb, feet in the yard, fingers picking his sweet guitar, left hand holding against its fingerboard the back of an old pocketknife, sliding the knife upward, downward, getting thus weird croons and sighs from the vibrating strings:

> O, I left ma mother
>
> An’ I cert’ly can leave you.
>
> Indeed I left ma mother
>
> An’ I cert’ly can leave you,
>
> For I’d leave any woman
>
> That mistreats me like you do.

Jimboy, remembering brown-skin mamas in Natchez, Shreveport, Dallas; remembering Creole women in Baton Rouge, Louisiana:

===Chinese===
> 你可曾见过桃子
>
> 长在西瓜藤上？
>
> 俺问你可曾见过桃子
>
> 长在西瓜藤上？
>
> 你可曾见过有哪个女人，
>
> 俺要不到手做俺的姑娘？

他长长的身子懒洋洋地摊在厨房门槛上，背抵着门框，两条腿伸在院子里，手指拨弄着那把音色甜润的吉他；左手把一把旧折刀的刀背抵在指板上，刀子往上一滑、往下一滑，就这样从颤动的琴弦上逗出一声声古怪的浅吟低叹：

> 噢，俺娘俺都撇得下，
>
> 自然也撇得下你。
>
> 真个的，俺娘俺都撇得下，
>
> 自然也撇得下你，
>
> 哪个女人像你这么作践俺，
>
> 俺就照样撇下哪个去。

吉姆博伊一边弹，一边想着纳奇兹（Natchez）、什里夫波特（Shreveport）、达拉斯（Dallas）那些棕色皮肤的相好；想着路易斯安那州巴吞鲁日（Baton Rouge）的克里奥尔女人们：

===Original===
> O, yo’ windin’ an’ yo’ grindin’
>
> Don’t have no effect on me,
>
> Babe, yo’ windin’ an’ yo’ grindin’
>
> Don’t have no ’fect on me,
>
> ’Cause I can wind an’ grind
>
> Like a monkey round a coconut-tree!

Then Harriett, standing under the ripening apple-tree, in the backyard, chiming in:

> Now I see that you don’t want me,
>
> So it’s fare thee, fare thee well!
>
> Lawd, I see that you don’t want me,
>
> So it’s fare—thee—well!
>
> I can still get plenty lovin’.
>
> An’ you can go to—Kansas City!

“O, play it, sweet daddy Jimboy!” She began to dance.

===Chinese===
> 噢，你那扭呀摆呀，
>
> 对俺一点儿不管用，
>
> 宝贝，你那扭呀摆呀，
>
> 对俺一点儿不管用，
>
> 因为俺也会扭也会摆，
>
> 赛过绕着椰子树转的猴儿精！

这时哈丽雅特在后院那棵快熟的苹果树底下接了腔：

> 这会儿俺看明白了，你不要俺，
>
> 那就别了，永别了吧！
>
> 主啊，俺看明白了，你不要俺，
>
> 那就别——了——吧！
>
> 俺照样有人疼来有人爱。
>
> 你就滚去——堪萨斯城吧！

“噢，弹呀，甜爹爹吉姆博伊！”她跳起舞来了。

===Original===
Then Hager, from her seat on the edge of the platform covering the well, broke out: “Here, madam! Stop that prancin’! Bad enough to have all this singin’ without turnin’ de yard into a show-house.” But Harriett kept on, her hands picking imaginary cherries out of the stars, her hips speaking an earthly language quite their own.

“You got it, kid,” said Jimboy, stopping suddenly, then fingering his instrument for another tune. “You do it like the stage women does. You’ll be takin’ Ada Walker’s place if you keep on.”

“Wha! Wha! … You chillen sho can sing!” Tom Johnson shouted his compliments from across the yard. And Sarah, beside him on the bench behind their shack, added: “Minds me o’ de ole plantation times, honey! It sho do!”

“Unhuh! Bound straight fo’ de devil, that’s what they is,” Hager returned calmly from her place beside the pump. “You an’ Harriett both—singin’ an’ dancin’ this stuff befo’ these chillens here.” She pointed to Sandy and Willie-Mae, who sat on the ground with their backs against the chicken-box. “It’s a shame!”

===Chinese===
这时黑格从她坐着的井台木盖边儿上开了腔：“嘿，大小姐！别那么蹦跶了！光是这么唱就已经够呛了，别再把院子变成戏园子。”可哈丽雅特照跳不误，两只手直往星星里摘那不存在的樱桃，一对胯骨说着一种全然只属于人间的语言。

“你就是这块料，丫头，”吉姆博伊说着猛地收住琴，手指又搭上去，另起一支曲子。“你跳起来跟台上那些女人一个做派。再这么下去，你就要把阿达·沃克（Ada Walker）的位置顶下来喽。”

“哇！哇！……你们这帮娃儿可真会唱！”汤姆·约翰逊隔着院子大声夸道。萨拉挨着他坐在自家窝棚后头的长凳上，补了一句：“叫俺想起老种植园那会儿了，宝贝儿！真真的！”

“嗯哼！一准儿直奔魔鬼去，这俩就是这么号人，”黑格在水泵旁边的老地方不紧不慢地回话。“你跟哈丽雅特俩都是——当着这些娃儿的面，又唱又跳这些玩意儿。”她指了指桑迪和薇莉-梅，两个孩子背靠着鸡笼坐在地上。“造孽哟！”

===Original===
“I likes it,” said Willie-Mae.

“Me too,” the little boy agreed.

“Naturally you would—none o’ you-all’s converted yet,” countered the old woman to the children as she settled back against the pump to listen to some more.

The music rose hoarse and wild:

> I wonder where ma easy rider’s gone?
>
> He done left me, put ma new gold watch in pawn.

===Chinese===
“我就爱听，”薇莉-梅说。

“我也是，”小家伙随声附和。

“你们爱听是自然——你们这帮娃儿还没一个皈依了的，”老太太冲孩子们顶了一句，重新靠回水泵上，还要往下多听几段。

乐声扬起来，沙哑而狂野：

> 俺纳闷，俺那潇洒情郎上哪儿去了？
>
> 他把俺甩了，还把俺的新金表押进了当铺。

===Original===
It was Harriett’s voice in plaintive moan to the night sky. Jimboy had taught her that song, but a slight, clay-colored brown boy who had hopped bells at the Clinton Hotel for a couple of months, on his way from Houston to Omaha, discovered its meaning to her. Puppy-love, maybe, but it had hurt when he went away, saying nothing. And the guitar in Jimboy’s hands echoed that old pain with an even greater throb than the original ache itself possessed.

Approaching footsteps came from the front yard.

“Lord, I can hear you-all two blocks away!” said Annjee, coming around the house, home from work, with a bundle of food under her left arm. “Hello! How are you, daddy? Hello, ma! Gimme a kiss Sandy. … Lord, I’m hot and tired and most played out. This late just getting from work! … Here, Jimboy, come on in and eat some of these nice things the white folks had for supper.” She stepped across her husband’s outstretched legs into the kitchen. “I brought a mighty good piece of cold ham for you, hon’, from Mis’ Rice’s.”

“All right, sure, I’ll be there in a minute,” the man said, but he went on playing “Easy Rider,” and Harriett went on singing, while the food was forgotten on the table until long after Annjee had come outdoors again and sat down in the cool, tired of waiting for Jimboy to come in to her.

===Chinese===
那是哈丽雅特的嗓子，正对着夜空哀哀地呻吟。这支歌是吉姆博伊教她的，可把歌里的意思给她点破的，却是一个瘦小、土黄色皮肤的棕色男孩——他从休斯敦（Houston）去奥马哈，半道上在克林顿酒店（Clinton Hotel）当了两个月门童。兴许只是小儿女的初恋，可他一声不吭地走掉的时候，她还是伤了心。而吉姆博伊手里这把吉他把那份旧痛应和出来，一阵一阵的悸动，竟比当初那疼本身还要来得凶。

前院传来越走越近的脚步声。

“天哪，隔着两个街区我就听见你们啦！”安吉绕过屋角走过来，下班到家了，左胳膊底下夹着一包吃食。“你们好啊！孩子他爹，你好吗？妈，你好！桑迪，亲我一个。……天哪，我又热又累，都快散架了。这么晚才下得了班！……喂，吉姆博伊，快进来，白人他们晚饭吃的这些好东西，你来吃点儿。”她跨过丈夫伸直的两条腿，走进厨房。“我给你带了块顶好的冷火腿，亲爱的，赖斯太太家的。”

“好嘞，放心，这就来，”男人应着，手底下却接着弹《潇洒情郎》（Easy Rider），哈丽雅特也接着唱。那包吃食就忘在了桌上——直到安吉又从屋里出来，坐进外头的凉快地里，等吉姆博伊进屋等得都没了脾气，那吃食还一直搁在那儿。

===Original===
Off and on for nine years, ever since he had married Annjee, Jimboy and Harriett had been singing together in the evenings. When they started, Harriett was a little girl with braided hair, and each time that her roving brother-in-law stopped in Stanton, he would amuse himself by teaching her the old Southern songs, the popular ragtime ditties, and the hundreds of varying verses of the blues that he would pick up in the big dirty cities of the South. The child, with her strong sweet voice (colored folks called it alto) and her racial sense of rhythm, soon learned to sing the songs as well as Jimboy. He taught her the *parse me la*, too, and a few other movements peculiar to Southern Negro dancing, and sometimes together they went through the buck and wing and a few taps. It was all great fun, and innocent fun except when one stopped to think, as white folks did, that some of the blues lines had, not only double, but triple meanings, and some of the dance steps required very definite movements of the hips. But neither Harriett nor Jimboy soiled their minds by thinking. It was music, good exercise—and they loved it.

“Do you know this one, Annjee?” asked Jimboy, calling his wife’s name out of sudden politeness because he had forgotten to eat her food, had hardly looked at her, in fact, since she came home. Now he glanced towards her in the darkness where she sat plump on a kitchen-chair in the yard, apart from the others, with her back to the growing corn in the garden. Softly he ran his fingers, light as a breeze, over his guitar strings, imitating the wind rustling through the long leaves of the corn. A rectangle of light from the kitchen-door fell into the yard striking sidewise across the healthy orange-yellow of his skin above the unbuttoned neck of his blue laborer’s shirt.

“Come on, sing it with us, Annjee,” he said.

“I don’t know it,” Annjee replied, with a lump in her throat, and her eyes on the silhouette of his long, muscular, animal-hard body. She loved Jimboy too much, that’s what was the matter with her! She knew there was nothing between him and her young sister except the love of music, yet he might have dropped the guitar and left Harriett in the yard for a little while to come eat the nice cold slice of ham she had brought him. She hadn’t seen him all day long. When she went to work this morning, he was still in bed—and now the blues claimed him.

===Chinese===
打从娶了安吉起，断断续续九年了，一到晚上吉姆博伊和哈丽雅特就这么一块儿唱歌。刚起头那会儿，哈丽雅特还是个梳着辫子的小丫头，每回她那好流浪的姐夫在斯坦顿落脚，总要拿教她唱歌当乐子：南方的老歌，时兴的拉格泰姆小调，还有他在南方那些又大又脏的城市里东零西碎拾来的成百上千种布鲁斯词儿。这孩子嗓子又亮又甜（黑人们管那叫女低音），又天生一副黑人特有的节奏感，没多久就唱得跟吉姆博伊一样好了。他还教了她帕斯马拉（parse me la），外加几种南方黑人舞蹈独有的步子，有时候爷儿俩一块儿走一遍巴克翼步舞，再加几步踢踏。这都是顶好玩的事儿，也是干干净净的乐子——除非有谁停下来细想，像白人那样，寻思有些布鲁斯唱词不但一语双关、简直一语三关，有些舞步还非得把胯骨明明白白地摆动起来。可哈丽雅特和吉姆博伊谁也不肯拿这种细想弄脏自己的脑子。这是音乐，是好锻炼——他们爱它。

“这支你晓得吧，安吉？”吉姆博伊问。他忽然客气起来，喊了妻子的名字——因为她带回来的吃食他忘了吃，说实在的，打她进家门起他就几乎没正眼瞧过她。这时他才朝黑暗里的她望过去：她独自坐在院里一把厨房椅上，离旁人远远的，背对着园子里一天天长高的玉米，丰满的身子安安稳稳坐成一团。他的手指轻得像一阵微风，柔柔地拂过琴弦，学那风穿过玉米长叶子时的沙沙声。厨房门口一方灯光斜斜投进院子，正落在他蓝色工装衬衫敞开的领口上方，那一片健康的橙黄色皮肤上。

“来嘛，跟我们一块儿唱，安吉，”他说。

“我不晓得这支，”安吉应道，喉咙口堵着一团东西，眼睛望着他那修长、筋肉虬结、野兽般结实的身子的侧影。她的毛病就出在太爱吉姆博伊了！她晓得，他跟自己这个小妹妹之间，除了对音乐的爱好什么也没有；可他原是可以放下吉他，把哈丽雅特撂在院子里一小会儿，过来把她带来的那片喷喷香的冷火腿吃掉的呀。她一整天没见着他了。今早她去上工的时候，他还在床上——这会儿，布鲁斯把他整个儿占去了。

===Original===
In the starry blackness the singing notes of the guitar became a plaintive hum, like a breeze in a grove of palmettos; became a low moan, like the wind in a forest of live-oaks strung with long strands of hanging moss. The voice of Annjee’s golden, handsome husband on the doorstep rang high and far away, lonely-like, crying with only the guitar, not his wife, to understand; crying grotesquely, crying absurdly in the summer night:

> I got a mule to ride.
>
> I got a mule to ride.
>
> Down in the South somewhere
>
> I got a mule to ride.

Then asking the question as an anxious, left-lonesome girl-sweetheart would ask it:

> You say you goin’ North.
>
> You say you goin’ North.
>
> How ’bout yo’ … lovin’ gal?
>
> You say you goin’ North.

===Chinese===
星光点点的黑夜里，吉他的歌音变成一阵哀婉的嗡鸣，像微风拂过一片矮棕榈林；又变成一声低低的呜咽，像风穿过挂满一绺绺长垂苔的常绿栎树林。安吉那肤色金黄、相貌英俊的丈夫坐在门槛上，歌声高亢邈远，孤零零的，哭腔一般——却只有吉他懂得他，而不是他的妻子；在这个夏夜里哭得怪诞，哭得荒唐：

> 俺有头骡子好骑。
>
> 俺有头骡子好骑。
>
> 在南边什么鬼地方，
>
> 俺有头骡子好骑。

接着又发问，像一个心里发慌、被撇下不管的姑娘相好会问的那样：

> 你说你要去北方。
>
> 你说你要去北方。
>
> 那你的……亲亲姑娘怎么办？
>
> 你说你要去北方。

===Original===
Then sighing in rhythmical despair:

> O, don’t you leave me here.
>
> Babe, don’t you leave me here.
>
> Dog-gone yo’ comin’ back!
>
> Said don’t you leave me here.

On and on the song complained, man-verses and woman-verses, to the evening air in stanzas that Jimboy had heard in the pinewoods of Arkansas from the lumbercamp workers; in other stanzas that were desperate and dirty like the weary roads where they were sung; and in still others that the singer created spontaneously in his own mouth then and there:

> O, I done made ma bed,
>
> Says I done made ma bed.
>
> Down in some lonesome grave
>
> I done made ma bed.

===Chinese===
接着又合着节拍，一声声绝望地叹息：

> 噢，别把俺扔在这儿。
>
> 宝贝，别把俺扔在这儿。
>
> 去你的“我还会回来”！
>
> 俺说别把俺扔在这儿。

那歌儿一声接一声地诉着苦，男人的词、女人的词，对着傍晚的空气唱下去：有的段子，是吉姆博伊在阿肯色（Arkansas）的松林里听伐木营的工人们唱过的；有的段子又绝望又腌臜，跟它们被唱出来的那些累人的道路一个样；还有的段子，是唱歌的人当场顺嘴自己造出来的：

> 噢，俺铺好了俺的床，
>
> 俺说俺铺好了俺的床。
>
> 在哪座冷冷清清的坟里头，
>
> 俺铺好了俺的床。

===Original===
It closed with a sad eerie twang.

“That’s right decent,” said Hager. “Now I wish you-all’d play some o’ ma pieces like ‘When de Saints Come Marchin’ In’ or ‘This World Is Not Ma Home’—something Christian from de church.”

“Aw, mama, it’s not Sunday yet,” said Harriett.

“Sing ‘Casey Jones,’” called old man Tom Johnson. “That’s ma song.”

So the ballad of the immortal engineer with another mama in the Promised Land rang out promptly in the starry darkness, while everybody joined in the choruses.

“Aw, pick it, boy,” yelled the old man. “Can’t nobody play like you.”

===Chinese===
结尾是一声凄切怪诞的弦响。

“这还像句正经话，”黑格说。“这会儿俺想叫你们弹几支俺的曲子，像《当圣徒们列队进场》（When de Saints Come Marchin’ In）啦，《这世界不是俺的家》（This World Is Not Ma Home）啦——来点儿教会里的基督玩意儿。”

“哎呀，妈，还没到礼拜天呢，”哈丽雅特说。

“唱《凯西·琼斯》（Casey Jones）吧，”汤姆·约翰逊老头儿喊道。“那是俺的歌。”

于是，那支唱不朽火车司机的歌谣——他上了应许之地，那里另有一位妈妈——当场在星光点点的黑夜里响亮唱开，人人都跟着进了副歌。

“嘿，弹呀，小子，”老头儿嚷嚷。“没人能弹得像你这样。”

===Original===
And Jimboy remembered when he was a lad in Memphis that W. C. Handy had said: “You ought to make your living out of that, son.” But he hadn’t followed it up—too many things to see, too many places to go, too many other jobs.

“What song do you like, Annjee?” he asked, remembering her presence again.

“O, I don’t care. Any ones you like. All of ’em are pretty.” She was pleased and petulant and a little startled that he had asked her.

“All right, then,” he said. “Listen to me:”

> Here I is in de mean ole jail.
>
> Ain’t got nobody to go ma bail.
>
> Lonesome an’ sad an’ chain gang bound—
>
> Ever’ friend I had’s done turned me down.

===Chinese===
吉姆博伊想起自己还是孟菲斯（Memphis）一个半大孩子的时候，W·C·汉迪（W. C. Handy）跟他说过：“你该靠这个吃饭才是，孩子。”可他没往这上头走——要看的世面太多，要去的地方太多，要干的别的活计也太多。

“你喜欢哪支歌，安吉？”他问，这才又记起她还在这儿。

“噢，我随便。你喜欢的都行。哪支都好听。”被他这一问，她又欢喜，又使小性子，又有点儿受宠若惊。

“那好，”他说。“听我唱：”

> 俺蹲在这歹毒的老牢房。
>
> 没有一个人来替俺保释。
>
> 孤零零、惨兮兮，就要锁上铁链去服苦役——
>
> 俺认得的每一个朋友，到头来全把俺抛弃。

===Original===
“That’s sho it!” shouted Tom Johnson in great sympathy. “Now, when I was in de Turner County Jail …”

“Shut up yo’ mouth!” squelched Sarah, jabbing her husband in the ribs.

The songs went on, blues, shouts, jingles, old hits: “Bon Bon Buddy, the Chocolate Drop”; “Wrap Me in Your Big Red Shawl”; “Under the Old Apple Tree”; “Turkey in the Straw”—Jimboy and Harriett breaking the silence of the small-town summer night until Aunt Hager interrupted:

“You-all better wind up, chillens, ’cause I wants to go to bed. I ain’t used to stayin’ ’wake so late, nohow. Play something kinder decent there, son, fo’ you stops.”

Jimboy, to tease the old woman, began to rock and moan like an elder in the Sanctified Church, patting both feet at the same time as he played a hymn-like, lugubrious tune with a dancing overtone:

===Chinese===
“可不是这话嘛！”汤姆·约翰逊大声附和，满脸的同情。“想当年俺蹲特纳县监狱（Turner County Jail）那会儿……”

“闭上你的嘴！”萨拉戳着丈夫的肋巴骨，把他这话头掐了。

歌一支接一支唱下去，布鲁斯、喊灵调、顺口溜小曲、当年红过的老歌：《邦邦伙伴，巧克力糖豆》（Bon Bon Buddy, the Chocolate Drop）啦，《用你的大红披肩裹住我》（Wrap Me in Your Big Red Shawl）啦，《老苹果树下》（Under the Old Apple Tree）啦，《稻草里的火鸡》（Turkey in the Straw）啦——吉姆博伊和哈丽雅特把小镇夏夜的寂静一路唱破，直到黑格外婆出来打断：

“你们这帮娃儿最好收场了，俺要上床睡觉喽。熬这么晚不睡，俺横竖不惯。停以前，儿子，再弹个体面点儿的吧。”

吉姆博伊存心逗老太太，学起圣洁会（Sanctified Church）里长老的做派，摇晃着身子哼哼起来，两只脚还一齐拍着地，手上弹一支赞美诗似的曲子，凄凄惨惨，底下又垫着一股跳舞的劲儿：

===Original===
> Tell me, sister,
>
> Tell me, brother,
>
> Have you heard de latest news?

Then seriously as if he were about to announce the coming of the Judgment:

> A woman down in Georgia
>
> Got her two sweet-men confused.

How terrible! How sad! moaned the guitar.

> One knocked on de front do’,
>
> One knocked on de back—

Sad, sad… sad, sad! said the music.

===Chinese===
> 告诉俺，姊妹，
>
> 告诉俺，弟兄，
>
> 最新消息你听说了没有？

接着他一本正经，仿佛马上就要宣告末日审判降临：

> 佐治亚州有个女人，
>
> 把她的两个情郎认混了。

多吓人哪！多伤心哪！吉他呜咽着说。

> 一个敲了前门，
>
> 一个敲了后门——

伤心啊，伤心……伤心啊，伤心！音乐说。

===Original===
> Now that woman down in Georgia’s
>
> Door-knob is hung with black.

O, play that funeral march, boy! while the guitar laughed a dirge.

> An’ de hearse is comin’ easy
>
> With two rubber-tired hacks!

Followed by a long-out, churchlike:

> Amen…!

===Chinese===
> 如今佐治亚那个女人家的
>
> 门把手挂上了黑纱。

噢，奏起那葬礼进行曲吧，小子！——吉他笑着唱了一支挽歌。

> 灵车正慢悠悠地驶来，
>
> 后头跟着两辆胶轮马车！

随后是一声拖得老长的、教堂腔的：

> 阿们……！

===Original===
Then with rapid glides, groans, and shouts the instrument screamed of a sudden in profane frenzy, and Harriett began to ball-the-jack, her arms flopping like the wings of a headless pigeon, the guitar string whining in ecstasy, the player rocking gaily to the urgent music, his happy mouth crying: “Tack ’em on down, gal! Tack ’em on down, Harrie!”

But Annjee had risen.

“I wish you’d come in and eat the ham I brought you,” she said as she picked up her chair and started towards the house. “And you, Sandy! Get up from under that tree and go to bed.” She spoke roughly to the little fellow, whom the songs had set a-dreaming. Then to her husband: “Jimboy, I wish you’d come in.”

===Chinese===
突然，一阵急速的滑弦、呻吟和呼喊里，那乐器带着渎神的狂热尖叫起来，哈丽雅特撒开了欢儿狂舞（ball-the-jack），两条胳膊扑腾扑腾，像没头的鸽子扇翅膀；琴弦兴奋得直打唿哨，弹琴的人合着这急切的音乐快活地摇撼，一张快活的嘴喊着：“踩实喽，丫头！踩实喽，哈丽！”

可安吉已经站起身了。

“我盼着你进屋吃我给你带的火腿呢，”她一边说，一边端起椅子朝屋里走。“还有你，桑迪！从树底下起来，睡觉去。”她对小家伙说话的口气很冲——那孩子叫这些歌听得出了神，正做着自己的梦呢。然后又对她丈夫说：“吉姆博伊，我想你进屋来。”

===Original===
The man stopped playing, with a deep vibration of the strings that seemed to echo through the whole world. Then he leaned his guitar against the side of the house and lifted straight up in his hairy arms Annjee’s plump, brown-black little body while he kissed her as she wriggled like a stubborn child, her soft breasts rubbing his hard body through the coarse blue shirt.

“You don’t like my old songs, do you, baby? You don’t want to hear me sing ’em,” he said, laughing. “Well, that’s all right. I like you, anyhow, and I like your ham, and I like your kisses, and I like everything you bring me. Let’s go in and chow down.” And he carried her into the kitchen, where he sat with her on his knees as he ate the food she so faithfully had brought him from Mrs. J. J. Rice’s dinner-table.

Outside, Willie-Mae went running home through the dark. And Harriett pumped a cool drink of water for her mother, then helped her to rise from her low seat, Sandy aiding from behind, with both hands pushing firmly in Aunt Hager’s fleshy back. Then the three of them came into the house and glanced, as they passed through the kitchen, at Annjee sitting on Jimboy’s lap with both dark arms tight around his neck.

“Looks like you’re clinging to the Rock of Ages,” said Harriett to her sister. “Be sure you don’t slip, old evil gal!”

But at midnight, when the owl that nested in a tree near the corner began to hoot, they were all asleep—Annjee and Jimboy in one room, Harriett and Hager in another, with Sandy on the floor at the foot of his grandmother’s bed. Far away on the railroad line a whistle blew, lonesome and long.

===Chinese===
男人停了手，琴弦一声深沉的震颤，仿佛震彻了整个世界。随后他把吉他靠在屋墙边，两条毛茸茸的胳膊一抄，把安吉那丰满的棕黑色小身子直直地抱了起来，一面亲她；她像个犟嘴的孩子似的在他怀里扭动，柔软的胸脯隔着那件粗蓝布衬衫，蹭着他硬邦邦的身子。

“你不喜欢我这些老歌，是吧，宝贝？你不想听我唱它们，”他笑着说。“嗐，没关系。反正我喜欢你，喜欢你的火腿，喜欢你的亲亲，喜欢你带给我的每一样东西。走，进屋吃去。”他把她抱进厨房，让她坐在自己膝盖上，吃着她一片忠心从赖斯太太家饭桌上给他带回来的吃食。

外头，薇莉-梅摸着黑一路跑回家去了。哈丽雅特压上一瓢凉水端给母亲喝，又搀她从矮座上起身，桑迪在后头帮衬，两只手结结实实推着黑格外婆那肉乎乎的后背。娘儿仨进了屋，穿过厨房时，一眼瞧见安吉坐在吉姆博伊腿上，两条黑黢黢的胳膊紧紧搂着他的脖子。

“瞧你那样儿，倒像抱着万古磐石（Rock of Ages）似的，”哈丽雅特对她姐姐说。“可千万抱稳了别滑喽，你个小妖精！”

可到了半夜，拐角旁边树上做窝的那只猫头鹰叫起来的时候，他们全都睡着了——安吉和吉姆博伊在一间屋，哈丽雅特和黑格在另一间屋，桑迪睡在外婆床脚边的地板上。老远的铁路线上，汽笛响了一声，孤零零的，长长的。

## VI Work / 六 干活

===Original===

The sunflowers in Willie-Mae’s backyard were taller than Tom Johnson’s head, and the hollyhocks in the fence corners were almost as high. The nasturtiums, blood-orange and gold, tumbled over themselves all around Madam de Carter’s house. Aunt Hager’s sweet-william, her pinks, and her tiger-lilies were abloom and the apples on her single tree would soon be ripe. The adjoining yards of the three neighbors were gay with flowers. “Watch out for them dogs!” his grandmother told Sandy hourly, for the days had come when the bright heat made gentle animals go mad. Bees were heavy with honey, great green flies hummed through the air, yellow-black butterflies suckled at the rambling roses﻿ ﻿… and watermelons were on the market.

The Royal African Knights and Ladies of King Solomon’s Scepter were preparing a drill for the September Emancipation celebration, a “Drill of All Nations,” in which Annjee was to represent Sweden. It was not to be given for a month or more, but the first rehearsal would take place tonight.

“Sandy,” his mother said, shaking him early in the morning as he lay on his pallet at the foot of Aunt Hager’s bed, “listen here! I want you to come out to Mis’ Rice’s this evening and help me get through the dishes so’s I can start home early, in time to wash and dress myself to go to the lodge hall. You hears me?”

“Yes’m,” said Sandy, keeping his eyes closed to the bright stream of morning sunlight entering the window. But half an hour later, when Jimboy kicked him and said: “Hey, bo! You wanta go fishin’?” he got up at once, slid into his pants; and together they went out in the garden to dig worms. It was seldom that his father took him anywhere, and, of course, he wanted to go. Sandy adored Jimboy, but Jimboy, amiable and indulgent though he was, did not often care to be bothered with his ten-year-old son on his fishing expeditions.

===Chinese===

薇莉-梅家后院的向日葵长得比汤姆·约翰逊的脑袋还高，篱笆角落里的蜀葵也几乎一般高。血橙色和金黄色的旱金莲，绕着德·卡特夫人的房子爬得满处都是。黑格外婆的石竹梅、石竹花和虎皮百合开得正盛，她家那棵独苗苹果树上的果子也快熟了。三家邻居连着的几方院子都开得热热闹闹。「当心那些狗！」外婆隔不了一会儿就叮嘱桑迪一遍，因为那种亮晃晃的大热天来了，连温驯的动物都会热得发疯。蜜蜂饱蘸着蜜，大绿蝇在空中嗡嗡，黄黑色的蝴蝶伏在蔓生蔷薇上吸吮花蜜……西瓜上市了。

皇家非洲骑士与所罗门王权杖女士会正为九月的解放纪念日庆典排一场操演，叫「万国操演（Drill of All Nations）」，安吉要在里头代表瑞典。演出还得一个多月才轮得上，可头一回排练就在今晚。

「桑迪，」一大早，母亲摇着他说。孩子睡在黑格外婆床脚的地铺上。「你听着！今儿傍晚我要你上赖斯太太家去，帮我赶完那些碗碟，好叫我早点动身回家，来得及洗一洗、换上身衣裳，上社团会所去。你听见没有？」

「听见了，妈，」桑迪说，眼睛还闭着，躲着从窗口流进来的那道明亮的晨光。可半个钟头之后，吉姆博伊踢了踢他说：「嘿，小子！想去钓鱼不？」他立刻爬起来，套上裤子；爷儿俩便一道到园子里挖蚯蚓去了。父亲难得带他上什么地方，他自然是想去的。桑迪崇拜吉姆博伊，可吉姆博伊虽然和气、由着孩子，出门钓鱼时却不大情愿让十岁的儿子跟着添乱。

===Original===

Harriett had gone to her job, and Hager had long been at the tubs under the apple-tree when the two males emerged from the kitchen-door. “Huh! You ain’t workin’ this mawnin’, is you?” the old woman grunted, bending steadily down, then up, over the washboard.

“Nope,” her tall son-in-law answered. “Donahoe laid me off yesterday on account o’ the white bricklayers said they couldn’t lay bricks with a nigger.”

“Always something to keep you from workin’,” panted Hager.

“Sure is,” agreed Jimboy pleasantly. “But don’t worry, me and Sandy’s gonna catch you a mess o’ fish for supper today. How’s that, ma?”

===Chinese===

哈丽雅特上工去了；爷儿俩从厨房门里出来时，黑格早就在苹果树下的洗衣桶边忙了好一阵了。「哼！今儿早上不干活，是吧？」老太太咕哝着，在搓衣板上一俯一起、一俯一起，不歇手地搓着。

「不干，」高个子的女婿答道。「多纳霍（Donahoe）昨儿把我辞了，就因为那些白人砖匠说，他们没法跟黑鬼一块儿砌砖。」

「总有个由头叫你不肯干活。」黑格喘着气说。

「可不是，」吉姆博伊痛痛快快地附和。「不过你别愁，我和桑迪今儿给你钓一顿鱼当晚饭。怎么样，妈？」

===Original===

“Don’t need no fish,” the old woman answered. “An’ don’t come ma-in’ me! Layin’ round here fishin’ when you ought to be out makin’ money to take care o’ this house an’ that chile o’ your’n.” The suds rose foamy white about her black arms as the clothes plushed up and down on the zinc washboard. “Lawd deliver me from a lazy darky!”

But Jimboy and Sandy were already behind the tall corn, digging for bait near the back fence.

“Don’t never let no one woman worry you,” said the boy’s father softly, picking the moist wriggling worms from the upturned loam. “Treat ’em like chickens, son. Throw ’em a little corn and they’ll run after you, but don’t give ’em too much. If you do, they’ll stop layin’ and expect you to wait on ’em.”

“Will they?” asked Sandy.

===Chinese===

「用不着鱼，」老太太回道。「也少『妈』呀『妈』的叫俺！该出去挣钱养这个家、养你那娃儿的时候，你倒赖在这儿钓鱼。」衣裳在白铁搓衣板上上下下地搓，肥皂泡雪白雪白地涌在她两条黑胳膊四周。「主啊，快把俺从这个懒老黑手里救出去吧！」

可吉姆博伊和桑迪早就钻到高高的玉米棵子后头，挨着后篱笆挖鱼饵去了。

「儿啊，可千万别叫任何一个女人搅得你心烦，」孩子的父亲轻声说，一边从翻起的沃土里拾起湿漉漉乱扭的蚯蚓。「要把她们当母鸡待，儿子。撒她们一把玉米，她们就追着你跑；可别喂得太足。喂足了，她们就不下蛋了，反倒要你来伺候啦。」

「真的会那样吗？」桑迪问。

---

===Original===

The warm afternoon sun made the river a languid sheet of muddy gold, glittering away towards the bridge and the flour-mills a mile and a half off. Here in the quiet, on the end of a rotting jetty among the reeds, Jimboy and his son sat silently. A long string of small silver fish hung down into the water, keeping fresh, and the fishing-lines were flung far out in the stream, waiting for more bites. Not a breeze on the flat brown-gold river, not a ripple, not a sound. But once the train came by behind them, pouring out a great cloud of smoke and cinders and shaking the jetty.

“That’s Number Five,” said Jimboy. “Sure is flyin’,” as the train disappeared between rows of empty boxcars far down the track, sending back a hollow clatter as it shot past the flour-mills, whose stacks could be dimly seen through the heat haze. Once the engine’s whistle moaned shrilly.

“She’s gone now,” said Jimboy, as the last click of the wheels died away. And, except for the drone of a green fly about the can of bait, there was again no sound to disturb the two fishermen.

Jimboy gazed at his lines. Across the river Sandy could make out, in the brilliant sunlight, the gold of wheat-fields and the green of trees on the hills. He wondered if it would be nice to live over there in the country.

===Chinese===

午后温热的太阳把河面照成一片懒洋洋的浑金色，波光粼粼地一直铺向一英里半开外的桥和面粉厂。在这份静谧里，吉姆博伊和儿子坐在芦苇丛中一根朽坏的木栈桥头上，一声不吭。一长串银亮的小鱼垂在水里保鲜，钓线远远抛进河心，等着鱼儿再来咬钩。平坦的黄褐色河面上，没有一丝风，没有一道涟漪，没有一点声响。只有一回，一列火车从他们背后驶过，喷出一大团煤烟火屑，震得栈桥直晃。

「那是五号列车，」吉姆博伊说。「飞得可真快。」火车在轨道远处一排排空车厢之间没了影，掠过面粉厂时送回一串空荡荡的哐当声；面粉厂的烟囱隔着暑气，只瞧得见影影绰绰的轮廓。有一回，机车的汽笛尖利地呜咽了一声。

「走远了，」车轮最后的咔嗒声消逝下去时，吉姆博伊说。这之后，除了一只绿蝇绕着饵罐嗡嗡，再没有什么声响来搅扰这两位钓鱼人。

吉姆博伊盯着他的钓线。河对岸，桑迪在灿烂的阳光下认得出小麦地的金黄和山坡上树木的青翠。他寻思着，住在河那边乡下不知好不好。

===Original===

“Man alive!” his father cried suddenly, hauling vigorously at one of the lines. “Sure got a real bite now.﻿ ﻿… Look at this catfish.” From the water he pulled a large flopping lead-colored creature, with a fierce white mouth bleeding and gaping over the hook.

“He’s on my line!” yelled Sandy. “I caught him!”

“Pshaw!” laughed Jimboy. “You was setting there dreaming.”

“No, I wasn’t!”

But just then, at the mills, the five-o’clock whistles blew. “Oh, gee, dad!” cried the boy, frightened. “I was s’posed to go to Mis’ Rice’s to help mama, and I come near forgetting it. She wants to get through early this evenin’ to go to lodge meeting. I gotta hurry and go help her.”

“Well, you better beat it then, and I’ll look out for your line like I been doing and bring the fishes home.”

===Chinese===

「好家伙！」父亲忽然喊起来，使劲拽着其中一根线。「这回可是真咬钩了。……瞧这条鲶鱼。」他从水里拽出一条扑腾乱跳的铅灰色大鱼，凶巴巴的白嘴淌着血，豁开在鱼钩上。

「它咬的是我的线！」桑迪嚷道。「是我钓到的！」

「嗐！」吉姆博伊笑了。「你刚才准是坐在那儿做梦呢。」

「我才没有！」

可就在这时，面粉厂那边，五点下班的汽笛响了。「哎呀，爸！」孩子吓了一跳，喊道。「我本该上赖斯太太家帮妈妈干活的，差点儿把这事给忘了。她今晚想早点干完活去社团聚会。我得赶紧跑去帮她。」

「那你还不快跑，」父亲说，「我照旧替你看着线，把鱼带回家。」

===Original===

So the little fellow balanced himself across the jetty, scrambled up the bank, and ran down the railroad track towards town. He was quite out of breath when he reached the foot of Penrose Street, with Mrs. Rice’s house still ten blocks away, so he walked awhile, then ran again, down the long residential street, with its large houses sitting in green shady lawns far back from the sidewalk. Sometimes a sprinkler, attached to a long rubber hose, sprayed fountain-like jets of cold water on the thirsty grass. In one yard three golden-haired little girls were playing under an elm-tree, and in another a man and some children were having a leisurely game of croquet.

Finally Sandy turned into a big yard. The delicious scent of frying beefsteak greeted the sweating youngster as he reached the screen of the white lady’s kitchen-door. Inside, Annjee was standing over the hot stove seasoning something in a saucepan, beads of perspiration on her dark face, and large damp spots under the arms of her dress.

“You better get here!” she said. “And me waiting for you for the last hour. Here, take this pick and break some ice for the tea.” Sandy climbed up on a stool and raised the icebox lid while his mother opened the oven and pulled out a pan of golden-brown biscuits. “Made these for your father,” she remarked. “The white folks ain’t asked for ’em, but they like ’em, too, so they can serve for both.﻿ ﻿… Jimboy’s crazy about biscuits.﻿ ﻿… Did he work today?”

“No’m,” said Sandy, jabbing at the ice. “We went fishing.”

===Chinese===

于是小家伙踩着栈桥稳稳当当地走过去，爬上河岸，顺着铁道朝城里跑。跑到彭罗斯街街口时，他已上气不接下气，可赖斯太太家还隔着十个街区，于是他走一程，再跑一程，顺着那条长长的住宅街往下走——大房子都远远缩在人行道后头，坐落在绿荫草坪深处。有时，一只接在长橡皮管上的洒水器，喷泉似的往干渴的草地上洒着凉水。一户院子里，三个金头发的小姑娘在榆树下玩耍；另一户院子里，一个男人和几个孩子正不紧不慢地打着槌球。

终于，桑迪拐进一个大院子。满头大汗的小家伙刚走到这位白人太太厨房门的纱门跟前，煎牛排的喷香就迎面而来。屋里，安吉正站在热炉子跟前，给长柄小锅里的一样什么东西调味，黑脸上一颗颗汗珠，衣裳腋下洇出两大片汗渍。

「你可算来了！」她说。「我可是等你等了一个钟头了。喏，拿这把冰锥，给茶水凿点儿冰。」桑迪爬上凳子，掀开冰柜盖子；母亲打开烤箱，端出一盘金黄透褐的烤饼。「这是给你爸做的，」她说。「白人没开口要，可他们也爱吃，一份正好两头吃。……吉姆博伊爱烤饼爱得要命。……他今儿干活了吗？」

「没干，」桑迪说，一边一下一下凿着冰。「我们钓鱼去了。」

===Original===

At that moment Mrs. Rice came into the kitchen, tall and blond, in a thin flowered gown. She was a middle-aged white woman with a sharp nasal voice.

“Annjee, I’d like the potatoes served just as they are in the casserole. And make several slices of very thin toast for my father. Now, be sure they *are* thin!”

“Yes, ma’am,” said Annjee stirring a spoonful of flour into the frying-pan, making a thick brown gravy.

“Old thin toast,” muttered Annjee when Mrs. Rice had gone back to the front. “Always bothering round the kitchen! Here ’tis lodge-meeting night﻿—dinner late anyhow﻿—and she coming telling me to stop and make toast for the old man! He ain’t too indigestible to eat biscuits like the rest of ’em.﻿ ﻿… White folks sure is a case!” She laid three slices of bread on top of the stove. “So spoiled with colored folks waiting on ’em all their days! Don’t know what they’ll do in heaven, ’cause I’m gonna sit down up there myself.”

===Chinese===

正在这时，赖斯太太走进厨房——高挑个儿，金头发，穿一件薄薄的花布裙。她是个嗓音尖利带鼻音的中年白人妇女。

「安吉，土豆就盛在砂锅里原样端上来。再给我父亲烤几片很薄的吐司。记住，一定要*薄*！」

「是的，太太。」安吉应道，往煎锅里搅进一勺面粉，调出浓稠的棕色肉汁。

「又是薄吐司，」赖斯太太回前头去了以后，安吉咕哝道。「老在厨房里绕来绕去！今晚赶上是社团聚会的日子——晚饭本来就晚了——她还跑来叫我停下，给那老头子烤吐司！他跟旁人一样吃得了烤饼，肠胃没那么娇气。……这些白人可真是活宝！」她在炉面上搁了三片面包。「叫有色人种伺候了一辈子，惯成这样！真不知道他们上了天堂可怎么办——我到了那儿，可要自个儿坐下歇着了。」

===Original===

Annjee took the biscuits, light and brown, and placed some on a pink plate she had warmed. She carried them, with the butter and jelly, into the dining-room. Then she took the steak from the warmer, dished up the vegetables into gold-rimmed serving-dishes, and poured the gravy, which smelled deliciously onion-flavored.

“Gee, I’m hungry,” said the child, with his eyes on the big steak ready to go in to the white people.

“Well, just wait,” replied his mother. “You come to work, not to eat.﻿ ﻿… Whee! but it’s hot today!” She wiped her wet face and put on a large white bungalow apron that had been hanging behind the door. Then she went with the iced tea and a pitcher of water into the dining-room, struck a Chinese gong, and came back to the kitchen to get the dishes of steaming food, which she carried in to the table.

It was some time before she returned from waiting on the table; so Sandy, to help her, began to scrape out the empty pans and put them to soak in the sink. He ate the stewed corn that had stuck in the bottom of one, and rubbed a piece of bread in the frying-pan where the gravy had been. His mother came out with the water-pitcher, broke some ice for it, and returned to the dining-room where Sandy could hear laughter, and the clinking of spoons in tea-glasses, and women talking. When Annjee came back into the kitchen, she took four custards from the icebox and placed them on gold-rimmed plates.

===Chinese===

安吉拿起烤饼——松软焦黄——往焐热过的粉红盘子里搁了几只，连同黄油和果酱一道端进饭厅。然后她从保温架上取下牛排，把蔬菜盛进金边的分菜盘，浇上肉汁——那肉汁带着洋葱香，闻着馋人。

「哎呀，我饿了，」孩子说，眼睛盯着那份就要端进去给白人吃的大牛排。

「哼，等着吧，」母亲回道。「你是来干活的，不是来吃的。……嚯！今天可真热！」她擦了擦湿漉漉的脸，把挂在门后的一件宽大的白色家常罩裙穿上。接着她端着冰茶和一壶水进了饭厅，敲响一面中国铜锣，又回厨房来取那些热气腾腾的菜盘，把它们一一端上桌去。

她在饭厅里伺候餐桌，好一阵子才回来；桑迪为了帮她，动手刮净空锅，泡进水槽里。他把粘在一口锅底的炖玉米吃了，又拿一块面包在煎过肉汁的锅里擦了擦。母亲提着水壶出来，给它凿了些冰，又回到饭厅去——桑迪听得见那边有笑声，有玻璃茶杯里勺子的叮当声，有女人们说话的声音。安吉回到厨房时，从冰柜里取出四份蛋奶冻，摆在金边的盘子上。

===Original===

“They’re about through,” she said to her son. “Sit down and I’ll fix you up.”

Sandy was very hungry and he hoped Mrs. Rice’s family hadn’t eaten all the steak, which had looked so good with its brown gravy and onions.

Shortly, his mother returned carrying the dishes that had been filled with hot food. She placed them on the kitchen-table in front of Sandy, but they were no longer full and no longer hot. The corn had thickened to a paste, and the potatoes were about gone; but there was still a ragged piece of steak left on the platter.

“Don’t eat it all,” said Annjee warningly. “I want to take some home to your father.”

The bell rang in the dining-room. Annjee went through the swinging door and returned bearing a custard that had been but little touched.

“Here, sonny﻿—the old man says it’s too sweet for his stomach, so you can have this.” She set the yellow cornstarch before Sandy. “He’s seen these ripe peaches out here today and he wants some, that’s all. More trouble than he’s worth, po’ old soul, and me in a hurry!” She began to peel the fruit. “Just like a chile, ’deed he is!” she added, carrying the sliced peaches into the dining-room and leaving Sandy with a plate of food before him, eating slowly. “When you rushing to get out, seems like white folks tries theirselves.”

===Chinese===

「他们差不多吃完了，」她对儿子说。「坐下吧，我给你张罗。」

桑迪饿得很，只盼着赖斯太太一家没把牛排吃个精光——浇着棕色肉汁、配着洋葱的那份牛排，看着多馋人啊。

不多会儿，母亲把方才盛满热菜的盘子端了回来。她把它们摆在桑迪面前的厨房桌上，可盘子不再满，也不再热了。玉米坨得成了浆，土豆也快见底；不过大盘子里还剩着一块不规整的牛排。

「别都吃了，」安吉提醒道。「我想带点儿回家给你爸。」

饭厅里铃响了。安吉穿过弹簧门出去，端回来一份只动过一小角的蛋奶冻。

「喏，小子——老头子说这东西太甜，他胃受不住，这份归你。」她把那黄澄澄的玉米粉冻摆在桑迪面前。「他今儿瞧见这儿的熟桃子了，想吃点儿，就这么档子事。可怜的老东西，真是伺候他不上算，我还赶着工夫呢！」她动手削起桃子来。「简直跟个孩子似的！」她添了一句，端着切好的桃片进了饭厅，撇下桑迪对着面前一盘饭慢慢吃着。「你越是急着要出门，这些白人就越要跟你过不去。」

===Original===

In a moment she returned, ill-tempered, and began to scold Sandy for taking so long with his meal.

“I asked you to help me so’s I can get to the lodge on time, and you just set and chew and eat!﻿ ﻿… Here, wipe these dishes, boy!” Annjee began hurriedly to lay plates in a steaming row on the shelf of the sink; so Sandy got up and, between mouthfuls of pudding, wiped them with a large dishtowel.

Soon Mrs. Rice came into the kitchen again, briskly, through the swinging door and glanced about her. Sandy felt ashamed for the white woman to see him eating a leftover pudding from her table, so he put the spoon down.

“Annjee,” the mistress said sharply. “I wish you wouldn’t put quite so much onion in your sauce for the steak. I’ve mentioned it to you several times before, and you know very well we don’t like it.”

===Chinese===

不多会儿她回来了，气冲冲的，开口数落桑迪吃饭拖拖拉拉。

「我叫你来帮我，好按时赶上社团的聚会，你倒好，光坐着嚼啊吃啊！……来，把这些盘子擦了，小子！」安吉急急忙忙把盘子一只只还冒着热气，码上水槽的架子；桑迪只好站起来，嘴里嚼着布丁，一边用一条大擦碗巾把盘子擦干。

不多会儿，赖斯太太又风风火火地从弹簧门进了厨房，四下扫了一眼。桑迪不好意思让这白人女人瞧见自己在吃她家桌上剩下的布丁，便放下了勺子。

「安吉，」女主人口气尖利地说。「牛排的浇汁里，请你别放这么多的洋葱。这话我从前跟你说过好几回了，你很清楚我们不喜欢。」

===Original===

“Yes, ma’am,” said Annjee.

“And do *please* be careful that our drinking water is cold before meals are served.﻿ ﻿… You were certainly careless tonight. You must think more about what you are doing, Annjee.”

Mrs. Rice went out again through the swinging door, but Sandy stood near the sink with a burning face and eyes that had suddenly filled with angry tears. He couldn’t help it﻿—hearing his sweating mother reprimanded by this tall white woman in the flowered dress. Black, hardworking Annjee answered: “Yes, ma’am,” and that was all﻿—but Sandy cried.

“Dry up,” his mother said crossly when she saw him, thinking he was crying because she had asked him to work. “What’s come over you, anyway?﻿—can’t even wipe a few plates for me and act nice about it!”

===Chinese===

「是的，太太。」安吉说。

「还有，请*务必*留神，上饭之前，我们的饮用水要冰过。……你今晚实在太马虎了。你得多用点儿心思在自己的活计上，安吉。」

赖斯太太又从弹簧门出去了，桑迪却站在水槽边，脸烧得火辣辣的，眼里突然涌满了愤怒的泪水。他忍不住——眼听着自己汗涔涔的母亲被这个穿花裙子的白人高个儿女人训斥。黑皮肤的、操劳的安吉应了一句：「是的，太太。」就完了——可桑迪哭了。

「把眼泪擦干，」母亲看见他，没好气地说，只当他是因为被叫干活才哭的。「你到底犯了什么毛病？——替我擦几个盘子都不行，还摆出这副哭丧脸！」

===Original===

He didn’t answer. When the dining-room had been cleared and the kitchen put in order, Annjee told him to empty the garbage while she wrapped in newspapers several little bundles of food to carry to Jimboy. Then they went out the back door, around the big house to the street, and trudged the fourteen blocks to Aunt Hager’s, taking shortcuts through alleys, passing under arc-lights that sputtered whitely in the deepening twilight, and greeting with an occasional “Howdy” other poor colored folks also coming home from work.

“How are you, Sister Jones?”

“Right smart, I thank yuh!” as they passed.

Once Annjee spoke to her son. “Evening’s the only time we niggers have to ourselves!” she said. “Thank God for night﻿ ﻿… ’cause all day you gives to white folks.”

===Chinese===

他没吭声。饭厅收拾停当、厨房归置整齐以后，安吉叫他去倒垃圾，她自己用报纸包了几小包吃食，要带给吉姆博伊。然后他们从后门出去，绕过大房子走到街上，深一脚浅一脚地走了十四个街区，回黑格外婆家去——他们抄小巷走近道，从暮色渐浓里噼啪闪着白光的弧光灯下走过，偶尔跟其他同样下班回家的穷有色人种道一声「你好啊」。

「你好啊，琼斯姊妹？」

「好着哩，多谢你嘞！」擦肩而过时，对方应道。

有一回，安吉对儿子说起了话。「晚上才是咱们黑人唯一归自己的时辰！」她说。「感谢上帝还有黑夜……因为白天一整天，你都给了白人。」

## VII White Folks / 七 白人

===Original===
When they got home, Aunt Hager was sitting in the cool of the evening on her new porch, which had been rebuilt for thirty-five dollars added to the mortgage. The old woman was in her rocking-chair, with Jimboy, one foot on the ground and his back against a pillar, lounging at her feet. The two were quarrelling amicably over nothing as Annjee and Sandy approached.

“Good-evenin’, you-all,” said Annjee. “I brought you a nice piece o’ steak, Jimboy-sugar, and some biscuits to go with it. Come on in and eat while I get dressed to go to the drill practice. I got to hurry.”

“We don’t want no steak now,” Jimboy answered without moving. “Aunt Hager and me had fresh fish for supper and egg-corn-bread and we’re full. We don’t need nothin’ more.”

“Oh!﻿ ﻿…” said Annjee disappointedly. “Well, come on in anyhow, honey, and talk while I get dressed.” So he rose lazily and followed his wife into the house.

===Chinese===
母子俩到家的时候，黑格外婆正趁着傍晚的凉快，坐在自家新门廊上——这门廊是花了三十五美元、加在抵押账上重新修起来的。老太太坐在摇椅里，吉姆博伊一只脚踩着地，背抵着廊柱，懒洋洋地歪在她脚边。安吉和桑迪走近的时候，这两个人正有一搭没一搭地、亲亲热热地拌着不咸不淡的嘴。

「晚上好啊，你们大伙儿，」安吉说。「我给你带了块好牛排，吉姆博伊宝贝，还有配着吃的烤饼。快进来吃吧，趁我换衣裳的工夫吃——换好衣裳我就得赶去操演排练，得抓紧呢。」

「这会儿可不想吃什么牛排，」吉姆博伊动也不动地答话。「黑格外婆和我晚饭吃了鲜鱼，还有鸡蛋玉米面包，撑得很。啥也不需要了。」

「噢！……」安吉好不失望。「那……好歹也进来吧，宝贝，我换衣裳的时候陪我说说话。」他这才懒洋洋起了身，跟着老婆进屋去了。

===Original===
Shortly, Sister Johnson, pursued by the ever-present Willie-Mae, came through the blue-grey darkness from next door. “Good-evenin’, Sister Williams; how you been today?”

“Tolable,” answered Hager, “ ’ceptin’ I’s tired out from washin’ an’ rinsin’. Have a seat.﻿ ﻿… You Sandy, go in de house an’ get Sister Johnson a settin’-chair.﻿ ﻿… Where’s Tom?”

“Lawd, chile, he done gone to bed long ago. That there sewer-diggin’ job ain’t so good fer a man old as Tom. He ’bout played out.﻿ ﻿… I done washed fer Mis’ Cohn maself today.﻿ ﻿… Umh! dis cheer feels good!﻿ ﻿… Looked like to me she had near ’bout fifty babies’ diddies in de wash. You know she done got twins, ’sides dat young-’un born last year.”

The conversation of the two old women rambled on as their grandchildren ran across the front yard laughing, shrieking, wrestling; catching fireflies and watching them glow in closed fists, then releasing them to twinkle in the sultry night-air.

===Chinese===
没多大会儿，约翰逊姊妹从隔壁过来了，身后照例紧跟着寸步不离的薇莉-梅，娘儿俩穿过蓝灰色的夜色。「晚上好，威廉斯姊妹；你今儿一天可好？」

「还凑合，」黑格答道，「就是又洗又涮的，把俺累乏了。坐吧。……桑迪，进屋去给约翰逊姊妹搬把坐的椅子。……汤姆呢？」

「主啊，娃儿，他早上床歇着啦。挖下水道那活儿，对汤姆这把年纪的人可不怎么样。他差不多累垮了……今儿俺是亲自给科恩太太（Mis' Cohn）洗的衣裳。……嗯哼！这椅子坐着真得劲！……俺瞅着她那一桶洗的衣裳里，差不多有五十块娃娃的尿布。你晓得的，她生了对双胞胎，还不算去年生的那个小娃儿。」

两个老太太的话头东拉西扯地续着，孙儿孙女们在前院里跑来跑去，又是笑又是叫，扭作一团摔跤；捉萤火虫，看它们在攥紧的拳头里一明一灭地发光，再撒手放走，让它们在闷热的夜气里忽闪忽闪。

===Original===
Harriett came singing out of the house and sat down on the edge of the porch. “Lord, it’s hot!﻿ ﻿… How are you, Mis’ Johnson? I didn’t see you in the dark.”

“Jest tolable, chile,” said the old woman, “but I can’t kick. Honey, when you gits old as I is, you’ll be doin’ well if you’s livin’ a-tall, de way you chillens runs round now’days! How come you ain’t out to some party dis evenin’?”

“O, there’s no party tonight,” said Harriett laughing. “Besides, this new job of mine’s a heartbreaker, Mis’ Johnson. I got to stay home and rest now. I’m kitchen-girl at that New Albert Restaurant, and time you get through wrestling with pots and arguing with white waitresses and colored cooks, you don’t feel much like running out at night. But the shifts aren’t bad, though, food’s good, and﻿—well, you can’t expect everything.” She shrugged her shoulders against the two-by-four pillar on which her back rested.

“Long’s it keeps you off de streets, I’s glad,” said Hager, rocking contentedly. “Maybe I can git you goin’ to church agin now.”

===Chinese===
哈丽雅特唱着歌从屋里出来，在门廊沿上坐下。「天哪，真热！……您好吗，约翰逊太太？黑灯瞎火的，我才没瞧见您。」

「也就是凑合，娃儿，」老太太说，「不过俺没啥可抱怨的。宝贝，等你活到俺这把年纪，照如今你们这些娃儿这么个疯跑法，还能活着就算你本事啦！今儿晚上你咋没出去赴个玩局呢？」

「哦，今儿晚上没有聚会，」哈丽雅特笑着说。「再说，我这份新差事可真磨人，约翰逊太太。如今我得待在家里歇着。我在那家新阿尔伯特餐馆（New Albert Restaurant）当厨房丫头，等你跟一口口大锅摔完跤、跟白人女侍和黑人厨子吵完嘴，夜里也就没多大心思往外跑了。不过班次倒不赖，伙食也好，还有——唉，人不能样样都称心。」她把背靠在那根细细的小方柱上，耸了耸肩。

「只要这差事能让你别满大街野，俺就高兴，」黑格说着，舒舒坦坦地摇着摇椅。「兴许俺这回还能哄得你重新上教堂去了呢。」

===Original===
“Aw, I don’t like church,” the girl replied.

“An’, chile, I can’t blame you much,” said Sister Johnson, fumbling in the pocket of her apron. “De way dese churches done got now’days.﻿ ﻿… Sandy, run in de house an’ ask yo’ pappy fo’ a match to light ma pipe.﻿ ﻿… It ain’t ‘Come to Jesus’ no mo’ a-tall. Ministers dese days an’ times don’t care nothin’ ’bout po’ Jesus. ’Stead o’ dat it’s rally dis an’ collection dat, an’ de aisle wants a new carpet, an’ de pastor needs a ’lectric fan fer his red-hot self.” The old sister spat into the yard. “Money! That’s all ’tis! An’ white folkses’ religion﻿—Lawd help! ’Taint no use in mentionin’ them.”

“True,” agreed Hager.

“ ’Cause if de gates o’ heaven shuts in white folkses’ faces like de do’s o’ dey church in us niggers’ faces, it’ll be too bad! Yes, sir! One thing sho, de Lawd ain’t prejudiced!”

“No,” said Hager; “but He don’t love ugly, neither in niggers nor in white folks.”

===Chinese===
「唉，我不喜欢上教堂，」姑娘答道。

「再说，娃儿，俺也真怪不得你，」约翰逊姊妹说着，往围裙兜里摸索。「如今这些教会都成了这副样子……桑迪，跑趟屋里，跟你爹要根火柴来点俺的烟斗……如今压根儿再没有『来就耶稣』喽。如今这年月的牧师们，一点儿也不把可怜的耶稣放在心上。取而代之的是今天一场集会募捐、明天一场收捐奉献，过道里要铺新地毯，牧师他自己那火烧火燎的贵身子还缺一台电扇呢。」老姊妹朝院子里啐了一口。「钱！除了钱没别的！还有白人那宗教——主啊，保佑！提他们都不值得提。」

「可不，」黑格应和着。

「因为天堂的大门要是也像他们教会的门关到咱们黑人脸上那样，关到白人的脸上，那可有他们受的！可不是嘛，先生！有一样是板上钉钉的：主不偏心眼儿！」

「是不偏，」黑格说，「可他也不容人使坏，黑人使坏也好，白人使坏也好，他都不容。」

===Original===
“Now, talking about white folks’ religion,” said Annjee, emerging from the house with a fresh white dress on, “why, Mis’ Rice where I work don’t think no more about playing bridge on Sunday than she does about praying﻿—and I ain’t never seen her pray yet.”

“You’re nuts,” said Jimboy behind her. “People’s due to have a little fun on Sundays. That’s what’s the matter with colored folks now﻿—work all week and then set up in church all day Sunday, and don’t even know what’s goin’ on in the rest of the world.”

“Huh!” grunted Hager.

“Well, we won’t argue, daddy.” Annjee smiled. “Come on and walk a piece with me, sweetness. Here ’tis nearly nine and I should a been at the hall at eight, but colored folks are always behind the clock. Come on, Jimboy.”

“Goodbye, mama,” yelled Sandy from the lawn as his parents strolled up the street together.

===Chinese===
「说到白人的宗教，」安吉从屋里出来，身上换了条簇新的白裙子，「嘿，我做工的那家赖斯太太，礼拜天打桥牌跟祷告一样不当回事——反正到现在，我还没见她祷告过一回呢。」

「你脑子出毛病啦，」吉姆博伊在她背后搭腔。「礼拜天人就该乐呵乐呵。这就是如今有色人种的毛病——一礼拜干到头，礼拜天又在教堂里坐上一整天，世界上别处出了什么事压根儿不晓得。」

「哼！」黑格哼了一声。

「得了，咱们不抬杠了，孩子他爹。」安吉笑了。「陪我走一截吧，亲爱的。这就快九点了，八点我就该到会所了，可有色人种永远落在钟点后头。走吧，吉姆博伊。」

「妈妈再见！」桑迪在草坪上大声喊。他爸妈相偕着沿街遛达去了。

===Original===
“Jimboy’s right,” said Harriett. “Darkies do like the church too much, but white folks don’t care nothing about it at all. They’re too busy getting theirs out of this world, not from God. And I don’t blame ’em, except that they’re so mean to niggers. They’re right, though, looking out for themselves﻿ ﻿… and yet I hate ’em for it. They don’t have to mistreat us besides, do they?”

“Honey, don’t talk that way,” broke in Hager. “It ain’t Christian, chile. If you don’t like ’em, pray for ’em, but don’t feel evil against ’em. I was in slavery, Harrie, an’ I been knowin’ white folks all ma life, an’ they’s good as far as they can see﻿—but when it comes to po’ niggers, they just can’t see far, that’s all.”

Harriett opened her mouth to reply, but Jimboy, who left Annjee at the corner and had returned to the porch, beat her to it. “We too dark for ’em, ma,” he laughed. “How they gonna see in the dark? You colored folks oughta get lighter, that’s what!”

“Shut up yo’ mouth, you yaller rooster!” said Sister Johnson. “White folks is white folks, an’ dey’s mean! I can’t help what Hager say,” the old woman disagreed emphatically with her crony. “Ain’t I been knowin’ crackers sixty-five years, an’ ain’t dey de cause o’ me bein’ here in Stanton ’stead o’ in ma home right today? De dirty buzzards! Ain’t I nussed t’ree of ’em up from babies like ma own chillens, and ain’t dem same t’ree boys done turned round an’ helped run me an’ Tom out o’ town?”

===Chinese===
「吉姆博伊说得对，」哈丽雅特说。「老黑们是太迷教堂了，白人呢，压根儿没把它当回事。他们忙着从这个世界上捞自己那份好处，不是从上帝那儿。这个我不怪他们——只怪他们对黑人太刻毒。为自己打算，这倒没错……可我还是恨他们这一点。话说回来，他们犯不着再作践咱们呀，是不是？」

「宝贝，别这么说话，」黑格插进来。「这不合基督的道，娃儿。你要是不喜欢他们，就为他们祷告，可别心里对他们存着歹意。俺当过奴隶，哈丽，俺一辈子都在跟白人打交道，在他们看得见的地方，他们是好的——可一碰上可怜的黑人，他们就是看不远，就这么回事。」

哈丽雅特张嘴正要回话，吉姆博伊却在街角把安吉送到、转回门廊来了，抢了先。「妈，咱们在他们眼里太黑啦，」他笑道。「黑咕隆咚的，他们拿什么看得见？你们有色人种就该往白里变一变，就这么回事！」

「闭上你的嘴，你这个浅皮肤的花公鸡！」约翰逊姊妹说。「白人就是白人，就是心眼坏！黑格爱怎么说随她，」老太太跟老姐妹斩钉截铁地唱起了反调。「俺跟南方穷白佬打了六十五年交道，要不是他们，俺今天会待在斯坦顿，而不是待在俺自个儿的家里？这帮脏兀鹫！俺把他们家那三个小子从奶娃娃带大，跟带大俺自个儿的娃儿一个样，可偏偏就是那三个小子，长大了回过头来帮着把俺和汤姆撵出了镇子！」

===Original===
The old sister took a long draw on her corncob pipe, and a fiery red spot glowed in its bowl, while Willie-Mae and Sandy stopped playing and sat down on the porch as she began a tale they had all heard at least a dozen times.

“I’s tole you ’bout it befo’, ain’t I?” asked Sister Johnson.

“Not me,” lied Jimboy, who was anxious to keep her going.

“No, you haven’t,” Harriett assured her.

“Well, it were like dis,” and the story unwound itself, the preliminary details telling how, as a young freed-girl after the Civil War, Sister Johnson had gone into service for a white planter’s family in a Mississippi town near Vicksburg. While attached to this family, she married Tom Johnson, then a fieldhand, and raised five children of her own during the years that followed, besides caring for three boys belonging to her white mistress, nursing them at her black breasts and sometimes leaving her own young ones in the cabin to come and stay with her white charges when they were ill. These called her mammy, too, and when they were men and married, she still went to see them and occasionally worked for their families.

===Chinese===
老姊妹深深吸了一口玉米芯烟斗，斗锅里一点火红亮了起来。薇莉-梅和桑迪也不玩了，在门廊上坐下——她要开讲那个大伙儿起码听过十来遍的老故事了。

「俺从前给你们讲过这事儿的，不是吗？」约翰逊姊妹问。

「没给我讲过，」吉姆博伊撒了个谎，一心巴望着她讲下去。

「真没讲过。」哈丽雅特给她作保。

「嗯，事情是这么回事，」故事就这样娓娓铺开，先垫了些开头的细目：南北战争以后，约翰逊姊妹还是个刚得了自由的年轻姑娘，进了密西西比州维克斯堡（Vicksburg）附近一座镇子上的一户白人庄园主家帮佣。在这户人家帮工期间，她嫁给了当时还是田间苦力的汤姆·约翰逊，往后那些年，生养拉扯大了自己生的五个孩子，还照看着白人女主人的三个男孩，用自己黑黑的乳房哺育他们；有时白人小主人病了，她就把自己年幼的娃儿撂在小木屋里，过来守着他们。这三个孩子也叫她嬷嬷（mammy）；他们长大成人、娶妻成家以后，她还去看他们，偶尔也给他们的家里帮工。

===Original===
“Now, we niggers all lived at de edge o’ town in what de whites called Crowville, an’ most of us owned little houses an’ farms, an’ we did right well raisin’ cotton an’ sweet ’taters an’ all. Now, dat’s where de trouble started! We was doin’ too well, an’ de white folks said so! But we ain’t paid ’em no ’tention, jest thought dey was talkin’ fer de pastime of it.﻿ ﻿… Well, we all started fixin’ up our houses an’ paintin’ our fences, an’ Crowville looked kinder decent-like when de white folks ’gin to ’mark, so’s we servants could hear ’em, ’bout niggers livin’ in painted houses an’ dressin’ fine like we was somebody!﻿ ﻿… Well, dat went on fer some time wid de whites talkin’ an’ de coloreds doin’ better’n better year by year, sellin’ mo’ cotton ever’ day an’ gittin’ nice furniture an’ buyin’ pianers, till by an’ by a prosp’rous nigger named John Lowdins up an’ bought one o’ dese here new autimobiles﻿—an’ dat settled it!﻿ ﻿… A white man in town one Sat’day night tole John to git out o’ dat damn car ’cause a nigger ain’t got no business wid a autimobile nohow! An’ John say: ‘I ain’t gonna git out!’ Den de white man, what’s been drinkin’, jump up on de runnin’-bo’ad an’ bust John in de mouth fer talkin’ back to him﻿—he a white man, an’ Lowdins nothin’ but a nigger. ‘De very idee!’ he say, and hit John in de face six or seven times. Den John drawed his gun! One! two! t’ree! he fiah, hit dis old redneck cracker in de shoulder, but he ain’t dead! Ain’t nothin’ meant to kill a cracker what’s drunk! But John think he done kilt this white man, an’ so he left him kickin’ in de street while he runs that car o’ his’n lickety-split out o’ town, goes to Vicksburg, an’ catches de river boat.﻿ ﻿… Well, sir! Dat night Crowville’s plumb full o’ white folks wid dogs an’ guns an’ lanterns, shoutin’ an’ yellin’ an’ scarin’ de wits out o’ us coloreds an’ waitin’ us up way late in de nighttime lookin’ fer John, an’ dey don’t find him.﻿ ﻿… Den dey say dey gwine teach dem Crowville niggers a lesson, all of ’em, paintin’ dey houses an’ buyin’ cars an’ livin’ like white folks, so dey comes to our do’s an’ tells us to leave our houses﻿—git de hell out in de fields, ’cause dey don’t want to kill nobody there dis evenin’!﻿ ﻿… Well, sir! Niggers in nightgowns an’ underwear an’ shimmies, half-naked an’ barefooted, was runnin’ ever’ which way in de dark, scratchin’ up dey legs in de briah patches, failin’ on dey faces, scared to death! Po’ ole Pheeny, what ain’t moved from her bed wid de paralytics fo’ six years, dey made her daughters carry her out, screamin’ an’ walleyed, an’ set her in de middle o’ de cotton-patch. An’ Brian, what was sleepin’ naked, jumps up an’ grabs his wife’s apron and runs like a rabbit with not another blessed thing on! Chillens squallin’ ever’where, an’ mens a-pleadin’ an’ a-cussin’, an’ womens cryin’ ‘Lawd ’a’ Mercy’ wid de whites of dey eyes showin’!﻿ ﻿… Den looked like to me ’bout five hundred white men took torches an’ started burnin’ wid fiah ever’ last house, an’ henhouse, an’ shack, an’ barn, an’ privy, an’ shed, an’ cow-slant in de place! An’ all de niggers, when de fiah blaze up, was moanin’ in de fields, callin’ on de Lawd fer help! An’ de fiah light up de whole country clean back to de woods! You could smell fiah, an’ you could see it red, an’ taste de smoke, an’ feel it stingin’ yo’ eyes. An’ you could hear de bo’ads a-fallin’ an’ de glass a-poppin’, an’ po’ animals roastin’ an’ fryin’ an’ a-tearin’ at dey halters. An’ one cow run out, fiah all ovah, wid her milk streamin’ down. An’ de smoke roll up, de cotton-fields were red﻿ ﻿… an’ dey ain’t been no mo’ Crowville after dat night. No, sir! De white folks ain’t left nothin’ fer de niggers, not nary bo’ad standin’ one ’bove another, not even a doghouse.﻿ ﻿… When it were done﻿—nothin’ but ashes!﻿ ﻿… De white mens was ever’where wid guns, scarin’ de po’ blacks an’ keepin’ ’em off, an’ one of ’em say: ‘I got good mind to try yo’-all’s hide, see is it bulletproof﻿—gittin’ so prosp’rous, paintin’ yo’ houses an’ runnin ovah white folks wid yo’ damn gasoline buggies! Well, after dis you’ll damn sight have to bend yo’ backs an’ work a little!’﻿ ﻿… Dat’s what de white man say.﻿ ﻿… But we didn’t﻿—not yit! ’Cause ever’ last nigger moved from there dat Sunday mawnin’. It were right funny to see ole folks what ain’t never been out o’ de backwoods pickin’ up dey feet an’ goin’. Ma Bailey say: ‘De Lawd done let me live eighty years in one place, but ma next eighty’ll be spent in St. Louis.’ An’ she started out walkin’ wid neither bag nor baggage.﻿ ﻿… An’ me an’ Tom took Willie-Mae an’ went to Cairo, an’ Tom started railroad-workin’ wid a gang; then we come on up here, been five summers ago dis August. We ain’t had not even a rag o’ clothes when we left Crowville﻿—so don’t tell me ’bout white folks bein’ good, Hager, ’cause I knows ’em.﻿ ﻿… Yes, indeedy, I really knows ’em.﻿ ﻿… Dey done made us leave our home.”

===Chinese===
「说正经的，俺们黑人当时全住在镇边上，那地方白人管它叫克罗维尔（Crowville）。俺们多半都有自己的小房子、小块地，种棉花、种甜薯什么的，日子过得美滋滋。祸根子就打这儿起的！俺们日子过得太红火了，白人都这么说了！可俺们没理他们那个茬儿，只当他们是嘴上没味儿，说闲话解闷。……后来大家伙儿又一齐动手修整房子、油漆栅栏，克罗维尔瞧着有模有样了，白人就开始发议论——故意说响亮得让俺们这些帮工的听得见——说黑人也配住油漆过的房子、穿光鲜衣裳，好像俺们也是个人物似的！……这光景就这么耗了些日子，白人嘴上不干不净，有色人种却一年强似一年，棉花越卖越多，添置起好家具，还买上了钢琴，直到后来有个发了家的黑人，名叫约翰·洛登斯（John Lowdins）的，一抬手买了辆那时新出的汽车——这一下可炸了锅！……一个礼拜六夜里，镇上一个白人叫约翰从那辆该死的车里滚出来，说黑人压根儿就不配摆弄什么汽车！约翰说：『俺就不出来！』那白人喝了酒，一步跳上车的踏脚板，照约翰的嘴就是一拳，就为他还嘴——他可是个白人，洛登斯不过是个黑鬼。『好大的胆子！』他说着，又朝约翰的脸上打了六七下。约翰这才拔出枪来！砰！砰！砰！三枪，打中那老红脖子穷白佬的肩膀——可他没死！喝醉的穷白佬，天生还轮不上让枪子儿打死哩！可约翰只当自己已经打死了这个白人，就把他撂在街上蹬腿，自己开着那辆车飞也似的冲出镇子，跑到维克斯堡，赶上了开船的轮船。……好家伙！当天夜里，克罗维尔就挤满了白人，带着狗、枪和灯笼，又是嚷又是叫，把俺们有色人种吓得魂儿都出了窍，深更半夜守着不让俺们睡，满处找约翰——可他们没找着。……接着他们放话说，要给克罗维尔的黑人们一个教训，一个不落——刷什么房子，买什么车，还想活得跟白人一样！于是他们闯到俺们家门口，叫俺们滚出自己的房子——都他妈滚到地里去，说是『今儿晚上还不想在那儿弄死谁』！……好家伙！黑人们穿着睡衣的、贴身衣裳的、衬裙的，半光着身子，赤着脚，黑地里四下乱窜，腿在荆棘丛里刮得稀烂，摔得嘴啃泥，一个个吓得要死！可怜的老菲尼（Pheeny），瘫在中风上六年没起过床，他们逼着她闺女把她抬出来，她又嚎又叫，眼珠子瞪得溜圆，他们把她搁在棉花地正当间儿。还有布莱恩（Brian），正光着身子睡觉，蹦起来抓了他老婆的围裙就跑，兔子似的，浑身上下再没一样东西！娃儿们到处嚎，男人们又求又咒骂，女人们哭喊着『主啊，可怜可怜』，眼白都翻出来了！……跟着，俺瞅着约莫五百个白人男人点起火把，放起火来，把那地方上的每一座房子，还有鸡窝、棚屋、谷仓、茅房、小披屋、牛棚，一处不落全烧了！大火一蹿起来，黑人们全在地里哀号，求主搭救！火光照亮了整个一片乡下，一直亮到树林子跟前！火的味儿闻得见，红彤彤的火看得见，烟呛得嘴里尝得出滋味，熏得眼睛生疼。还听得见木板塌下来、玻璃噼啪炸响，可怜的牲口在火里烤着、烙着，死命扯着拴它们的缰绳。有一头母牛跑了出来，浑身是火，奶顺着身子往下淌。浓烟滚滚往上冒，棉花地红彤彤一片……打那天夜里起，世上就再没有克罗维尔了。可不是嘛，先生！白人没给黑人们剩下一样东西，一块木板摞在一块木板上的都没有，连个狗窝都没留。……等一切完了——只剩下灰！……白人男人们端着枪四处把守，吓唬可怜的黑人们，不让他们靠近，其中一个说：『我倒真想试试你们这帮家伙的皮，看它挡不挡子弹——发达起来了是吧，刷你们的房子，开着你们那该死的汽油车满街压白人！哼，往后你们可就得乖乖弯下腰来，多干点儿活儿了！』……白人就是这么说的。……可俺们没弯——那会儿还没有！因为那个礼拜天一大早，最后一个黑人也都从那儿搬走了。眼见那些一辈子没出过穷乡僻壤的老人们抬脚就走，也真是稀奇。贝利老奶奶（Ma Bailey）说：『主让俺在一个地方活了八十年，俺下一个八十年要挪到圣路易斯去过了。』说着她就迈开步上了路，一个包袱、一件行李都没带。……俺和汤姆带着薇莉-梅去了开罗（Cairo），汤姆跟上修铁路的一伙人做工；后来俺们一路来到这儿，到这个八月，满五个夏天了。俺们离开克罗维尔的时候，身上连一块布片都没有——所以别跟俺说什么白人心眼好，黑格，俺太知道他们了。……可不是嘛，真真的，俺真真是知道他们的。……是他们逼得俺们离了自个儿的家。」

===Original===
The old woman knocked her pipe against the edge of the porch, emptying its dead ashes into the yard, and for a moment no one spoke. Sandy, trembling, watched a falling star drop behind the trees. Then Jimboy’s deep voice, like a bitter rumble in the dark, broke the silence.

“I know white folks, too,” he said. “I lived in the South.”

“And I ain’t never been South,” added Harriett hoarsely, “but I know ’em right here﻿ ﻿… and I hate ’em!”

“De Lawd hears you,” said Hager.

“I don’t care if He does hear me, mama! You and Annjee are too easy. You just take whatever white folks give you﻿—*coon* to your face, and *nigger* behind your backs﻿—and don’t say nothing. You run to some white person’s back door for every job you get, and then they pay you one dollar for five dollars’ worth of work, and fire you whenever they get ready.”

===Chinese===
老太太把烟斗在门廊沿上磕了磕，把死灰磕进院子里，一时谁也没作声。桑迪哆嗦着，望着一颗流星坠到树后头去了。随后，吉姆博伊低沉的嗓音像黑暗里一声苦涩的闷雷，打破了寂静。

「我也了解白人，」他说。「我在南方住过。」

「我压根儿没去过南方，」哈丽雅特哑着嗓子接口道，「可就在这儿，我也认得他们……我恨他们！」

「主听着呢。」黑格说。

「听见就听见，妈，我才不在乎！你和安吉太好拿捏了。白人给你们什么你们就受什么——当着你们的面叫*黑崽子*，背着你们叫*黑鬼*——你们一声也不吭。你们找的每一样活计，都是跑到白人的后门口去讨来的，然后五块钱的活儿他们只付你一块钱，想什么时候打发你走，就什么时候打发你走。」

===Original===
“They do that all right,” said Jimboy. “They don’t mind firin’ you. Wasn’t I layin’ brick on the *Daily Leader* building and the white union men started sayin’ they couldn’t work with me because I wasn’t in the union? So the boss come up and paid me off. ‘Good man, too,’ he says to me, ‘but I can’t buck the union.’ So I said I’d join, but I knew they wouldn’t let me before I went to the office. Anyhow, I tried. I told the guys there I was a bricklayer and asked ’em how I was gonna work if I couldn’t be in the union. And the fellow who had the cards, secretary I guess he was, says kinder sharp, like he didn’t want to be bothered: ‘That’s your lookout, big boy, not mine.’ So you see how much the union cares if a black man works or not.”

“Ain’t Tom had de same trouble?” affirmed Sister Johnson. “Got put off de job mo’n once on ’count o’ de white unions.”

“O, they’ve got us cornered, all right,” said Jimboy. “The white folks are like farmers that own all the cows and let the niggers take care of ’em. Then they make you pay a sweet price for skimmed milk and keep the cream for themselves﻿—but I reckon cream’s too rich for rusty-kneed niggers anyhow!”

They laughed.

===Chinese===
「他们就是这么干的，没跑。」吉姆博伊说。「撵你走他们眼都不眨。我不是在《每日导报》（*Daily Leader*）那栋楼上砌砖吗？白人工会的人就开始放话，说我不入会，他们就不能跟我一块儿干活。于是工头过来，把我的工钱结了。『人是把好手，』他对我说，『可我拗不过工会。』我说那我就入会，可还没去办事处，我就知道他们不会要我。不管怎么说，我试过了。我跟里头那帮人说我是砌砖匠，问他们我入不了会，往后靠什么干活。管会卡的那个家伙——我估摸是个书记——说话冲得很，一副懒得搭理人的样子：『那是你自己个儿的事，大个子，不关我的事。』所以你瞧，一个黑人有没有活干，工会才不在乎呢。」

「可不是，汤姆不也吃过同样的亏？」约翰逊姊妹作证道。「不止一回叫白人工会给撵下了活儿。」

「嗐，他们算是把俺们逼进死胡同了，没错，」吉姆博伊说。「白人就像农场主，天下的牛全归他们，光叫黑人来伺候。回头还让你出大价钱买撇过奶油的脱脂奶，奶油他们自己留着——不过我琢磨着，奶油太滋补，反正也轮不上膝盖跪得生锈的黑人消受！」

大家哄笑起来。

===Original===
“That’s a good one!” said Harriett. “You know old man Wright, what owns the flour-mill and the new hotel﻿—how he made his start off colored women working in his canning factory? Well, when he built that orphan home for colored and gave it to the city last year, he had the whole place made just about the size of the dining-room at his own house. They got the little niggers in that asylum cooped up like chickens. And the reason he built it was to get the colored babies out of the city home, with its nice playgrounds, because he thinks the two races oughtn’t to mix! But he don’t care how hard he works his colored help in that canning factory of his, does he? Wasn’t I there thirteen hours a day in tomato season? Nine cents an hour and five cents overtime after ten hours﻿—and you better work overtime if you want to keep the job!﻿ ﻿… As for the races mixing﻿—ask some of those high yellow women who work there. They know a mighty lot about the races mixing!”

“Most of ’em lives in de Bottoms, where de sportin’ houses are,” said Hager. “It’s a shame de way de white mens keeps them sinful places goin’.”

===Chinese===
「这个比方绝了！」哈丽雅特说。「你们认识赖特老头（old man Wright）吧，就是开着面粉厂和新旅馆的那个——他起家的底子怎么来的？靠他罐头厂里干活的黑人妇女！去年他盖了那座有色人种孤儿院送给市里，好家伙，整个地方修得跟他自家饭厅差不多一般大。他把那院里的小黑人们圈得跟小鸡似的。他盖那玩意儿，就是要让黑娃娃们从市立孤儿院里搬出来——那院里还有漂漂亮亮的游戏场——因为他觉得两个种族不该混在一块儿！可他自己罐头厂里的黑人帮工，他往死里使唤，他倒不嫌，是不是？西红柿旺季我不是一天在那儿干十三个钟头吗？一个钟头九分钱，干满十个钟头以后，加班一个钟头五分钱——想保住饭碗，加班你就加吧！……要说两个种族混不混——去问问在厂里做工的那些浅肤色女人吧。种族怎么个混法，她们懂得可多着呢！」

「她们多半住在洼地区，窑子都开在那儿，」黑格说。「白人男人们养着那些罪孽地方不散，真是造孽。」

===Original===
“It ain’t Christian, is it?” mocked Harriett.﻿ ﻿… “White folks!”﻿ ﻿… And she shrugged her shoulders scornfully. Many disagreeable things had happened to her through white folks. Her first surprising and unpleasantly lasting impression of the pale world had come when, at the age of five, she had gone alone one day to play in a friendly white family’s yard. Some mischievous small boys there, for the fun of it, had taken hold of her short kinky braids and pulled them, dancing round and round her and yelling: “Blackie! Blackie! Blackie!” while she screamed and tried to run away. But they held her and pulled her hair terribly, and her friends laughed because she *was* black and she *did* look funny. So from that time on, Harriett had been uncomfortable in the presence of whiteness, and that early hurt had grown with each new incident into a rancor that she could not hide and a dislike that had become pain.

Now, because she could sing and dance and was always amusing, many of the white girls in high school were her friends. But when the three-thirty bell rang and it was time to go home, Harriett knew their polite “Goodbye” was really a kind way of saying: “We can’t be seen on the streets with a colored girl.” To loiter with these same young ladies had been all right during their grade-school years, when they were all younger, but now they had begun to feel the eyes of young white boys staring from the windows of pool halls, or from the tennis-courts near the park﻿—so it was not proper to be seen with Harriett.

But a very unexpected stab at the girl’s pride had come only a few weeks ago when she had gone with her classmates, on tickets issued by the school, to see an educational film of the undersea world at the Palace Theatre, on Main Street. It was a special performance given for the students, and each class had had seats allotted to them beforehand; so Harriett sat with her class and had begun to enjoy immensely the strange wonders of the ocean depths when an usher touched her on the shoulder.

===Chinese===
「这可不合基督教的教义，对吧？」哈丽雅特讥诮道。……「白人！」……她轻蔑地耸了耸肩。白人让她受过的窝囊气可太多了。她对这个苍白世界的头一遭印象，来得猝不及防，又出奇地经久不散：那年她五岁，有一天独自跑到一户和气的白人家的院子里去玩。院里几个调皮的小男孩存心取乐，一把揪住她短短的鬈毛辫子扯了又扯，围着她一圈一圈地跳，尖声怪叫：「小黑炭！小黑炭！小黑炭！」她哭叫着想跑，他们却拽住她，狠狠地扯她的头发，而她的那些朋友笑作一团，因为她*确实*是黑的，样子*确实*滑稽。打那时起，哈丽雅特一到白人跟前就浑身不自在，那道早年的创伤随着每一桩新的事故越长越大，长成一种她藏不住的怨毒，长成一种化成了疼的厌恶。

如今呢，因为她会唱会跳，又总能逗人乐，中学里许多白人姑娘都跟她交好。可是三点半的下课铃一响、到了回家的时候，哈丽雅特就知道她们客客气气的那声「再见」，其实是在婉转地说：「我们不能让人瞧见跟一个有色人种姑娘一起走在街上。」念小学那几年大家都还小，跟这些小姐们东游西逛没什么不行；如今她们开始觉出台球房的窗口里、公园旁边的网球场上，年轻白人小伙子的目光盯在身上——所以，让人瞧见跟哈丽雅特在一起，就不体面了。

可就在几个星期以前，姑娘的自尊又冷不丁挨了一刀：她拿着学校发的票，跟同学们一起到大街上的皇宫影院（Palace Theatre），去看一部关于海底世界的教育影片。那是专为学生开的场次，各班的座位都事先分派好了；哈丽雅特正跟自己班坐在一起，刚开始津津有味地看那海洋深处的种种奇观，一个领座员碰了碰她的肩膀。

===Original===
“The last three rows on the left are for colored,” the girl in the uniform said.

“I﻿—But﻿—But I’m with my class,” Harriett stammered. “We’re all supposed to sit here.”

“I can’t help it,” insisted the usher, pointing towards the rear of the theatre, while her voice carried everywhere. “Them’s the house rules. No argument now﻿—you’ll have to move.”

So Harriett rose and stumbled up the dark aisle and out into the sunlight, her slender body hot with embarrassment and rage. The teacher saw her leave the theatre without a word of protest, and none of her white classmates defended her for being black. They didn’t care.

“All white people are alike, in school and out,” Harriett concluded bitterly, as she told of her experiences to the folks sitting with her on the porch in the dark.

===Chinese===
「左边最后三排是有色人种坐的。」穿制服的姑娘说。

「我——可是——可是我是跟我们班一起的，」哈丽雅特结结巴巴地说。「我们都应该坐在这儿。」

「我也没办法，」领座员一口咬定，手指着影院后头，嗓门大得四面八方都听得见。「这是本院的规矩。别争了——你得挪。」

哈丽雅特站起身，跌跌撞撞地摸黑走过通道，走进外面的太阳地里，纤瘦的身子因羞窘和狂怒而滚烫。老师眼看着她走出影院，一句抗议的话也没有，白人同学里也没有一个因为她是黑人而出面替她说话。她们根本不在乎。

「白人全都一个样，在学校里是这样，出了学校还是这样。」哈丽雅特把这段经历讲给跟她一起坐在黑暗门廊上的人们听，末了恨恨地说。

===Original===
Once, when she had worked for a Mrs. Leonard Baker on Martin Avenue, she accidentally broke a precious cut-glass pitcher used to serve some out-of-town guests. And when she tried to apologize for the accident, Mrs. Baker screamed in a rage: “Shut up, you impudent little black wench! Talking back to me after breaking up my dishes. All you darkies are alike﻿—careless sluts﻿—and I wouldn’t have a one of you in my house if I could get anybody else to work for me without paying a fortune. You’re all impossible.”

“So that’s the way white people feel,” Harriett said to Aunt Hager and Sister Johnson and Jimboy, while the two children listened. “They wouldn’t have a single one of us around if they could help it. It don’t matter to them if we’re shut out of a job. It don’t matter to them if niggers have only the back row at the movies. It don’t matter to them when they hurt our feelings without caring and treat us like slaves down South and like beggars up North. No, it don’t matter to them.﻿ ﻿… White folks run the world, and the only thing colored folks are expected to do is work and grin and take off their hats as though it don’t matter.﻿ ﻿… O, I hate ’em!” Harriett cried, so fiercely that Sandy was afraid. “I hate white folks!” she said to everybody on the porch in the darkness. “You can pray for ’em if you want to, mama, but I hate ’em!﻿ ﻿… I hate white folks!﻿ ﻿… I hate ’em all!”

===Chinese===
还有一回，她在马丁大道（Martin Avenue）莱昂纳德·贝克太太（Mrs. Leonard Baker）家帮工，失手打碎了一把贵重的雕花玻璃水壶，那是拿来招待外地客人的。她刚要为这桩意外赔不是，贝克太太就暴跳如雷地尖叫起来：「闭嘴，你这放肆的小黑蹄子！砸了我的器皿，还敢跟我顶嘴。你们这些黑鬼全都一个样——一个个粗心大意的贱货——要不是出大价钱雇不着别人给我干活，我一个都不愿意让我家里用你们。你们没一个不是这副德行。」

「白人心里就是这么想的，」哈丽雅特对黑格外婆、约翰逊姊妹和吉姆博伊说，两个孩子在旁边听着。「但凡有法子，他们身边一个黑人也不想留。我们把活路断了，他们无所谓。黑人们在电影院里只配坐后排，他们无所谓。他们没心没肺地伤我们的感情，在南边把我们当奴隶，在北边把我们当叫花子，他们全都无所谓。不，他们无所谓……白人主宰着这个世界，有色人种就只配干活、赔笑、脱帽哈腰，好像一切都不值一提……哦，我恨他们！」哈丽雅特喊道，那股凶劲儿把桑迪都吓着了。「我恨白人！」她对黑暗中门廊上的每一个人说。「妈，你想为他们祷告就祷告去吧，可我恨他们！……我恨白人！……他们我一个都恨！」

## VIII Dance / 八 舞会

===Original===
Mrs. J. J. Rice and family usually spent ten days during the August heat at Lake Dale, and thither they had gone now, giving Annjee a forced vacation with no pay. Jimboy was not working, and so his wife found ten days of rest without income not especially agreeable. Nevertheless, she decided that she might as well enjoy the time; so she and Jimboy went to the country for a week with Cousin Jessie, who had married one of the colored farmers of the district. Besides, Annjee thought that Jimboy might help on the farm and so make a little money. Anyway, they would get plenty to eat, because Jessie kept a good table. And since Jessie had eight children of her own, they did not take Sandy with them—eight were enough for a woman to be worried with at one time!

Aunt Hager had been ironing all day on the Reinharts’ clothes—it was Friday. At seven o’clock Harriett came home, but she had already eaten her supper at the restaurant where she worked.

“Hello, mama! Hy, Sandy!” she said, but that was all, because she and her mother were not on the best of terms. Aunt Hager was attempting to punish her youngest daughter by not allowing her to leave the house after dark, since Harriett, on Tuesday night, had been out until one o’clock in the morning with no better excuse than a party at Maudel’s. Aunt Hager had threatened to whip her then and there that night.

===Chinese===
八月酷暑时节，赖斯太太一家照例要去戴尔湖（Lake Dale）避上十天，如今他们已经去了，安吉便落得一个不带工钱的强制假期。吉姆博伊没有活儿干，于是他妻子觉得，这十天没有进项的休息实在算不上什么美事。不过她还是打定主意，不如索性享受这几天；于是她和吉姆博伊下乡到杰茜表姐（Cousin Jessie）家过了一星期——杰茜嫁给了本地一个有色人种农夫。再说，安吉寻思吉姆博伊兴许能在农场上搭把手，挣几个钱。不管怎样，他们能吃饱肚子，因为杰茜家的饭食向来丰盛。而杰茜自己就有八个孩子，他们便没带桑迪同去——一个女人一时操八个孩子的心，已经够受的了！

黑格外婆一整天都在熨赖因哈特家的衣裳——这天是星期五。七点钟哈丽雅特回来了，不过她在做工的那家餐馆里已经吃过晚饭。

“你好，妈妈！嘿，桑迪！”她说，此外便没再多话，因为她和母亲正处得不睦。黑格外婆正想法子惩戒她的小女儿：不许她天黑之后离家——因为星期二夜里，哈丽雅特在外头一直耍到凌晨一点，理由无非是莫德尔家有个聚会。那天夜里，黑格外婆当场就威胁要抽她。

===Original===
“You ain’t had a switch on yo’ hide fo’ three years, but don’t think you’s gettin’ too big fo’ me not to fan yo’ behind, madam. ‘Spare de rod an’ spoil de chile,’ that’s what de Bible say, an’ Lawd knows you sho is spoiled! De idee of a young gal yo’ age stayin’ out till one o’clock in de mawnin’, an’ me not knowed where you’s at. … Don’t you talk back to me! … You rests in this house ever’ night this week an’ don’t put yo’ foot out o’ this yard after you comes from work, that’s what you do. Lawd knows I don’t know what I’s gonna do with you. I works fo’ you an’ I prays fo’ you, an’ if you don’t mind, I’s sho gonna whip you, even if you is goin’ on seventeen years old!”

Tonight as soon as she came from work Harriett went into her mother’s room and lay across the bed. It was very warm in the little four-room house, and all the windows and doors were open.

“We’s got some watermelon here, daughter,” Hager called from the kitchen. “Don’t you want a nice cool slice?”

“No,” the girl replied. She was fanning herself with a palm-leaf fan, her legs in their cheap silk stockings hanging over the side of the bed, and her heels kicking the floor. Benbow’s Band played tonight for the dance at Chaver’s Hall, and everybody was going—but her. Gee, it was hard to have a Christian mother! Harriett kicked her slippers off with a bang and rolled over on her stomach, burying her powdery face in the pillows. … Somebody knocked at the back door.

===Chinese===
“你都三年没让柳条沾过你的皮了，可别自以为长大了，俺就打不得你的屁股了，大小姐。‘舍不得棍子，惯坏了孩子’，圣经上就是这么说的，主啊晓得你真是给惯坏了！像你这么大年纪的姑娘家，在外头一直耍到半夜一点才进门，俺还不知道你人在哪儿。……不许跟俺顶嘴！……这个礼拜你每天夜里都给我待在这屋里，下了工就别把脚迈出这个院子半步，就这么办。主啊晓得俺不知道该拿你怎么好。俺为你操劳，俺为你祷告，你要是不听话，俺准得抽你，哪怕你都快满十七岁了！”

这天晚上，哈丽雅特一下工就进了母亲的屋子，横躺在床上。小小的四间屋里很热，所有的门窗都敞开着。

“闺女，俺们这儿有西瓜，”黑格在厨房里喊。“来一块凉凉的不？”

“不吃，”姑娘答道。她摇着一把蒲扇，穿着便宜丝袜的两条腿悬在床边，鞋跟一下一下磕着地板。今晚本鲍乐队（Benbow’s Band）要在查弗舞厅（Chaver’s Hall）给舞会伴奏，人人都去——偏偏她去不成。唉，有个信基督的妈妈，真是难啊！哈丽雅特啪的一声踢掉拖鞋，翻过身趴着，把扑了粉的脸埋进枕头。……有人敲响了后门。

===Original===
A boy’s voice was speaking excitedly to Hager: “Hemorrhages… and papa can’t stop ’em… she’s coughin’ something terrible… says can’t you please come over and help him”—frightened and out of breath.

“Do, Jesus!” cried Hager. “I’ll be with you right away, chile. Don’t worry.” She rushed into the bedroom to change her apron. “You, Harriett, listen; Sister Lane’s taken awful sick an’ Jimmy says she’s bleedin’ from de mouth. If I ain’t back by nine o’clock, see that that chile Sandy’s in de bed. An’ you know you ain’t to leave this yard under no circumstances. … Po’ Mis’ Lane! She sho do have it hard.” In a whisper: “I ’spects she’s got de T.B., that what I ’spects!” And the old woman hustled out to join the waiting youngster. Jimmy was leaning against the door, looking at Sandy, and neither of the boys knew what to say. Jimmy Lane wore his mother’s cast-off shoes to school, and Sandy used to tease him, but tonight he didn’t tease his friend about his shoes.

“You go to bed ’fore it gets late,” said his grandmother, starting down the alley with Jimmy.

“Yes’m,” Sandy called after her. “So long, Jim!” He stood under the apple-tree and watched them disappear.

===Chinese===
一个男孩的声音正激动地对黑格说话：“她咯血了……俺爸止不住……她咳得吓死人……她说求您能不能过去帮帮他”——又惊又喘。

“哎呀，耶稣啊！”黑格喊道。“俺这就跟你去，娃儿。别担心。”她冲进卧房去换围裙。“你，哈丽雅特，听着；莱恩姊妹病得厉害，吉米说她嘴里出血。要是俺九点钟还没回来，你看着点儿，叫桑迪那娃儿上床睡觉。还有，你知道，不管怎么说你都不许迈出这个院子。……可怜的莱恩太太！她的日子真是苦啊。”她压低嗓子：“俺估摸她是得了痨病（T.B.），俺估摸就是这么回事！”老太太急匆匆出门，跟上等候她的小伙子。吉米斜倚在门边，望着桑迪，两个男孩都不知道说什么好。吉米·莱恩上学穿的是他母亲穿剩的旧鞋，桑迪平日老拿这个取笑他，可今晚他没有拿鞋的事打趣他的朋友。

“天不早了你就上床睡，”他外婆说着，同吉米一道往巷子那头去了。

“是啦，”桑迪在她背后喊。“回见，吉米！”他站在苹果树下，望着他们走没了影儿。

===Original===
Aunt Hager had scarcely gotten out of sight when there was a loud knock at the front door, and Sandy ran around the house to see Harriett’s boyfriend, Mingo, standing in the dusk outside the screen-door, waiting to be let in.

Mingo was a patent-leather black boy with wide, alive nostrils and a mouth that split into a lighthouse smile on the least provocation. His body was heavy and muscular, resting on bowed legs that curved backward as though the better to brace his chunky torso; and his hands were hard from mixing concrete and digging ditches for the city’s new water-mains.

“I know it’s tonight, but I can’t go,” Sandy heard his aunt say at the door. They were speaking of Benbow’s dance. “And his band don’t come here often, neither. I’m heartsick having to stay home, dog-gone it all, especially this evening!”

“Aw, come on and go anyway,” pleaded Mingo. “After I been savin’ up my dough for two weeks to take you, and got my suit cleaned and pressed and all. Heck! If you couldn’t go and knew it yesterday, why didn’t you tell me? That’s a swell way to treat a fellow!”

===Chinese===
黑格外婆前脚刚走没影，前门就响起重重的敲门声，桑迪绕过屋角跑去一看，是哈丽雅特的相好明戈（Mingo）站在纱门外的暮色里，等着开门让他进去。

明戈是个黑得像漆皮似的黑小伙，鼻孔宽宽的活泛得很，嘴巴稍稍一逗就咧开一脸灯塔般亮堂的笑。他身子敦实，肌肉发达，撑在一双往后弯的弓子腿上，仿佛那样更能稳稳托住他粗壮的身板；一双手硬邦邦的，是给城里新铺水管搅混凝土、挖沟挖出来的。

“我知道就是今晚，可我去不了，”桑迪听见他小姨在门口说。她们说的是本鲍的舞会。“他那乐队也不常来这儿。偏要闷在家里，心里真不是滋味，这一切都见鬼去，尤其是今天晚上！”

“嗐，管它呢，照样去，”明戈求她。“我攒了两个礼拜的钱，就为了带你去，西装也送去洗熨得挺挺的。嘿！你要是去不成、昨天就知道了，干吗不跟我说？这么对哥们儿，可真够意思！”

===Original===
“Because I wanted to go,” said Harriett; “and still want to go. … Don’t make so much difference about mama, because she’s mad anyhow … but what could we do with this kid? We can’t leave him by himself.” She looked at Sandy, who was standing behind Mingo listening to everything.

“You can take me,” the child offered anxiously, his eyes dancing at the delightful prospect. “I’ll behave, Harrie, if you take me, and I won’t tell on you either. … Please lemme go, Mingo. I ain’t never seen a big dance in my life. I wanta go.”

“Should we?” asked Harriett doubtfully, looking at her boyfriend standing firmly on his curved legs.

“Sure, if we got to have him… damn ’im!” Mingo replied. “Better the kid than no dance. Go git dressed.” So Harriett made a dash for the clothes-closet, while Sandy ran to get a clean waist from one of his mother’s dresser-drawers, and Mingo helped him put it on, cussing softly to himself all the while. “But it ain’t your fault, pal, is it?” he said to the little boy.

===Chinese===
“因为我想去呀，”哈丽雅特说。“现在也还想。……妈那边倒不打紧，反正她已经气坏了……可这小家伙怎么办？总不能把他一个人撂下。”她望着桑迪，他正站在明戈身后，一字不落地听着。

“你们可以带上我，”孩子急切地自荐，一想到这桩美事眼睛都亮了。“带我去了我就乖乖的，哈丽，我也不会去告状。……求你了，让我去吧，明戈。我这辈子还没见过大舞会呢。我想去。”

“带他去？”哈丽雅特迟疑地问，望着稳稳立在两条弯腿上的男朋友。

“行，非带上他不可的话……去他的！”明戈答道。“带上这小子，总比跳不成舞强。去换衣裳吧。”于是哈丽雅特一头冲向衣柜，桑迪跑去从他母亲的五斗柜抽屉里翻出一件干净罩衫，明戈帮他套上，嘴里一直小声骂骂咧咧。“不过这可不是你的错，伙计，对吧？”他对小家伙说。

===Original===
“Sure not,” Sandy replied. “I didn’t tell Aunt Hager to make Harrie stay home. I tried to ’suade grandma to let her go,” the child lied, because he liked Mingo. “I guess she won’t care about her goin’ to just one dance.” He wanted to make everything all right so the young man wouldn’t be worried. Besides, Sandy very much wanted to go himself.

“Let’s beat it,” Harriett shrilled excitedly before her dress was fastened, anxious to be gone lest her mother come home. She was powdering her face and neck in the next room, nervous, happy, and afraid all at once. The perfume, the voice, and the pat, pat, pat of the powder-puff came out to the waiting gentleman.

“Yo’ car’s here, madam,” mocked Mingo. “Step right this way and let’s be going!”

===Chinese===
“当然不是，”桑迪答道。“我没叫黑格外婆罚哈丽待在家里。我还劝外婆放她去呢，”孩子撒了谎，因为他喜欢明戈。“我看就跳一场舞，外婆不会怎么样的。”他想把一切都抹平，好叫这位年轻人不用操心。再说了，桑迪自己也特别想去。

“快溜吧，”裙子扣子还没扣好，哈丽雅特就兴奋地尖声喊，急着想趁母亲没回来赶紧走。她在隔壁屋里往脸上脖子上扑粉，一时又是紧张，又是快活，又是害怕。香水味儿、说话声，还有粉扑噗、噗、噗的轻响，一齐传到门外等候的绅士耳朵里。

“您的车到啦，夫人，”明戈打趣道。“请这边走，咱们这就出发！”

===Original===
> Wonder where ma easy rider’s gone—
>
> He done left me, put ma new gold watch in pawn!

Like a blare from hell the second encore of “Easy Rider” filled every cubic inch of the little hall with hip-rocking notes. Benbow himself was leading and the crowd moved like jellyfish dancing on individual seashells, with Mingo and Harriett somewhere among the shakers. But they were not of them, since each couple shook in a world of its own, as, with a weary wail, the music abruptly ceased.

Then, after scarcely a breath of intermission, the band struck up again with a lazy one-step. A tall brown boy in a light tan suit walked his partner straight down the whole length of the floor and, when he reached the corner, turned leisurely in one spot, body riding his hips, eyes on the ceiling, and his girl shaking her full breasts against his pink silk shirt.
